Unipotent Ideals and Harish-Chandra Bimodules
Ivan Losev, Lucas Mason-Brown, Dmytro Matvieievskyi

Abstract
. Let G G be a real reductive Lie group. One of the most basic unsolved problems in representation theory and abstract Harmonic analysis is the classification of the set Irr u ​ ( G ) \mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G) of irreducible unitary G G -representations. Although this problem remains unsolved, some general patterns have emerged. For each group G G there should be a finite set of ‘building blocks’ Unip ​ ( G ) ⊂ Irr u ​ ( G ) \mathrm{Unip}(G)\subset\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G) called unipotent representations with an array of distinguishing properties. Every representation ℬ ∈ Irr u ​ ( G ) \mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G) should be obtained through one of several procedures (like parabolic induction) from a unipotent representation ℬ L ∈ Unip ⁡ ( L ) \mathcal{B}_{L}\in\mathrm{Unip}(L) of a suitable Levi subgroup L ⊂ G L\subset G . Furthermore, the representations in Unip ⁡ ( G ) \mathrm{Unip}(G) should be indexed by nilpotent co-adjoint G G -orbits and their equivariant covers. This roadmap has emerged over many decades (see [ Vog87 , Chps 7-13] for an overview) and is supported by numerous successes in important special cases (e.g. [ Vog86a ] and [ Bar89 ] ). A crucial problem with this approach is that the set Unip ⁡ ( G ) \mathrm{Unip}(G) has not yet been defined in the appropriate generality. The main contribution of this paper is a definition of ‘unipotent’ in the case when G G is complex. Our definition generalizes the notion of special unipotent , due to Barbasch-Vogan and Arthur ( [ BV85 ] , [ Art83 ] ). The representations we define arise from finite equivariant covers of nilpotent co-adjoint G G -orbits. To each such cover 𝕆 ~ \widetilde{\mathbb{O}} , we attach a distinguished filtered algebra 𝒜 0 \mathcal{A}_{0} equipped with a graded Poisson isomorphism gr ⁡ ( 𝒜 0 ) ≃ ℂ ⁡ [ 𝕆 ~ ] \gr(\mathcal{A}_{0})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}] . The existence of this algebra follows from the theory of filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities, see [ Los16 ] . The algebra 𝒜 0 \mathcal{A}_{0} receives a distinguished homomorphism from the universal enveloping algebra U ⁡ ( 𝔤 ) U(\mathfrak{g}) , and the kernel of this homomorphism is a completely prime primitive ideal in U ⁡ ( 𝔤 ) U(\mathfrak{g}) with associated variety 𝕆 ¯ \overline{\mathbb{O}} . A unipotent ideal is any ideal in U ⁡ ( 𝔤 ) U(\mathfrak{g}) which arises in this fashion. A unipotent representation is an irreducible Harish-Chandra bimodule which is annihilated (on both sides) by a unipotent ideal. Our definitions are vindicated by the many favorable properties which these ideals and bimodules enjoy. First of all, we show that both unipotent ideals and bimodules have nice geometric classifications. Unipotent ideals are classified by certain geometrically-defined equivalence classes of covers of nilpotent orbits. Unipotent bimodules are classified by irreducible representations of certain finite groups. For classical groups, we show that all unipotent ideals are maximal and all unipotent bimodules are unitary. In addition, we show that all unipotent bimodules are, as G G -representations, of a very special form, proving a conjecture of Vogan ( [ Vog91 ] ). Finally, we show that all special unipotent bimodules are unipotent. The final assertion is proved using a certain refinement of Barbasch-Vogan-Lusztig-Spaltenstein duality, inspired by the symplectic duality of [ BLPW16a ] . Along the way, we establish combinatorial algorithms (in classical and exceptional types) for computing the infinitesimal characters of unipotent ideals. Our definition of ‘unipotent’ admits an obvious generalization to real reductive Lie groups. If G G is such a Lie group, it is natural to call ‘unipotent’ any irreducible Harish-Chandra module which is annihilated by a unipotent ideal. This set includes, as a proper subset, all special unipotent representations in the sense of [ ABV92 , Sec 27] . Unfortunately, some of the representations in this larger set are not unitary. We conjecture that this problem disappears if we assume that 𝕆 \mathbb{O} is birationally rigid.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
17B35, 17B08, 22E46

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Concept of unipotent representations

The concept of a unipotent representation has its origins in the representation theory of finite Chevalley groups. Let
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be the group of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rational points of a connected reductive algebraic group
G
. Consider the set
\operatorname{Irr}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
of complex irreducible representations of
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. The classification of
\operatorname{Irr}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
is a very old problem in representation theory, of fundamental importance. In
[
DL76
]
, Deligne and Lusztig defined a finite set of representations (called
unipotent representations
)

\mathrm{Unip}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\subset\mathrm{Irr}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q})).

These representations play a central role in the classification of
\operatorname{Irr}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
, which was later completed by Lusztig in
[
Lus84
]
. The importance of these representations is two-fold

(i)

They are classified by certain parameters related to complex nilpotent co-adjoint orbits (in particular, their classification is independent of
q
).

(ii)

The classification of

\mathrm{Irr}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
reduces to the classification of
\mathrm{Unip}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
.

For details, we refer the reader to
[
Lus84
]
.

Now, replace
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with a local field
k
and let
G(k)
be the group of
k
-rational points. Consider the set
\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G(k))
of irreducible
unitary
representations of
G(k)
. The classification
\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G(k))
is a classical problem in representation theory. Unlike its
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-analog, it remains unsolved in general (for an introduction to the theory of unitary representations, we refer the reader to
[
Kna86
]
). An intriguing idea, with its origins in
[
BV85
]
, is to find a finite set of representations (called
unipotent
, by analogy)

\mathrm{Unip}(G(k))\subset\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G(k)).

which plays the same role with respect to
\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G(k))
that
\mathrm{Unip}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
plays with respect to
\mathrm{Irr}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
.
In particular, these representations should satisfy suitable analogs of properties (i) and (ii) above.

The goal of this paper is to give a definition of
\mathrm{Unip}(G(k))
in the case when
k=\mathbb{C}
. The definition we propose is geometric in nature, and well-aligned with the orbit method, which is a guiding philosophy in the subject (see
[
Vog92
]
for an overview). Our approach will shed some light on the more complicated case of
k=\mathbb{R}
, see Section
6.3.4
.

In this paper, we will take an algebraic point of view: our main objects of study are Harish-Chandra bimodules (rather than group representations, which are analytic in nature). It is well known that these two formalisms are equivalent. We recall the basic theory of Harish-Chandra bimodules in Section
3.4
.

1.2.
Existing constructions

In
[
BV85
]
, Barbasch and Vogan, following ideas of
Arthur
[
Art83
]
, defined a set of representations called
special unipotent
. We will briefly review their construction and discuss its limitations.

Let
G
be a complex reductive algebraic group and let
\mathfrak{g}
be its Lie algebra. Let
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
be the Langlands dual of
\mathfrak{g}
. If we fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, there is a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, canonically identified with
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. To each nilpotent
\Ad(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
, we can associate a maximal ideal in the enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
as follows. First, using an
\Ad(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-invariant isomorphism
(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, we can identify
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
with a nilpotent
\Ad(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-orbit in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
(still denoted by
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
). Choose an element
e^{\vee}\in\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
and an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e^{\vee},f^{\vee},h^{\vee})
. Acting by
\Ad(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
if necessary, we can arrange so that
h^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
. This element is well-defined modulo the usual action of
W
. Every
W
-orbit in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
determines an infinitesimal character for
U(\mathfrak{g})
by means of the Harish-Chandra isomorphism
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W}
. Consider the infinitesimal character corresponding to the element
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, and let
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
be the (unique) maximal ideal with this given infinitesimal character.

Definition 1.2.1

(Def 1.17,
[
BV85
]
)
.

The
special unipotent ideal
attached to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
is the maximal ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
. A
special unipotent bimodule
attached to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is an irreducible Harish-Chandra bimodule which is annihilated (on both sides) by
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})
. Denote the set of such bimodules by
\mathrm{Unip}^{s}_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}(G)
.

There is a distinguished class of nilpotent orbits, first defined in
[
Lus79
]
, called
special
nilpotent orbits. Below we will recall one of several equivalent definitions. If
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is special, there is a well-known classification of
\mathrm{Unip}^{s}_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}(G)
. Namely, there is a finite group
\overline{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
attached to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
called the
Lusztig canonical quotient
(see
[
Lus84
, Chp 13]
) and a natural bijection

(1.2.1)

\mathrm{Unip}^{s}_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}(G)\simeq\{\text{irreducible representations of }\overline{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})\}.

In the case when
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}
is integral, this statement is contained in
[
BV85
, Thm III]
. The non-integral case can be handled by similar methods, see
[
Won18a
]
. If
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is
not
special, there is (to our knowledge) no known classification of
\mathrm{Unip}^{s}_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}(G)
. As an easy application of the main results in this paper, we obtain one (see Remark
9.2.3
). Our classification is of the same form as (
1.2.1
), except that
\overline{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
is replaced with the
Galois group of a certain finite cover of a nilpotent co-adjoint
G
-orbit.

Definition
1.2.1
has a number of limitations, which were known to Barbasch and Vogan at the time when
[
BV85
]
was written. Recall that to every irreducible Harish-Chandra bimodule, there is an associated nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(see Section
3.1
for details). There is also an order-reversing map

\mathsf{D}:\{\text{nilpotent orbits in }(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{nilpotent orbits in }\mathfrak{g}^{*}\}

called Barbasch-Vogan-Lusztig-Spaltenstein (BVLS) duality (see Section
3.2
). In
[
BV85
]
it is shown that the nilpotent orbit associated to a special unipotent bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Unip}^{s}_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}(G)
is
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

The main limitation of Definition
1.2.1
is related to the fact that
\mathsf{D}
is not (usually) surjective. Its image is the set of special nilpotent orbits (this explains the word ‘special’ in ‘special unipotent’). If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
, then
\mathsf{D}
is a bijection and hence every orbit is special, but in all other types, there are non-special orbits. There are many bimodules attached to such orbits which deserve to be called unipotent, for example the ‘metaplectic’ bimodules for
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, see Example
6.2.1
(iv). Thus, Definition
1.2.1
is incomplete with respect to the problem posed in Section
1.1
(as mentioned above, Barbasch and Vogan were well aware of this difficulty when they formulated their definition, see e.g. the final paragraphs of
[
BV85
, Section 1]
. In fact, this issue is the main subject of the second half of
[
Vog87
]
).

Since the publication of
[
BV85
]
, various attempts have been made to generalize the notion of ‘special unipotent’. Some examples include:

•

Vogan’s weakly unipotent ideals (
[
Vog84
]
).

•

Barbasch’s unipotent representations (
[
Bar89
]
).

•

McGovern’s q-unipotent ideals (
[
McG94
]
).

•

Brylinski’s unipotent Dixmier algebras (
[
Bry03
]
).

None of these approaches is completely satisfactory. Vogan’s definition picks out an
infinite
set of ideals, which is inconsistent with the orbit method. The approaches of Barbasch and McGovern are based on ad hoc definitions which make sense only in classical types. Brylinski’s approach is appealingly geometric but only applies in special cases. Worse still, the approaches of Vogan, McGovern, and Brylinski allow for non-unitary bimodules (see Section
6.2
for examples).

1.3.
Vogan’s desiderata

In
[
Vog87
, Chps 6-12]
, Vogan lists some expected properties of unipotent bimodules. The most concrete of these expectations are catalogued below:

(1)

\mathrm{Unip}(G)
is a finite subset of
\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G)
, attached in a natural fashion to
G
. See
[
Vog87
, Chp 6, Problem I.2]
.

(2)

The set
\mathrm{Unip}(G)
is partitioned into ‘packets’
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
parameterized by equivariant covers of nilpotent co-adjoint
G
-orbits, i.e.

\mathrm{Unip}(G)=\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G).

See
[
Vog87
, ‘Def’ 12.8]
.

(3)

For each such
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, there is maximal, completely prime ideal
I(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
such that

\mathrm{LAnn}(\mathcal{B})=\mathrm{RAnn}(\mathcal{B})=I(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}),\qquad\forall\ \mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G).

See
[
Vog87
, Conj 9.19]
and
[
Vog87
, ‘Def’ 9.20]
.

(4)

For every
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G)
, there is a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a unipotent bimodule
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Unip}(L)
such that
\mathcal{B}
is obtained from
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
through parabolic induction and/or a complementary series construction (
[
Vog87
, Chp 6, Problem I.1]
)

(5)

For every
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
and
e\in\mathbb{O}
, there is a finite-dimensional representation
\chi
of the component group
G_{e}/G_{e}^{\circ}
such that

(1.3.1)

\mathcal{B}\simeq_{G}\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{G_{e}}\chi

where
\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{G_{e}}
denotes induction of algebraic group representations (see
[
Jan87
, Sec 3.3]
) and
\mathcal{B}
is regarded as a
G
-representation via the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}
. See
[
Vog87
, Requirement 11.23]
. See also
[
Vog91
, Conj 12.1]
for a more precise criterion.

(6)

\mathrm{Unip}(G)
includes (as a proper subset) all special unipotent bimodules (cf. Definition
1.2.1
). See the remarks following
[
Vog87
, ‘Def’ 12.8]
as well as
[
Vog87
, Chp 8]
.

(7)

For each
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, the set
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
is in one-to-one correspondence with irreducible representations of a finite group, which is related to the geometry of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. See
[
Vog87
, Chp 7]
as well as
[
Vog87
, Chp 10]
.

1.4.
Definitions

The main contribution of this paper is a definition of
\mathrm{Unip}(G)
. Our definition is case-free, and geometric in nature. The main technical input is the theory of quantizations of conical symplectic singularities.

Conical symplectic singularities are a remarkable class of affine Poisson varieties with contracting
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-actions. Some basic examples include Kleinian singularities (i.e. varieties of the form
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
, for a finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2)
) and the nilpotent cones of complex reductive Lie algebras. If
X
is a conical symplectic singularity, it makes sense to talk about its filtered quantizations (for a general discussion of graded Poisson varieties and their filtered quantizations, see Section
4.1
). The classification of filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities was completed by the first-named author in
[
Los16
]
. Roughly speaking, he shows that the set of filtered quantizations of
X
is in bijection with points in a complex vector space
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
, which is recovered from the geometry of
X
. A consequence of this result, which is of primary importance for our purposes, is that every conical symplectic singularity has a distinguished quantization, i.e. the one corresponding to
0\in\mathfrak{P}^{X}
. We call this quantization the
canonical quantization
and denote it by
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{X}
. We conjecture that
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{X}
is a simple algebra, for all
X
, and gather a great deal of evidence in Section
5.5
.

Now let
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a nilpotent
G
-orbit and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
be a finite connected
G
-equivariant cover. Note that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a smooth symplectic variety, and its ring of regular functions
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
is a finitely-generated algebra. Consider the affine variety
\widetilde{X}:=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
. It is not difficult to show that
\widetilde{X}
is a conical symplectic singularity (see
[
Los21
, Lem 2.5]
)).

If
\mathcal{A}
is
any
filtered quantization of
\widetilde{X}
, the
G
-action on
\widetilde{X}
lifts to a
G
-action on
\mathcal{A}
(by filtered algebra automorphisms). We show that the latter action is Hamiltonian, i.e. there is a quantum co-moment map
\Phi:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}
(lifting the classical co-moment map
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
induced from the natural map of varieties
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
). Note that
\Phi
turns
\mathcal{A}
into a Dixmier algebra, in the sense of
[
Vog90
, Def 2.1]
. Our main definition is as follows.

Definition 1.4.1
.

The
unipotent ideal
attached to
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is the two-sided ideal

I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}):=\ker{(\Phi:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})}.

A
unipotent bimodule
attached to
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is an irreducible Harish-Chandra bimodule with left and right annihilator equal to
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. Denote the set of such bimodules by
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
.

It is easy to see that
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a completely prime primitive ideal with associated variety
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. We will see below that it is maximal (we supply a complete proof of this fact for linear classical groups. In the remaining cases, we provide only a sketch, leaving the details to a future paper). In fact, we will show that the maximality of
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is equivalent to the simplicity of
\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{X}}_{0}
, which, as stated above, is a general expectation.

Much of this paper is devoted to checking Vogan’s desiderata for the definition above. Conditions (2), (5), (6), and (7) are verified for arbitrary
G
. Condition (1) and (3) are verified for linear classical
G
(in both cases, we indicate how the methods carry over to spin and exceptional groups).
Condition (4) is left more or less untouched (proving this condition is equivalent to the classification of
\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G)
for arbitrary
G
, a problem which we do not solve in this paper). Further motivation for Definition
1.4.1
will be provided in Section
6.3
.

1.5.
Results

We now summarize the main results of this paper.

1.5.1.
Infinitesimal characters and maximality

In Section
8
, we give a recipe for computing the infinitesimal characters of all unipotent ideals. There are two steps:

(1)

Reduce the calculation to
rigid
nilpotent orbits. This requires a fairly detailed analysis of the birational geometry of nilpotent covers.

(2)

Determine the infinitesimal characters attached to rigid nilpotent orbits. For this step we rely heavily on the work of McGovern (
[
McG94
]
) and Premet (
[
Pre13
]
).

Following this recipe, one can, in principle, compute the infinitesimal character of an arbitrary unipotent ideal. For linear classical groups, we use this recipe to produce simple combinatorial formulas for unipotent infinitesimal characters, see Proposition
8.2.8
. In (
[
MM23
]
), we use a similar procedure to compute the unipotent infinitesimal characters for spin and exceptional groups.

If
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a primitive ideal with known infinitesimal character and associated variety, there is a combinatorial condition for deciding whether
I
is a maximal ideal. We check this condition in many cases, and prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.5.1

(Propositions
B.1.1
, and
B.2.1
, Corollary
B.4.4
below)
.

Suppose that
G
is a linear classical group, i.e.
G=\operatorname{SL}(n)
,
\operatorname{SO}(n)
,
or
\operatorname{Sp}(2n)
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant cover of a nilpotent orbit in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is maximal.

In
[
MM23
]
, we extend this result to arbitrary reductive groups.

1.5.2.
Classification

In Section
6
, we give geometric classifications of unipotent ideals and bimodules. First, we explain the classification of unipotent ideals. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
and
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
be
G
-equivariant covers of a common nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Consider the affine varieties
\widetilde{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
,
\widecheck{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}])
.
Both
\widetilde{X}
and
\widecheck{X}
contain finitely many
G
-orbits and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
(resp.
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
) is the unique open orbit in
\widetilde{X}
(resp.
\widecheck{X}
). Furher, every
G
-equivariant morphism
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
extends to a unique finite
G
-equivariant morphism
\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{X}
. The latter morphism is étale over the open orbit
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\subset\widecheck{X}
.

Definition 1.5.2
.

A finite
G
-equivariant morphism
\widetilde{X}\rightarrow\widecheck{X}
is
almost étale
if it is étale over the open subset in
\widecheck{X}
obtained by removing all
G
-orbits of codimension
\geqslant 4
.

Write
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\succeq\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
if there is a
G
-equivariant morphism
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
such that the induced morphism
\widetilde{X}\rightarrow\widecheck{X}
is almost étale. This defines a partial order on the set of
G
-equivariant covers of
\mathbb{O}
. Taking the symmetric closure, we get an equivalence relation on the same set. It is not difficult to see that each equivalence class contains a unique maximal element, see Lemma
6.5.3
below.

Theorem 1.5.3

(Theorem
6.5.5
below)
.

The ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
depends only on the equivalence class of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
with respect to the equivalence relation above.
The passage from
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
to
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
defines a bijection between the following two sets

•

Equivalence classes of
G
-equivariant covers
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
of
\mathbb{O}
.

•

Unipotent ideals with associated variety
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
.

Next, we explain the classification of
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
. By Theorem
1.5.3
, the set
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
depends only on the equivalence class
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. Thus, we can assume for this discussion that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is maximal in
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. Let
\Pi
denote the automorphism group of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
. This is a finite group, and can be described explicitly as follows. Choose an element

e\in\mathbb{O}
and
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
lying over
e
. Write
G_{e}
and
G_{x}
for the stabilizers in
G
of
e
and
x
, repsectively. Then
G_{x}
is a subgroup of finite-index in
G_{e}
and
\Pi\simeq N_{G_{e}}(G_{x})/G_{x}
.

Theorem 1.5.4

(Theorem
6.6.2
below)
.

There is a natural bijection

\{\text{irreducible representations of }\Pi\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G).

1.5.3.
Unitarity

The most basic requirement of unipotent bimodules is that they are unitary. We have some partial results in this direction.

Theorem 1.5.5

(Theorem
10.6.1
below)
.

Suppose that
G
is a linear classical group, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant cover of a nilpotent orbit in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Then every bimodule in
\operatorname{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
is unitary.

The proof is structured as follows. For classical
\mathfrak{g}
, there is a well-know classification of irreducible unitary Harish-Chandra bimodules, due to Barbasch (
[
Bar89
]
). The main result of his paper, namely Theorem 12.8, is difficult to apply in general. An intermediate result in the same paper establishes the unitarity of a large collection of bimodules attached to
rigid
nilpotent orbits (see
[
Bar89
, Prop 10.6]
). As an easy consequence of this result, we show that all unipotent bimodules attached to rigid orbits are unitary. To complete the proof of Theorem
1.5.5
, we show that all of the remaining unipotent bimodules (i.e. those attached to covers of induced nilpotent orbits) are obtained from rigid ones by certain unitarity-preserving procedures, namely parabolic induction and complementary series.

1.5.4.
G
-types

Assume that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}

is maximal in its equivalence class. Recall that the bimodules in

\operatorname{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
are classified by irreducible representation of
\Pi
(i.e. the automorphism group of the cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
).

Theorem 1.5.6

(Corollary
6.6.3
below)
.

Suppose that
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is maximal, and let
\mathcal{B}\in\operatorname{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
. Let
V
denote the irreducible representation of
\Pi
corresponding to
\mathcal{B}
. Then there is a good filtration on

\mathcal{B}
and an isomorphism of graded
G
-equivariant
S(\mathfrak{g})
-modules

\gr(\mathcal{B})\simeq(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]\otimes V)^{\Pi}.

The isomorphism of
G
-representations (
1.3.1
) predicted by Vogan is an easy consequence of this result (see Proposition
6.7.1
below).

1.5.5.
‘Special unipotent’ implies
‘unipotent’

In Section
9
we prove the following result.

Theorem 1.5.7

(Corollary
9.2.2
below)
.

Every special unipotent bimodule is unipotent.

In fact, we prove a more precise result. To each nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
, we associate an equivalence class of covers
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
. This defines an injection

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}:\{\text{nilpotent orbits in }(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}\}\hookrightarrow\{\text{equivalence classes of covers of nilpotent orbits in }\mathfrak{g}^{*}\}.

We show that for each
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, the unipotent ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}))
coincides with the special unipotent ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})
. Thus,
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}^{s}(G)=\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})}(G)
.

Our construction of
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}
should be viewed as a special case of a more general (but still largely conjectural) duality known as
symplectic duality
, see
[
BLPW16a
, Sec 10]
. In Section
9.3
, we will explain how our construction fits into this picture.

Remark 1.5.8
.

For
G=\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, there is a notion, due to Barbasch, Ma, Sun, and Zhu, of a ‘metaplectic special’ unipotent bimodule, see
[
BMSZ20
]
(these are irreducible bimodules with prescribed annihilators arising via a certain ‘metaplectic’ modification of BVLS duality). It is easy to see that these bimodules are unipotent as well, compare to
Section
9
. Since we will not use this result, we omit the proof.

1.5.6.
Dixmier conjecture

In Section
7.8
, we prove an old conjecture of Vogan (see
[
Vog90
, Conj 2.3]
). To each
G
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
of a co-adjoint
G
-orbit
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(not necessarily nilpotent) we attach a canonically defined Dixmier algebra
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
such that

\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]\simeq_{G}\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

as representations of
G
. We prove the following result.

Theorem 1.5.9

(Theorem
7.8.1
below)
.

The assigment
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\mapsto\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
defines an injective correspondence between
G
-equivariant covers of co-adjoint orbits and Dixmier algebras for
G
.

Moreover, if
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
nilpotent
cover, then
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
corresponds to the canonical quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. This is one of the principal justifications for Definition
1.4.1
.

1.5.7.
Discussion of approach

Here we provide a brief description of our approach in this paper, as it differs substantially from previous work in the subject.
Whereas existing work in the area of unipotent representations has been largely Lie-theoretic, our approach in this paper is mostly geometric. A recurring theme is that many interesting properties of unipotent bimodules which are very difficult to establish using Lie-theoretic methods, become much more elementary and transparent with a geometric point of view. The most striking examples are Theorems
1.5.4
and
1.5.6
.

Consider the problem of classifying unipotent bimodules. As discussed in Section
1.2
, if
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is special, the special unipotent bimodules attached to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
are classified by irreducible representations of the Lusztig quotient
\overline{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})

(this is essentially
[
BV85
, Thm III]
).
The proof appearing in
[
BV85
]
is based on a complicated (and in places, case-by-case) argument involving translation functors, Kazhdan-Lusztig cells, and the Springer correspondence. Even the definition of
\overline{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
is quite involved. Our approach, on the other hand, is geometric in nature. The group which replaces
\overline{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
is easy to define, our result is much more general, and our proof is more elementary.

Another (related) example is Vogan’s conjecture (Theorem
1.5.6
). Our proof of this result is easy and conceptual, whereas Lie-theoretic proofs, which exist in several cases, are difficult and ad hoc.

When it comes to results which are more Lie-theoretic in nature, our arguments become more technical. A good example is our (partial) computation of unipotent infinitesimal characters (Section
8
). In the Barbasch-Vogan picture, special unipotent ideals are
defined
by their infinitesimal characters, which are given by the elegant formula
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}
. In our generalization, there is no such simple formula for unipotent infinitesimal characters, and lots of work is required to compute them. Similarly, in the Barbasch-Vogan picture, special unipotent ideals are
defined
to be maximal, whereas in our generalization, lots of work is required to prove this. We note that the simplicity of the canonical quantization (Conjecture
5.5.1
) is equivalent to the maximality of the unipotent ideal (Theorem
6.3.3
) and seems to be a general phenomenon. However, proving the simplicity conjecture in general seems to be currently out of reach.

An interesting question is whether it is possible to come up with a simple
conceptual
description of the infinitesimal characters we obtain, perhaps in terms of the dual group
G^{\vee}
. It is possible that further developments in the area of symplectic duality will shed some light on this question.

1.6.
Content of paper

Here is an outline of the paper. In Sections
2
,
3
, and
4
, we review some preliminary results which are needed in later sections. Much of the material in these sections already exists in the literature.

In Section
2
we collect some elementary facts about nilpotent orbits in complex semisimple Lie algebras and their (finite, connected) covers.

In Section
3
we recall some basic facts from Lie theory. This includes a discussion of primitive ideals in enveloping algebras, Harish-Chandra bimodules, Barbasch-Vogan-Lusztig-Spaltenstein (BVLS) duality, and
\mathcal{W}
-algebras.

In Section
4
we review the key elements of the theory of conical symplectic singularities, their filtered quantizations, and Harish-Chandra bimodules over these filtered quantizations. This section contains two main results. The first is a classification of filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities, obtained by the first-named author in
[
Los16
]
.
To each conical symplectic singularity
X
we attach a finite-dimensional parameter space
\mathfrak{P}
. Filtered quantizations of
X
are parameterized by points in this parameter space, modulo the action of a certain finite group. The second main result is a classification of Harish-Chandra bimodules with full support over a filtered quantization of a conical symplectic singularity. In Section
4.13
we recall a category equivalence, first obtained in
[
Los21
]
, between the category of Harish-Chandra bimodules with full support and the category of representations of a certain finite group. In Sections
4.14
,
4.15
we provide a (partial) description of this finite group (see, in particular, Corollary
4.14.4
, Theorem
4.15.2
).

In Section
5
, we introduce the notion of a canonical quantization: the canonical quantization of a conical symplectic singularity is the filtered quantization corresponding to the zero parameter. If
\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is a finite Galois covering of conical symplectic singularities, we get a quantization of
X
by taking the algebra of invariants in the canonical quantization of
\widetilde{X}
. This quantization of
X
, which is typically not canonical, will play an important role in our description of unipotent ideals attached to nontrivial nilpotent covers. In Section
5.3
, we compute its quantization parameter. Also in this section we compute the finite group which controls the category of Harish-Chandra bimodules with full support over a canonical quantization, see Proposition
5.1.2
. In fact, we conjecture that canonical quantizations are simple algebras (and hence that
all
Harish-Chandra bimodules are automatically of full support). This conjecture is discussed in Section
5.5
.

In Section
6
we introduce our main objects of study: unipotent ideals and unipotent bimodules. These objects are closely related to canonical quantizations, so the results of Section
5
are applicable. Our primary objective in Sections
6
-
10
is to show that these distinguished ideals and bimodules have all (or at least, many) of the properties one would hope for—in other words, to convince the reader that our definitions are the right ones. As a first step towards this goal, we show in Section
6
that our unipotent ideals and bimodules have geometric classifications. Unipotent ideals are classified by equivalence classes of covers with respect to a geometrically defined equivalence relation connected to codimension 2 singularities. Unipotent bimodules are classified by irreducible representations of certain finite groups. The latter classification bears an encouraging resemblance to the Barbasch-Vogan classification of special unipotent bimodules.

One of the most interesting questions which arises from this paper is whether our notion of ‘unipotent’ can be meaningfully extended to all real reductive Lie groups. We discuss this question briefly in Section
6.3.4
. One can naively extend our definition to representations of a (non-complex) Lie group by requiring that the annihilator of the underlying
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module is a unipotent ideal. Some very simple examples show that this naive definition is flawed—many of the representations in question are non-unitary. Remarkably, this issue seems to vanish if
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid.

In Sections
7
-
10
, we endeavor to establish some additional key properties of unipotent ideals and bimodules. Unlike the results in Section
6
, these require a detailed understanding of the infinitesimal characters of unipotent ideals. This turns out to be a rather delicate matter. In Sections
7
and
8
we present an algorithm for computing the infinitesimal character
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
of the unipotent ideal attached to an arbitrary nilpotent cover. The main formula is stated in Section
8
: for each nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, there is a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a rigid
L
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{L}
such that

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{L})+\delta,

where
\delta
is a small ‘shift’ related to the quantization parameter constructed in Section
5
. Thus, the computation of
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a two-step process: first, compute the infinitesimal character attached to the rigid nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}_{L}
, and then compute the element
\delta
. For the first step, we appeal to known results of McGovern (for
\mathfrak{g}
a classical Lie algebra) and Premet (for
\mathfrak{g}
exceptional). There are six “bad” orbits in exceptional types which are not handled by Premet (we handle these orbit separately in Appendix
C
—they are the most difficult cases). On the other hand, computing the element
\delta
requires a fairly detailed analysis of the birational geometry of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. The required machinery is developed in Section
7
.

Using our computation of unipotent infinitesimal characters, we prove in Section
8.5
that all unipotent ideals for linear classical groups are maximal. The argument relies on some rather tedious combinatorics, which is relegated to Appendix
B
.

In Section
9
we introduce our refined BVLS duality. This is a map which takes nilpotent orbits for the Langlands dual group
G^{\vee}
to nilpotent covers for
G
. One consequence of this duality is that all special unipotent ideals (in the sense of
[
BV85
]
) are unipotent.

In Section
10
, we turn our attention to the unitarity of unipotent bimodules and the related issue of their construction via parabolic induction. The main result in this section, Theorem
10.6.1
, is that all unipotent bimodules for linear classical groups are unitary. The proof has two components: first, we show that all unipotent bimodules attached to rigid orbits are unitary. Then, we show that these ‘rigid’ unipotents generate all others (through several unitarity-preserving operations). The first step is an immediate consequence of an old result of Barbasch (
[
Bar89
]
) and our infinitesimal character computations. For the second step, we introduce a version of parabolic induction for Harish-Chandra bimodules over filtered quantizations. Under suitable conditions, this construction coincides with the usual induction of Harish-Chandra bimodules (this is proved in Appendix
A
), and thus preserves unitarity.

1.7.
Acknowledgements

The authors would like to thank Jeffrey Adams, Dan Barbasch, Alexander Premet and David Vogan for many helpful conversations. The work of I.L. and D.M. was partially supported by the NSF
under grant DMS-2001139.

2.
Nilpotent orbits and their covers

Let
G
be a complex connected reductive algebraic group and let
\mathfrak{g}
be its Lie algebra. For some statements, we will assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is
classical
, i.e. a simple Lie algebra of type
A
,
B
,
C
, or
D
. Occasionally, we will assume that
G
is
linear classical
. For us this will mean that
G
belongs to one of four infinite families:
\mathrm{SL}(n)
,
\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)
,
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, and
\mathrm{SO}(2n)
. Note that this condition is strictly stronger than the previous. For example,
\mathrm{Spin}(n)
is classical, but not linear classical.

In this section, we will collect some basic facts about nilpotent
G
-orbits and their equivariant covers.

2.1.
Nilpotent orbits

If
\mathfrak{g}
is classical, the nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
are parameterized by (decorated) integer partitions.

Definition 2.1.1
.

A partition
p
is of
type C
(resp type
B/D
) if every odd part (resp. even part) occurs with even multiplicity.

The following result is well-known.

Proposition 2.1.2

(Section 5.1,
[
CM93
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}
is classical. The set of nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is parameterized by (decorated) partitions as follows.

(a)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
n
.

(b)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
2n+1
of type B/D.

(c)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
2n
of type C.

(d)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
2n
of type B/D, except that each
very even
partition (i.e. a partition containing only even parts) corresponds to two nilpotent orbits, labeled
\mathbb{O}^{\mathrm{I}}
and
\mathbb{O}^{\mathrm{II}}
.

There is a partial order on nilpotent orbits defined by the relation

\mathbb{O}^{1}\leq\mathbb{O}^{2}\iff\overline{\mathbb{O}}^{1}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{O}}^{2}.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is classical, this order can be described in terms of partitions. Let
p^{1}=(p^{1}_{1},p^{1}_{2},...,p^{1}_{r})
and
p^{2}=(p^{2}_{1},p^{2}_{2},...,p^{2}_{s})
be the partitions corresponding to
\mathbb{O}^{1}
and
\mathbb{O}^{2}
, respectively. Then
\mathbb{O}^{1}\leq\mathbb{O}^{2}
if and only if

\sum_{i=1}^{j}p^{1}_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}p^{2}_{i}\qquad\text{for every }j,

where we complete partitions with zero parts as necessary. See
[
CM93
, Thm 6.2.5]
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is exceptional, the classification of nilpotent orbits is more complicated. In this paper, we will use the
Bala-Carter
classification. For details, see Section
2.6
. Tables can be found in
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
.

2.2.
Nilpotent covers

A G
-equivariant nilpotent cover
is a triple consisting of a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, a homogeneous space
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
for
G
, and a finite
G
-equivariant map
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
. The latter is automatically an étale cover of
\mathbb{O}
, since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is homogeneous. A
morphism of nilpotent covers
is a
G
-equivariant map
f:\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
such that the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{O}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Denote the set of morphisms
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
by
\Hom_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})
. Since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is homogeneous and
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
is finite,
\Hom_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})
is a finite set. To describe it, choose
e\in\mathbb{O}
and a preimage
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. Write
G_{e},G_{x}\subset G
for the stabilizers. Note that
G_{x}\subseteq G_{e}
is a finite index subgroup (in particular,
G_{x}^{\circ}=G_{e}^{\circ}
). The set
\Hom_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})
can be described as follows

(2.2.1)

\Hom_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})\simeq\{x^{\prime}\in\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\mid G_{x}\subseteq G_{x^{\prime}}\}.

As a special case of (
2.2.1
), we obtain the following isomorphism

\Aut_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\simeq N_{G_{e}}(G_{x})/G_{x}.

The group above is called the
Galois group
of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. We say that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is
Galois
if
G_{x}
is a normal subgroup in
G_{e}
. In this case,
\mathbb{O}\simeq\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}/\Aut_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
.

There is a partial order on the set of isomorphism classes of nilpotent covers, defined by

(2.2.2)

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\geq\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\iff\Hom_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})\neq\emptyset.

Consider the
universal cover

\widehat{\mathbb{O}}=G/G_{e}^{\circ}
of
\mathbb{O}
. It follows from (
2.2.1
) that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is Galois and the (unique) maximal cover with respect to the order defined by (
2.2.2
).

The
equivariant fundamental group
of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is by definition

\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}):=\Aut_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})\simeq G_{x}/G_{e}^{\circ}.

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\geq\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
, then there is an embedding

\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\simeq G_{x}/G_{e}^{\circ}\hookrightarrow G_{x^{\prime}}/G_{e}^{\circ}\simeq\pi_{1}^{G}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}).

In particular,
\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a subgroup of
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
(well-defined up to conjugacy). The passage from
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
to
\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
defines a Galois correspondence

\pi_{1}^{G}:\{\text{isom. classes of nilpotent covers}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\text{conj. classes of subgroups }H\subseteq\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})\}.

For any subgroup
H\subset\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
, the preimage under
G_{e}\twoheadrightarrow G_{e}/G_{e}^{\circ}
is a finite index subgroup
\widetilde{H}\subset G_{e}
, which defines a nilpotent cover
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}=G/\widetilde{H}
with
\pi_{1}^{G}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})\simeq H
. Furthermore,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is Galois if and only if
\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
. If it is, there is a group isomorphism

\Aut_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\simeq\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})/\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}).

If
G
is classical, the groups
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
can be described in terms of partitions (see
[
CM93
, Cor 6.1.6]
). Suppose
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a nilpotent orbit corresponding to a partition
p=(p_{1},p_{2},...,p_{k})
. Put

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\text{number of distinct odd }p_{i}.

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\text{number of distinct even }p_{i}.

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\mathrm{gcd}(p_{i}).

For example, if
p=(4,3^{2},2^{4},1)
, then
a=2
,
b=2
, and
d=1
.

If
G
is
linear classical, then
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
is given by the following table:

G

\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})

\mathrm{SL}(n)

\mathbb{Z}_{d}

\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)

(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{a-1}

\mathrm{Sp}(2n)

(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{b}

\mathrm{SO}(2n)

(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{\mathrm{max}(a-1,0)}

We will also need a description of
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
for
G=\mathrm{Spin}(m)
. A partition
p
is
rather odd
if every odd part occurs with multiplicity 1. If
G=\mathrm{Spin}(m)
and
p
is
not
rather odd, then
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})\simeq\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{SO}(m)}(\mathbb{O})
. If
p
is rather odd, then
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
is a central extension of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{SO}(m)}(\mathbb{O})

by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

If
G
is exceptional and simply connected, the groups
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
can be found in
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
.

2.3.
Lusztig-Spaltenstein induction

Let
M\subset G
be a Levi subgroup of
G
, and let
\mathbb{O}_{M}
be a nilpotent
M
-orbit. Fix a parabolic subgroup
Q\subset G
with a Levi decomposition
Q=MU
. The annihilator of
\mathfrak{q}
in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
Q
-stable subspace, denoted by
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Choosing a nondegenerate invariant symmetric form on
\mathfrak{g}
, we get a
Q
-invariant identification
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\simeq\mathfrak{u}
. Form the
G
-equivariant fiber bundle
G\times^{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})
over the partial flag variety
G/Q
. There is a proper
G
-equivariant map

\mu:G\times^{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}^{*},\qquad\mu(g,\xi)=\Ad^{*}(g)\xi.

The image of
\mu
is a closed irreducible
G
-invariant subset of the nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Hence it is the closure in
\mathcal{N}
of a uniquely defined nilpotent
G
-orbit
\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. The correspondence

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}:\{\text{nilpotent }M\text{-orbits}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{nilpotent }G\text{-orbits}\}

is called
Lusztig-Spaltenstein
induction (or simply ‘induction’ when there is no risk for confusion). A nilpotent orbit is said to be
rigid
if it cannot be induced from a proper Levi subgroup. Of course, every nilpotent orbit can be induced from a rigid nilpotent orbit (typically there are several). Some properties of induction are catalogued below.

Proposition 2.3.1

(
[
LS79
]
or
[
CM93
]
, Sec 7)
.

Induction has the following properties

(i)

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}
depends only on
M
(and not on
Q
)

(ii)

If
L\subset M
is a Levi subgroup of
M
, then

\Ind^{G}_{L}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\circ\Ind^{M}_{L}.

(iii)

If
\mathbb{O}_{M}\subset\mathfrak{m}^{*}
is a nilpotent
M
-orbit and
\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
, then

\mathrm{codim}(\mathbb{O}_{M},\mathcal{N}_{M})=\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N}),

where
\mathcal{N}_{M}
stands for the nilpotent cone in
\mathfrak{m}^{*}
.

For classical
\mathfrak{g}
, one can describe the effect of induction on partitions and the partitions corresponding the rigid nilpotent orbits. We will begin by recalling the classification of Levi subgroups in classical types. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
and let
\Delta\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be the set of roots. Choose standard coordinates on
\mathfrak{h}
(see
[
CM93
, Sec 5.2]
) and denote the coordinate functions by
\{e_{i}\}
.

If
G=\mathrm{SL}(n)
and
a=(a_{1},...,a_{t})
is a partition of
n
, there is a Levi subgroup

\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})):=\mathrm{SL}(n)\cap(\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t}))\subset G

corresponding to the roots

\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j})\}_{1\leq i<j\leq a_{1}}\cup...\cup\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j})\}_{n-a_{t}+1\leq i<j\leq n}\subset\Delta

Every Levi subgroup in
G
is conjugate to one of this form, and no two such are conjugate.

If
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)
,
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, or
\mathrm{SO}(2n)
,
0\leq m\leq n
, and
a
is a partition of
n-m
, there is a Levi subgroup

(2.3.1)

\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})\times G(m)\subset G

corresponding to the roots

\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j})\}_{1\leq i<j\leq a_{1}}\cup...\cup\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j})\}_{n-m-a_{t}+1\leq i<j\leq n-m}\cup\Delta(m)\subset\Delta

where
\Delta(m)\subset\Delta
has the obvious meaning. If
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)
or
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, then every Levi subgroup in
G
is conjugate to one of this form, and no two such are conjugate.

If
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n)
, and
a
is a partition of
n
with only even parts, there is a Levi subgroup

(2.3.2)

\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})^{\prime}\subset G

corresponding to the roots

\{\alpha\in\Delta\mid\alpha(\underbrace{1,...,1}_{a_{1}},\underbrace{2,...,2}_{a_{2}},...,\underbrace{t,...,t,-t}_{a_{t}})=0\}\subset\Delta.

The prime is included to distinguish this subgroup from the subgroup
\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})\subset G
defined above (to which it is
O(2n)
-, but not
SO(2n)
-conjugate). Every Levi subgroup in
G
is conjugate to one of the form (
2.3.1
) or (
2.3.2
), and no two such are conjugate.

We now proceed to describing the effect of induction on partitions. If
p
is a partition, write
B(p)
(resp.
C(p)
) for the unique largest partition of type B/D (resp. C) dominated by
p
, and write
p^{t}
for the transpose of
p
. If
q
is another partition (of any size), define the sum
p+q
‘row by row’, e.g.
(5,3,1^{2})+(4,3^{3})=(9,6,4^{2})
. The following proposition is standard. For maximal Levi subgroups, it is proved in
[
CM93
, Thm 7.3.3]
. The general case follows from the transitivity of induction (see Proposition
2.3.1
(ii)).

Proposition 2.3.2
.

The following are true:

(i)

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
. Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{gl}(a_{1})\times\mathfrak{gl}(a_{t}))\subset\mathfrak{g}
and let

\mathbb{O}_{M}=\mathbb{O}_{M}^{1}\times...\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{t}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})}\times...\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})}=\mathcal{N}_{M}.

Write
p^{j}
for the partition of
a_{j}
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}_{M}^{j}
and define

p^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}p^{j}.

Then
p^{\prime}
is the partition corresponding to
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
.

(ii)

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
or
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
. Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{gl}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathfrak{gl}(a_{t})\times\mathfrak{g}(m)
or possibly (if
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
)
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{gl}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathfrak{gl}(a_{t})^{\prime}
(with all
a_{i}
even) and let

\mathbb{O}_{M}=\mathbb{O}_{M}^{1}\times...\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{t}\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{0}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})}\times...\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{SO}(2m+1)}=\mathcal{N}_{M}

Write
p^{j}
for the partition corresponding to
\mathbb{O}_{M}^{j}
and define
p^{\prime}
by the formula

p^{\prime}=B(p^{0}+2\sum_{j=1}^{t}p^{j}).

Then
p^{\prime}
is the partition corresponding to
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
. If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
and
p^{\prime}
is very even, then its decoration can be deduced from
[
CM93
, Cor. 7.3.4]
.

(iii)

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
. Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{gl}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathfrak{gl}(a_{t})\times\mathfrak{sp}(2m)
and let

\mathbb{O}_{M}=\mathbb{O}_{M}^{1}\times...\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{t}\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{0}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})}\times...\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{Sp}(2m)}=\mathcal{N}_{M}.

Write
p^{j}
for the partition corresponding to
\mathbb{O}_{M}^{j}
and define
p^{\prime}
by the formula

p^{\prime}=C(p^{0}+2\sum_{j=1}^{t}p^{j}).

Then
p^{\prime}
is the partition corresponding to
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
.

As an easy consequence we obtain

Corollary 2.3.3

(
[
CM93
]
, Cor 7.3.5)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}
is classical, and let
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a nilpotent orbit corresponding to a partition
p
. Then the
\mathbb{O}
is rigid if and only if one of the following is true:

(i)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
and
\mathbb{O}=\{0\}
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
or
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
,
p
satisfies

p_{i}\leq p_{i+1}+1,\qquad\forall i,

and no odd part of
p
occurs exactly twice.

(iii)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
,
p
satisfies

p_{i}\leq p_{i+1}+1,\qquad\forall i,

and no even part of
p
occurs exactly twice.

For exceptional
\mathfrak{g}
, a list of rigid orbits, and the effect of induction on them, can be found in
[
DE09
, Sec 4]
.

2.4.
Birational induction of nilpotent covers

Choose a Levi subgroup
M\subset G
, a nilpotent
M
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{M}
, and a (finite, connected)
M
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
of
\mathbb{O}_{M}
. Let
\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
. Consider the affine variety
\widetilde{X}_{M}:=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}])
. There is an
M
-action on
\widetilde{X}_{M}
(induced from the
M
-action on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
) and a finite surjective
M
-equivariant map
\mu_{M}:\widetilde{X}_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. Choose a parabolic subgroup
Q\subset G
with Levi decomposition
Q=MU
. Let
Q
act on
\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
as follows:
M
acts diagonally, and
U
acts by

u\cdot(x,y)=(x,u\mu_{M}(x)-\mu_{M}(x)+uy),\quad u\in U,\quad x\in\widetilde{X}_{M},\quad y\in\mathfrak{q}^{\perp},

By our construction of the
Q
-action, the map
\mu_{M}\times\mathrm{id}:\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
is
Q
-equivariant. It gives rise to a
G
-equivariant map
G\times^{Q}(\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G\times^{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})
of fiber bundles over
G/Q
. Consider the composition

\widetilde{\mu}:G\times^{Q}(\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G\times^{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\overset{\mu}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}.

Note that
\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}(\mathbb{O})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
is a (finite, connected)
G
-equivariant cover. The correspondence

\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}:\{M\text{-eqvt nilpotent covers}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{G\text{-eqvt nilpotent covers}\},\qquad\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}):=\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}(\mathbb{O}).

is called
birational induction
. By construction, the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{M\text{-eqvt nilpotent covers}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{G\text{-eqvt nilpotent covers}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{nilpotent }M\text{-orbits}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{nilpotent }G\text{-orbits}\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}}

A
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is said to be
birationally rigid
if it cannot be be birationally induced from a proper Levi subgroup.

Note that
G
acts ‘by conjugation’ on pairs of the form
(M,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
. Indeed, if
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
, then
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\simeq M/M_{x}
as
M
-equivariant nilpotent covers. The
G
-action is defined by

g\cdot(M,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})=(\Ad(g)M,\Ad(g)M/\Ad(g)M_{x}).

Note that
\Ad(g)M/\Ad(g)M_{x}
is a cover of
\Ad(g)\mathbb{O}_{M}
and is independent of
x
(up to isomorphism of
\Ad(g)M
-equivariant covers).

The main properties of birational induction are catalogued below.

Proposition 2.4.1
.

Birational induction has the following properties

(i)

\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}
depends only on
M
(and not on
Q
)

(ii)

If
L\subset M
is a Levi subgroup of
M
, then

\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\circ\mathrm{Bind}^{M}_{L}.

(iii)

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover, there is a Levi subgroup
M\subset G
and a birationally rigid
M
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
such that

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}.

The pair
(M,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
is unique up to conjugation by
G
.

(iv)

For any nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
, write
\deg(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
for the degree of the covering map. Then

\deg(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})\text{ divides }\deg(\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})).

Proof.

Properties (i) and (iii) were established for nilpotent orbits in

[
Los16
, Lem 4.1]
and
[
Los16
, Thm 4.4]
. The proofs can easily be generalized to nilpotent covers (see
[
Mat20
, Cor 4.3]
). Properties (ii) and (iv) are trivial.
∎

It is possible to classify birationally rigid nilpotent orbits and covers. We will prove several results in this direction in Section
7.6
.

We conclude this subsection by describing a large class of orbits which are birationally induced from
\{0\}
. Suppose
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a nilpotent
G
-orbit. Using an
\Ad(\mathfrak{g})
-invariant identification
\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, we can regard
\mathbb{O}
as a nilpotent
G
-orbit in
\mathfrak{g}
. Choose an element
e\in\mathbb{O}
and an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e,f,h)
. The operator
\ad(h)
defines a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on
\mathfrak{g}

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{i},\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{i}:=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}\mid\ad(h)(\xi)=i\xi\}.

We say that
\mathbb{O}
is
even
if
\mathfrak{g}_{i}=0
for every odd integer
i
. In any case, we can define a parabolic subalgebra

(2.4.1)

\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{O}}=\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{O}}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\mathbb{O}},\qquad\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{O}}:=\mathfrak{g}_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{n}_{\mathbb{O}}:=\bigoplus_{i\geq 1}\mathfrak{g}_{i}.

We call
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{O}}
(resp.
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{O}}
) the
Jacobson-Morozov
parabolic (resp. Levi) associated to
\mathbb{O}
. Both
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{O}}
and
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{O}}
are well-defined up to conjugation by
G
. The following result is well-known. The proof is contained in
[
Kos59
]
, see also
[
CM93
, Thm 3.3.1]
.

Proposition 2.4.2
.

Suppose
\mathbb{O}
is an even nilpotent
G
-orbit. Then

\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L_{\mathbb{O}}}\{0\}.

2.5.
Birational induction and equivariant fundamental groups

Fix the notation of Section
2.4
. Choose an
M
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. Consider the fiber bundle
\widetilde{Z}^{0}:=G\times^{Q}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})
over the flag variety
G/Q
.
Note, there is an inclusion
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}(\mathbb{O})\subset\widetilde{Z}^{0}
. Let
i
denote the inclusion map. It gives rise to a group homomorphism

(2.5.1)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{1}(\widetilde{Z}^{0}).

Since
i:\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{Z}^{0}
is an embedding of smooth complex manifolds, the complement
\widetilde{Z}^{0}-i(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is of
real
codimension
\geq 2
. Hence, the homomorphism (
2.5.1
) is surjective. We will use it to define a surjective homomorphism
f:\pi^{G}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi^{M}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
.

First, note that
\widetilde{Z}^{0}
is a vector bundle over the homogeneous space
G\times^{Q}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
with fiber equal to
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
. Hence, there is a natural isomorphism

\pi_{1}(\widetilde{Z}^{0})\simeq\pi_{1}(G\times^{Q}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}).

If we fix a base point
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, we get a fibration
G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
with fiber
G_{x}
. This fibration gives rise to an exact sequence of homotopy groups

\pi_{1}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{0}(G_{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1.

The final (nontrivial) term is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
.

Similarly, if we fix a base point
(1,y)\in G\times^{Q}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
, we get a fibration
G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G\times^{Q}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
with fiber
Q_{y}
and hence an exact sequence

\pi_{1}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{1}(G\times^{Q}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{0}(Q_{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1.

Since
U\subset Q_{y}
, we have
Q_{y}=M_{y}\ltimes U
. It follows that
\pi_{0}(Q_{y})\simeq\pi_{0}(M_{y})\simeq\pi_{1}^{M}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
. Choose
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
and
y\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
such that
x
maps to
(1,y)
under
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\subset\widetilde{Z}^{0}\twoheadrightarrow G\times^{Q}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. There is a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\widetilde{Z}^{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{M}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}

where the fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(G)
,
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
,
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{Z}^{0})
, and
\pi_{1}^{M}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
are defined with respect to the base points
1\in G
,
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
,
x\in\widetilde{Z}^{0}
, and
y\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
, respectively. The following lemma is immediate.

Lemma 2.5.1
.

There is a uniquely defined homomorphism
f:\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{1}^{M}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
which makes the above diagram commute. This homomorphism is surjective.

2.6.
Bala-Carter inclusion

Let
M\subset G
be a Levi subgroup of
G
. We defined in
Section

2.3
a map
\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}
from nilpotent
M
-orbits to nilpotent
G
-orbits. There is a second map with the same source and target called
Bala-Carter inclusion
. It is defined by

\mathrm{Sat}^{G}_{M}:\{\text{nilpotent }M\text{-orbits}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{nilpotent }G\text{-orbits}\},\qquad\mathrm{Sat}^{G}_{M}(\mathbb{O}_{M})=G\cdot\mathbb{O}_{M}

(‘Sat’ stands for ‘saturation’). An orbit
\mathbb{O}
is said to be
distinguished
if it is not obtained by Bala-Carter inclusion from a proper Levi subgroup.

Proposition 2.6.1

(
[
BC76
]
, see also
[
CM93
]
, Thm 8.1.1)
.

If
\mathbb{O}
is a nilpotent
G
-orbit, there is a Levi subgroup
M\subset G
and a distinguished nilpotent
M
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{M}
such that

\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Sat}^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}.

The pair
(M,\mathbb{O}_{M})
is unique up to conjugation by
G
.

Suppose
Q\subset G
is a parabolic subgroup with Levi decomposition
Q=MU
. We say that
Q
is
distinguished
if
\dim(M)=\dim(Q/[Q,Q])
(e.g. a Borel subgroup is distinguished).

Proposition 2.6.2

(
[
BC76
]
, see also
[
CM93
]
, Thms 8.2.6, 8.2.8)
.

If
\mathbb{O}
is a distinguished nilpotent
G
-orbit, there is a distinguished parabolic
P=LN\subset G
such that
\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{L}\{0\}
. Furthermore,
P
is unique up to conjugation by
G
.

Remark 2.6.3
.

We note that every distinguished nilpotent orbit is even, see the discussion preceding
2.4.2
. A convenient reference for this fact is
[
CM93
, Thm 8.2.3]
. Thus by Proposition
2.4.2
, every distinguished orbit
\mathbb{O}
is birationally induced from the
\{0\}
-orbit of its Jacobson-Morozov Levi
L_{\mathbb{O}}\subset G
. Furthermore, the associated parabolic
P_{\mathbb{O}}
is automatically distinguished, see
[
CM93
, Thm 8.2.6]
. It follows that the parabolic
P
appearing in Proposition
2.6.2
is
G
-conjugate to
P_{\mathbb{O}}
.

Combining Propositions
2.6.1
and
2.6.2
, we obtain the following well known result.

Corollary 2.6.4

(
[
BC76
]
, see also
[
CM93
]
, Thm 8.2.12)
.

There is a bijection between nilpotent
G
-orbits and
G
-conjugacy classes of pairs
(M,P_{M})
consisting of a Levi subgroup
M\subset G
and a distinguished parabolic
P_{M}\subset M
. This bijection is defined by

(M,P_{M})\mapsto\mathrm{Sat}^{G}_{M}\mathrm{Ind}^{M}_{P_{M}}\{0\}.

We are now prepared to explain the Bala-Carter notation for nilpotent orbits. A nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
corresponding to a pair
(M,P_{M})
(as in Corolary
2.6.4
) is assigned a
Bala-Carter label

X(s_{i})
, where:

•

X
is the Lie type of
[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}]
.

•

i
is the number of simple roots in a Levi subalgebra of
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

•

s
is a letter (either
a
or
b
) chosen arbitrarily to distinguish orbits with identical
X
and
i
.

If
i=0
, the parenthetical
(s_{0})
is omitted. In a few cases, the orbit
\mathbb{O}
is undetermined by the notation
X(s_{i})
and additional symbols are needed to identify it uniquely. For standard notation in these cases, we refer the reader to
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
.

For classical
\mathfrak{g}
, one can describe the effect of Bala-Carter inclusion on partitions and the partitions corresponding to distinguished nilpotent orbits. If
p
and
q
are partitions (of any size), define the union
p\cup q
by adding multiplicities, e.g.
(5,3,1^{2})\cup(4,3^{3})=(5,4,3^{4},1^{2})
. The following proposition is standard.

Proposition 2.6.5
.

The following are true:

(i)

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
. Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{gl}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathfrak{gl}(a_{t}))
and let

\mathbb{O}_{M}=\mathbb{O}_{M}^{1}\times...\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{t}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})}\times...\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})}=\mathcal{N}_{L}=M.

Write
p^{j}
for the partition of
a_{j}
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}_{M}^{j}
and define
p^{\prime}
by the formula

p^{\prime}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{t}p^{j}.

Then
p^{\prime}
is the partition corresponding to
\mathrm{Sat}^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
.

(ii)

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
,
\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
or
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
. Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{gl}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathfrak{gl}(a_{t})\times\mathfrak{g}(m)
or possibly (if
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
)
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{gl}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathfrak{gl}(a_{t})^{\prime}
(with all
a_{i}
even) and let

\mathbb{O}_{M}=\mathbb{O}_{M}^{1}\times...\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{t}\times\mathbb{O}_{M}^{0}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})}\times...\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})}\times\mathcal{N}_{G(m)}=\mathcal{N}_{M}.

Write
p^{j}
for the partition corresponding to
\mathbb{O}_{M}^{j}
. Define
p^{\prime}
by the formula

p^{\prime}=p^{0}\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{t}(p^{j}\cup p^{j}).

Then
p^{\prime}
is the partition corresponding to
\mathrm{Sat}^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
. If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
and
p^{\prime}
is very even, then
p^{0}
is very even. In this case,
p^{\prime}
has the same decoration as
p^{0}
.

Corollary 2.6.6

(
[
CM93
]
, Thm 8.2.14)
.

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
, then the only distinguished nilpotent
G
-orbit is the principal one. If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
,
\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, or
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, then
\mathbb{O}
is distinguished if and only if the corresponding partition has no repeated parts.

3.
Lie theory preliminaries

Let
G
be a complex connected reductive algebraic group. Choose a Borel subgroup
B\subset G
and a maximal torus
H\subset B
. Denote the root system by
\Delta
and the Weyl group by
W
. In this section, we will review some Lie theoretic facts which are needed in later sections.

3.1.
Associated varieties and primitive ideals

Let
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
be a two-sided ideal. If we equip
U(\mathfrak{g})
with its usual filtration, there is a
G
-equivariant graded algebra isomorphism isomorphism
\gr U(\mathfrak{g})\simeq S(\mathfrak{g})
by Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt. Under this identification,
\gr(I)
corresponds to a
G
-invariant ideal in
S(\mathfrak{g})
, which defines a
G
-invariant subset
V(I)\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}=\Spec(S(\mathfrak{g}))
, called the
associated variety
of
I
.

Recall that
I
is said to be
primitive
if it is the annihilator of a simple left
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module. Denote the set of such ideals by
\mathrm{Prim}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. If
I\in\mathrm{Prim}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
, then by a theorem of Joseph (
[
Jos85
]
),
V(I)
is the closure of a single nilpotent orbit.

Let
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})
denote the center of
U(\mathfrak{g})
. If
I
is primitive, then by Quillen’s lemma, the intersection
I\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})
is the kernel of an algebra homomorphism
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{C}
, called the
infinitesimal character
of
I
. Infinitesimal characters are identified with
W
-orbits in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
via the Harish-Chandra isomorphism
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W}
. For each
\gamma\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, define

\mathrm{Prim}_{\gamma}(U(\mathfrak{g})):=\{I\in\mathrm{Prim}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\mid W\gamma=\text{infinitesimal character of }I\}.

Proposition 3.1.1
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. Then

(i)

\mathrm{Prim}_{\gamma}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
is a finite, nonempty set.

(ii)

\mathrm{Prim}_{\gamma}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
contains a unique maximal element
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma)
with respect to the inclusion ordering on ideals.

(iii)

If
I,I^{\prime}
are prime (for example, primitive) ideals in
U(\mathfrak{g})
, then

I\subsetneq I^{\prime}\implies V(I^{\prime})\subsetneq V(I).

Proof.

(i) and (ii) follow from the results of
[
Duf77
]
. (iii) follows from
[
BK76
, Cor 3.6]
.
∎

3.2.
BVLS duality

Let
G^{\vee}
be the Langlands dual of
G
and let
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
be the Lie algebra of
G^{\vee}
. By construction,
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
contains a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
which is naturally identified with
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. To every nilpotent
G^{\vee}
-orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
, one can attach a ‘dual’ nilpotent
G
-orbit
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
as follows. First, replace
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
with its counterpart in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
using a
G^{\vee}
-invariant identification
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\simeq(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
. Choose an element
e^{\vee}\in\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
and an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e^{\vee},f^{\vee},h^{\vee})
. Replacing this triple with a
G^{\vee}
-conjugate triple if necessary, we can arrange so that
h^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
. This element is well-defined modulo
W
. Consider the infinitesimal character for
U(\mathfrak{g})
corresponding to
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{*}
via the Harish-Chandra isomorphism and let
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
be the unique maximal ideal of infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}
, cf. Proposition
3.1.1
(ii). Finally, let

\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}):=\text{unique open }G\text{-orbit in }V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})).

This defines a map

\mathsf{D}:\{\text{nilpotent }G^{\vee}\text{-orbits}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{nilpotent }G\text{-orbits}\}

called
Barbasch-Vogan-Lusztig-Spaltenstein (BVLS) duality
. The definition above is due to Barbasch-Vogan (see
[
BV85
]
). Variants of this map can be found in the earlier work of Lusztig (
[
Lus84
]
) and Spaltenstein (
[
Spa82
]
).

A nilpotent
G
-orbit is said to be
special
if it lies in the image of
\mathsf{D}
. The map
\mathsf{D}
restricts to a bijection

\mathsf{D}:\{\text{special nilpotent }G^{\vee}\text{-orbits}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\text{special nilpotent }G\text{-orbits}\}.

The inverse bijection coincides with the duality map for
G^{\vee}
, see
[
BV85
, Prop A2]
.

BVLS duality intertwines Bala-Carter inclusion (see Section
2.6
) and Lusztig-Spaltenstein induction (see Section
2.3
) in the following sense.

Proposition 3.2.1

(
[
BV85
]
, Prop A2)
.

Suppose
L\subset G
is a Levi subgroup of
G
. Then
L^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
is a Levi subgroup of
G^{\vee}
. If
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{L}
is a nilpotent
L^{\vee}
-orbit, then

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{L}\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{L}\right)=\mathsf{D}\left(\mathrm{Sat}^{G^{\vee}}_{L^{\vee}}\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{L}\right).

Remark 3.2.2
.

It is worth noting that Proposition
3.2.1
is false if we swap
\Ind
and
\mathrm{Sat}
. That is, in general
\mathrm{Sat}^{G}_{L}\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{L}^{\vee})\neq\mathsf{D}\left(\Ind^{G^{\vee}}_{L^{\vee}}\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{L}\right)
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is classical, one can describe
\mathsf{D}
in terms of partitions.

Definition 3.2.3
.

Let
p=(p_{1},...,p_{k})
be a partition of
n
. Define partitions
l(p)
and
e(p)
of
n-1
and
n+1
, respectively:

l(p):=(p_{1},...,p_{k-1},p_{k}-1),\qquad e(p):=(p_{1},...,p_{k},1).

Proposition 3.2.4

(
[
McG94
]
, Thm 5.2)
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is classical,
\mathsf{D}
can be described in terms of partitions as follows

(i)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
and
\mathsf{D}(p)=p^{t}
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
and
\mathsf{D}(p)=B(e(p)^{t})
.

(iii)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
and
\mathsf{D}(p)=C(l(p^{t}))
.

(iv)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
and
\mathsf{D}(p)=B(p^{t})
. If
n
is divisible by 4 and
p
is very even, then
\mathsf{D}(p)
preserves decoration. If
n
is not divisible by 4 and
p
is very even, then
\mathsf{D}(p)
reverses decoration.

For exceptional
\mathfrak{g}
, a description of
\mathsf{D}
can be recovered from the information given in
[
Car93
, Sec 13.4]
.

3.3.
Maximal ideals

Let
\gamma\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and let
I\in\mathrm{Prim}_{\gamma}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. In this section, we will describe a (well-known) criterion for deciding whether
I
is a maximal ideal.

Fix
G^{\vee}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
as in Section
3.2
. Using the identification
\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, we can regard
\gamma
as an element of
\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
. This element defines two subsystems of
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee})=\Delta^{\vee}

\Delta^{\vee}_{\gamma}:=\{\alpha^{\vee}\in\Delta^{\vee}:\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\},\qquad\Delta^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}:=\{\alpha^{\vee}\in\Delta^{\vee}:\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=0\}\subset\Delta^{\vee}_{\gamma}

called the subsystems of
integral
and
singular
co-roots. These subsystems define reductive subalgebras
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}_{\gamma}
and
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}

(3.3.1)

\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}_{\gamma}=Z_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}(\exp(2\pi i\gamma)),\qquad\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=Z_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}(\gamma)\subset\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}_{\gamma},

where
Z_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}
stands for the centralizer in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
.

By definition,
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}
is a Levi subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. In general,
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
is not (it is the centralizer in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
of a semisimple group element). Using the bijection
\Delta^{\vee}\simeq\Delta
, we can produce from
\Delta^{\vee}_{\gamma}
and
\Delta^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}
two subsystems of
\Delta

\Delta_{\gamma}:=\left(\Delta^{\vee}_{\gamma}\right)^{\vee}\subset\Delta,\qquad\Delta_{\gamma,0}:=\left(\Delta^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}\right)^{\vee}\subset\Delta_{\gamma}.

These root systems define reductive Lie algebras, denoted by
\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}
and
\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma,0}
. The smaller Lie algebra
\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma,0}
can be identified with a Levi subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. In general, there is no natural embedding
\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}\subset\mathfrak{g}
.

Consider the nilpotent orbits

\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma}:=\Ind^{L_{\gamma}^{\vee}}_{L_{\gamma,0}^{\vee}}\{0\}\subset(\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}^{\vee})^{*},\qquad\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}:=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma})\subset\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}^{*}.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

Suppose
V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Then
I
is a maximal ideal if and only if

\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}}).

Proof.

In
[
Lus84
, Chp 13.3]
, Lusztig defines a map

J^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}:\{\mathbb{O}_{L_{\gamma}}\subset\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\mathbb{O}\subset\mathcal{N}\}

called
truncated induction
. In the special case when
\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}
is a Levi subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
,
J^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}
coincides with
\Ind^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}
. In any case, there is an equality

(3.3.2)

\codim(\mathbb{O}_{L_{\gamma}},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})=\codim(J^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{L_{\gamma}},\mathcal{N}).

Both statements appear in
[
Lus84
, Chp 13.3]
.

Consider the maximal ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma)\in\mathrm{Prim}_{\gamma}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. By
[
BV85
, Prop A2]

(3.3.3)

V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))=\overline{J^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma})}

By Proposition
3.1.1
(ii),
I\subseteq I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma)
and therefore

(3.3.4)

\overline{J^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma})}=V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))\subseteq V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}.

Now

\displaystyle I\text{ is maximal}

\displaystyle\iff I=I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma)

Proposition
3.1.1
(ii)

\displaystyle\iff V(I)=V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))

Proposition
3.1.1
(iii)
