\displaystyle\iff\mathbb{O}=J^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma})

(
3.3.3
)

\displaystyle\iff\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}}).

(
3.3.2
),(
3.3.4
)

∎

Remark 3.3.2
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}
is a Levi subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. Then
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is maximal if and only if
\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}
. This follows easily from the proof of Proposition
3.3.1
.

3.4.
Harish-Chandra bimodules

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule. A
compatible filtration
on
\mathcal{B}
is an ascending filtration by subspaces

0=\mathcal{B}_{-1}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{1}\subseteq...,\qquad\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{i}=\mathcal{B}

such that

U_{i}(\mathfrak{g})\mathcal{B}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i+j}\quad\text{and}\quad[U_{i}(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{j}]\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i+j-1},\qquad\forall i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Here,
\mathbb{C}=U_{0}(\mathfrak{g})\subset U_{1}(\mathfrak{g})\subset...
denote the usual (PBW) filtration of
U(\mathfrak{g})
. Under the conditions above,
\gr(\mathcal{B})
has the structure of a graded
S(\mathfrak{g})
-module (because
\gr U(\mathfrak{g})\simeq S(\mathfrak{g})
). A compatible filtration is
good
if
\gr(\mathcal{B})
is finitely-generated for
S(\mathfrak{g})
.

Definition 3.4.1
.

A
Harish-Chandra bimodule
is a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule which admits a good filtration. A morphism of Harish-Chandra bimodules is a morphism of
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodules. Denote the category of Harish-Chandra bimodules by
\HC(U(\mathfrak{g}))
.

Note that a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule
\mathcal{B}
is Harish-Chandra if and only if it is finitely-generated (as a left or right
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module) and the adjoint action
\ad
of
\mathfrak{g}
on
\mathcal{B}
is locally finite. The following lemma is standard, and we omit the proof.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

For
\mathcal{B}\in\HC(U(\mathfrak{g}))
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

Every irreducible
\mathfrak{g}
-representation appears with finite multiplicity in
\mathcal{B}
(regarded as a
\mathfrak{g}
-module via
\ad
).

(ii)

\mathcal{B}
has finite length.

(iii)

\mathcal{B}
has finite support over
\Spec(\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}))
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{B}\in\HC(U(\mathfrak{g}))
has left and right infinitesimal characters, then
\mathcal{B}
satisfies properties (i) and (ii) above.

We say that a Harish-Chandra bimodule is
G
-
equivariant
if
\ad
integrates to a rational action of
G
. Denote the full subcategory of
G
-equivariant bimodules by
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\subset\HC(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. Let
G^{\mathrm{sc}}
denote the universal cover of
G
and
K
the kernel of the covering map
G^{\mathrm{sc}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G
. Then
\HC(U(\mathfrak{g}))=\HC^{G^{\mathrm{sc}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
and
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
is the full subcategory consisting of all bimodules
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G^{\mathrm{sc}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
with trivial
K
-action.

Given a Harish-Chandra bimodule
\mathcal{B}
and a chosen good filtration, we can define the
associated variety
of
\mathcal{B}

\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B}):=\mathrm{Supp}(\gr(\mathcal{B}))=V(\mathrm{Ann}_{S(\mathfrak{g})}\gr(\mathcal{B}))\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*},

where
V(\bullet)
denotes the zero locus of an ideal in
S(\mathfrak{g})=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
.

If
Z
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})
, we can define the
generic multiplicity
of
\mathcal{B}
along
Z

m_{Z}(\mathcal{B}):=\mathrm{mult}_{Z}(\gr(\mathcal{B}))\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

By a standard argument, both
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})
and
m_{Z}(\mathcal{B})
are independent of the filtration. Furthermore,
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})=V(\mathrm{LAnn}(\mathcal{B}))=V(\mathrm{RAnn}(\mathcal{B}))
(where
\mathrm{LAnn}(\mathcal{B})
and
\mathrm{RAnn}(\mathcal{B})
denote the left and right annihilatiors, and
V(\bullet)
is as defined in Section
3.1
).

Next, we recall the notion of parabolic induction for Harish-Chandra bimodules. Suppose
Q\subset G
is a parabolic subgroup with Levi decomposition
Q=MU
. If
\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{m})
and
\alpha_{L}-\alpha_{R}
integrates to a character of
M
, we can define a one-dimensional Harish-Chandra bimodule
\mathbb{C}(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R})\in\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m}))
by the formula

(3.4.1)

\xi t=\alpha_{L}(\xi)t\quad t\xi=\alpha_{R}(\xi)t,\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{m},\ t\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}).

Starting with a bimodule
\mathcal{B}_{M}\in\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m}))
, we will construct an induced bimodule
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}\in\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
, thus defining a functor

\Ind^{G}_{M}:\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))

called
parabolic induction
. First, let
\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}:=\mathcal{B}_{M}\otimes\mathbb{C}(\rho_{\mathfrak{u}},\rho_{\mathfrak{u}})
, where
\rho_{\mathfrak{u}}
is the one-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{m}
defined by

(3.4.2)

\rho_{\mathfrak{u}}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}(\ad(\xi)|_{\mathfrak{u}}),\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{m}.

Let
\mathfrak{q}^{-}=\mathfrak{m}\oplus\mathfrak{u}^{-}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be the opposite parabolic, and regard
\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}
as a
U(\mathfrak{q})-U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})
-bimodule via the quotient maps
\mathfrak{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{m}
. Next define

\widetilde{\Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}=\mathrm{Hom}_{U(\mathfrak{q})\otimes U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})}\left(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}\right)

where
\Hom
is defined using the left action of
\mathfrak{q}
on the first
U(\mathfrak{g})
factor and the right action of
\mathfrak{q}^{-}
on the second. Regard
\widetilde{\Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}
as a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule via

u_{1}fu_{2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=f(v_{1}u_{1}\otimes u_{2}v_{2}),\qquad u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\in U(\mathfrak{g}),\ f\in\widetilde{\Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}.

Finally, let
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}
be the subspace of
\widetilde{\Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}
consisting of the
\mathfrak{g}
-finite vectors for the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Proposition 3.4.3

(
[
Vog81
]
, Chapter 6)
.

The construction given above defines an exact covariant functor

\Ind^{G}_{M}:\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})).

This functor enjoys the following properties:

(i)

\Ind^{G}_{M}
depends only on
M
(and not on
Q
).

(ii)

If
L\subset M
is a Levi subgroup of
M
, then there is a natural isomorphism

\Ind^{G}_{L}\simeq\Ind^{G}_{M}\circ\Ind^{M}_{L}.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{B}_{M}\in\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m}))
, then as representations of
G

\left(\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}\right)|_{G}\simeq\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}(\mathcal{B}_{M}|_{G}),

where
\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}
is the functor of algebraic induction (see
[
Jan87
, Sec 3.3]
).

(iv)

If
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathcal{B}_{M}
has left and right infinitesimal characters
(\gamma_{\ell},\gamma_{r})\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W_{M}\times\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W_{M}
, then
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}
has left and right infinitesimal characters
(\gamma_{\ell},\gamma_{r})\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W\times\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W
.

For what follows, we will need an alternative description of
\Ind^{G}_{M}
. For
\mathcal{B}_{M}\in\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m}))
, define
U(\mathfrak{g})
-
U(\mathfrak{m})
-bimodules

\Delta^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{M}):=U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{q})}\mathcal{B}_{M},\qquad\nabla^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{M}):=\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}_{U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}).

Here
\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}
denotes the direct sum of Homs from graded components as in the definition
of (parabolic) dual Verma modules. Let
\mathcal{A}_{M}=U(\mathfrak{m})/\mathrm{RAnn}(\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})
and consider the space of right
\mathcal{A}
-module homomorphisms

\Hom_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}(\Delta^{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{M}),\nabla^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})).

This space has the structure of a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule via the left actions of
U(\mathfrak{g})
on
\Delta^{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{M})
and
\nabla^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})
. We will consider the subspace of
\ad(\mathfrak{g})
-finite vectors

\Hom^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}(\Delta^{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{M}),\nabla^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})).

Lemma 3.4.4
.

There is a natural isomorphism of
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodules

\Hom_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}(\Delta^{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{M}),\nabla^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}))\simeq\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}.

Proof.

By the tensor-hom adjunction

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}(\Delta^{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{M}),\nabla^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{q})}\mathcal{A}_{M},\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})}^{\mathrm{fin}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}))

\displaystyle\simeq\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q})}^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\Hom_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}(\mathcal{A}_{M},\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})}^{\mathrm{fin}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})))

\displaystyle\simeq\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q})}^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})}^{\mathrm{fin}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})).

Under the natural isomorphism

\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}))\simeq\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})

the subspace
\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q})}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})}^{\mathrm{fin}}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#}))
maps to
\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{q})\otimes U(\mathfrak{q}^{-})}(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M}^{\#})=\widetilde{\Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}
. The lemma follows immediately.
∎

We conclude this section by recalling the Langlands classification of irreducible Harish-Chandra bimodules and listing some easy properties of the resulting parameterization.

Definition 3.4.5
.

A
Langlands parameter
for
G
is a pair
(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}\times\mathfrak{h}^{*}
such that
\lambda_{\ell}-\lambda_{r}
integrates to a character of
H
. For any such pair, let
I_{H}^{G}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})=\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{H}\mathbb{C}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
(cf. (
3.4.1
)).

Note: in the literature, the term ‘Langlands parameter’ is often reserved for the pair
(\lambda_{\ell}-\lambda_{r},\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{r})
, where
\lambda_{\ell}-\lambda_{r}
and
\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{r}
are called, respectively, the ‘discrete’ and ‘continuous’ parameters. We prefer the more symmetric formulation above.

Theorem 3.4.6

(
[
Zel73
]
)
.

The following are true:

(i)

If
(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
is a Langlands parameter for
G
, then
I^{G}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
has a unique irreducible subquotient
\overline{I}_{H}^{G}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
with the following property:
\overline{I}_{H}^{G}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
contains the irreducible
G
-representation of extremal weight
\lambda_{\ell}-\lambda_{r}
.

(ii)

Every irreducible
G
-equivariant Harish-Chandra bimodule is isomorphic to
\overline{I}^{G}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
for some Langlands parameter
(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
.

(iii)

If
(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
and
(\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime},\lambda_{r}^{\prime})
are Langlands parameters, then
\overline{I}^{G}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})\simeq\overline{I}^{G}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime},\lambda_{r}^{\prime})
if and only if there is an element
w\in W
such that

\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime}=w\lambda_{\ell},\qquad\lambda_{r}^{\prime}=w\lambda_{r}.

Lemma 3.4.7
.

Suppose
M\subset G
is a Levi subgroup and
(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
is a Langlands parameter. The following are true:

(i)

\Ind^{G}_{M}I^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})\simeq I^{G}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
.

(ii)

If
\Ind^{G}_{M}\overline{I}^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
is irreducible, then
\Ind^{G}_{M}\overline{I}^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})\simeq\overline{I}^{G}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
.

Proof.

(i) follows immediately from the transitivity of induction, see Proposition
2.3.2
(ii). We proceed to proving (ii). Since
\overline{I}^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
is a subquotient of
I^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
and
\Ind^{G}_{M}
is exact,
\Ind^{G}_{M}\overline{I}^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
is a subquotient of
\Ind^{G}_{M}I(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})\simeq I^{G}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
. Let
V_{G}
(resp.
V_{M}
) denote the irreducible representation of
M
(resp.
G
) of extremal weight
\lambda_{\ell}-\lambda_{r}
. It suffices to show that
V_{G}\hookrightarrow\Ind^{G}_{M}\overline{I}^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
. By definition,
V_{M}\hookrightarrow\overline{I}^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
. Thus by Proposition
3.4.3
(iii),
\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}V_{M}\hookrightarrow\Ind^{G}_{M}\overline{I}^{M}_{H}(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{r})
. By Frobenius reciprocity, there is an isomorphism

\Hom_{G}(V_{G},\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}V_{M})\simeq\Hom_{M}(V_{G},V_{M})

Note that an extremal weight for
G
in
V_{G}
is also an extremal weight for
M
. Thus,
\Hom_{M}(V_{G},V_{M})\neq 0
, as desired.
∎

3.5.
{W}
-algebras

To each nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
, one can attach a finitely generated associative algebra, called the
W
-
algebra
for
\mathbb{O}
. In this section, we will recall some basic facts about these algebras and their connection to Harish-Chandra bimodules. For details and proofs, we refer the reader to
[
GG02
]
,
[
Pre02
]
, and
[
Los10
]
.

View
\mathbb{O}
as an orbit in
\mathfrak{g}

using a
G
-invariant identification
\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.
Pick
e\in\mathbb{O}
, and fix an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e,f,h)
. The
Slodowy slice
associated to
e
is the affine subspace of
\mathfrak{g}
defined by

S=e+\Ker(\ad f)\subset\mathfrak{g}.

Using the same identification
\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, we can regard
S
as an affine subspace of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. The ring of regular functions
\mathbb{C}[S]
has a number of additional structures. First, the Poisson structure on
S(\mathfrak{g})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
(defined using the Lie bracket) induces a Poisson structure on
\mathbb{C}[S]
, see
[
GG02
, Sec 3]
. Second, the reductive group
R:=Z_{G}(e,f,h)
stabilizes
S
and thus acts on
\mathbb{C}[S]
, see
[
Los10
, Sec 2]
. This action is Hamiltonian—the moment map is the restriction to
S
of the projection
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{r}^{*}
. Lastly, there is a positive algebra grading on
\mathbb{C}[S]
. To define it, consider the co-character
\gamma:\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow G
corresponding to
h
, and let
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
act on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by

t\cdot\xi=t^{-2}\Ad^{*}(\gamma(t))(\xi),\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

This action (called the
Kazhdan action
) stabilizes
S
, fixes
e
, and contracts the former onto the latter. Thus, it defines a positive grading (called the
Kazhdan grading
) on
\mathbb{C}[S]
, see
[
GG02
, Sec 4]
.

The
W
-algebra for
\mathbb{O}
is a certain filtered associative algebra
\mathcal{W}
such that
\gr(\mathcal{W})\simeq\mathbb{C}[S]
(as graded Poisson algebras). For a precise definition of
\mathcal{W}
, we refer the reader to
[
Pre02
]
, see also
[
Los10
, Sec 2.3]
. For what follows, it is important to note that
\mathcal{W}
carries a Hamiltonian action of
R
lifting that on
\mathbb{C}[S]
, see
[
Pre07
, Lemma 2.4]
.

Similarly to Definition
3.4.1
, one can define the category of
R
-equivariant Harish-Chandra bimodules for
\mathcal{W}
, denoted
\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W})
(see
[
Los11a
, Sec. 2.5]
). Write
\HC^{R}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})\subset\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W})
for the subcategory of finite-dimensional bimodules. In
[
Los11a
, Sec 3.4]
, the first-named author defines a pair of functors

\bullet_{\dagger}:\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W}),\qquad\bullet^{\dagger}:\HC^{R}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{G}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g})).

Here and below we write
\HC^{G}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
for the full subcategory in
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
consisting of all bimodules
\mathcal{B}
with
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
.

Some properties of these functors are catalogued below.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

The following are true:

(i)

\bullet_{\dagger}
is an exact monoidal functor.

(ii)

A good filtration on
\mathcal{B}
gives rise to a good filtration on
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}
with the property that the graded
\mathbb{C}[S]
-module
\gr(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})
is
R
-equivariantly identified with the pullback of
\gr\mathcal{B}
to
S
.

(iii)

In particular,
\mathbb{O}\not\subset\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})
if and only if
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}=0
. And if
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})
, then
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}
is finite dimensional, and
\dim\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}=m_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(\mathcal{B})
.

(iv)

\bullet^{\dagger}
is right adjoint to the functor
\bullet_{\dagger}:\HC^{G}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\rightarrow\HC^{R}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
.

(v)

If
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, both kernel and cokernel of the adjunction morphism
\mathcal{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
are supported in
\partial\mathbb{O}
.

Proof.

Part (ii) follows from
[
Los11a
, Lemma 3.3.2]
. The rest is

[
Los11a
, Proposition 3.4.1]
.
∎

We will make repeated use of the following lemma in our study of unipotent ideals.

Lemma 3.5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. Assume
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
and
I=\mathrm{LAnn}_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\mathcal{B})
is a maximal ideal. Then the adjunction unit
\mathcal{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Write
K,C\in\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
for the kernel and the cokernel of
\mathcal{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
. Note that
\mathcal{V}(K),\mathcal{V}(C)\subseteq\partial\mathbb{O}
by
3.5.1
(v). In particular,
\mathcal{V}((\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger})=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. We will show that
K=C=0
.

Since
K\subset\mathcal{B}
,

I=\mathrm{LAnn}_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathrm{LAnn}_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(K)
. This inclusion must be strict since
\mathcal{V}(K)\subseteq\partial\mathbb{O}\subsetneq\overline{\mathbb{O}}=\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})
. Hence,
\mathrm{LAnn}_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(K)=U(\mathfrak{g})
, i.e.
K=0
.

Now we show that
C=\{0\}
. As in the previous paragraph, it is enough to show that
IC=\{0\}
. Since
I\mathcal{B}=\{0\}
and
\mathcal{B}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
, we see that the multiplication map
I\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}\rightarrow(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
factors through

I\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{g})}C\rightarrow(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
. The image is a sub-bimodule supported on
\partial\mathbb{O}
. From (iii) and (iv) of Proposition
3.5.1
it follows that any bimodule in the image of
\bullet^{\dagger}
has no nonzero subbimodules supported on
\partial\mathbb{O}
. In particular,
I(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}=\{0\}
and hence
IC=\{0\}
.
∎

Let
\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
denote the set of primitive ideals
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
such that the associated variety
V(I)
is equal to
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Let
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
(resp.
\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
) denote the set of ideals
J\subset\mathcal{W}
(resp. primitive ideals
J\subset\mathcal{W}
) of finite codimension. Note that a primitive ideal is of finite codimension if and only if it is the annihilator of a finite-dimensional irreducible module.

If
I\in\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
, then
I_{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
by (i) and (iii) of Proposition
3.5.1
. Conversely, suppose
J\in\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
. Since the
R
action on
\mathcal{W}
is Hamiltonian,
J
is stable under the identity component
R^{\circ}
. Let
\underline{J}
denote the intersection of all
R
-conjugates of
J
. Then
\mathcal{W}/\underline{J}\in\HC_{\mathrm{fin}}^{R}(\mathcal{W})
. By (i) of Proposition
3.5.1
,
U(\mathfrak{g})_{\dagger}=\mathcal{W}
. Applying adjunction to the map
\mathcal{W}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{W}/\underline{J}
, we get a bimodule homomorphism
U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}
. Its kernel is an element in
\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
, see
[
Los10a
, Thm 1.2.2(iv),(v) and (vi)]
, which we will henceforth denote by
J^{\ddagger}
. Thus, we get maps

\bullet_{\dagger}:\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W}),\qquad\bullet^{{\ddagger}}:\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g})).

We will need the following result from
[
Los11a
]
.

Theorem 3.5.3

(Thm 1.2.2, Section 4.2,
[
Los11a
]
)
.

The following are true:

(1)

The map
\bullet^{{\ddagger}}:\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Prim_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
is surjective. Every fiber is a single orbit for the
R
-action on
\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
.

(2)

The map
\bullet_{\dagger}:\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
is injective. Moreover,
for every
I\in\Prim_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
, we have

I_{\dagger}=\bigcap J
, where the intersection is taken over all
J\in\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
such that
J^{{\ddagger}}=I
.

In view of Theorem
3.5.3
, we can make the following definition.

Definition 3.5.4
.

If
I\in\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
, define

\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathcal{W}-\dim}}(I):=\sqrt{\dim(\mathcal{W}/J)}

for
J\in\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})
such that
I=J^{{\ddagger}}
. The number
\operatorname{\mathcal{W}-\dim}(I)
is called the
\mathcal{W}
-
dimension
of
I
. It is well-defined by Theorem
3.5.3
and coincides with the dimension of the irreducible
\mathcal{W}
-module annihilated by
J
.

Let
J\subset\mathcal{W}
be a two-sided ideal of codimension
1
and define
\underline{J}\subset\mathcal{W}
as in the paragraph preceding Theorem
3.5.3
. Let
R_{1}
denote the stabilizer of
J
in
R
and
H_{1}
its preimage in
G_{e}
, a finite index subgroup. The Harish-Chandra bimodule

(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}
has a natural algebra structure, see the discussion preceding
[
Los16
, Lemma 5.2]
. The homomorphism
U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}
is that of algebras. By the construction in
[
Los11a
, Section 3.3]
, this algebra has a distinguished algebra filtration. The next lemma follows from the proof of
[
Los11a
, Lemma 3.3.3]
.

Lemma 3.5.5
.

There is an inclusion of
G
-equivariant graded algebras

\gr[(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[G/H_{1}]
, and hence an inclusion of
G
-representations
(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[G/H_{1}]
.

Corollary 3.5.6
.

Let
J
be a codimension
1
ideal in
\mathcal{W}
. Then the ideal

J^{\ddagger}
is completely prime.

Proof.

Let
H_{1},\underline{J}
have the same meaning as in Lemma
3.5.5
.
Since the variety
G/H_{1}
is irreducible, the algebra
\mathbb{C}[G/H_{1}]
is a domain. Thus, by Lemma
3.5.5
, the same is true of
\gr[(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}]
, and hence
(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}
. Note that there is an algebra embedding
U(\mathfrak{g})/J^{\ddagger}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}
. The corollary follows.
∎

4.
Deformations and quantizations of conical symplectic singularities

In this section, we will review some basic facts about Poisson deformations and filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities.

4.1.
Poisson deformations and filtered quantizations

Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and let
A
be a
graded Poisson algebra
of degree
-d
. By this, we will mean a finitely-generated commutative associative unital algebra equipped with two additional structures: an algebra grading

A=\bigoplus_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}A_{i}

and a Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
of degree
-d

\{A_{i},A_{j}\}\subset A_{i+j-d},\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}.

To any algebra of this form, one can associate various classes of filtered associative algebras which ‘deform’ the Poisson bracket in a suitable sense. In this paper, we will consider two such classes of algebras:
filtered Poisson deformations
and
filtered quantizations
. The definitions are below.

Definition 4.1.1
.

•

A
filtered Poisson deformation
of
A
is a pair
(\mathcal{A}^{0},\theta)
consisting of

(i)

a Poisson algebra
\mathcal{A}^{0}
, equipped with a complete and separated filtration by subspaces

\mathcal{A}^{0}=\bigcup_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\leq i},\qquad...\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{0}_{-1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{0}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{0}_{1}\subseteq...

such that

\{\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\leq i},\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\leq j}\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\leq i+j-d},\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{Z},

and

(ii)

an isomorphism of graded Poisson algebras

\theta:\gr(\mathcal{A}^{0})\simeq A,

where the Poisson bracket on
\gr(\mathcal{A}^{0})
is defined by

\{a+\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\leq i-1},b+\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\leq j-1}\}:=\{a,b\}+\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\leq i+j-d-1},\qquad a\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq i}^{0},\ b\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq j}^{0}.

•

An isomorphism of filtered Poisson deformations
(\mathcal{A}^{0}_{1},\theta_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{A}^{0}_{2},\theta_{2})
is an isomorphism of filtered Poisson algebras
\phi:\mathcal{A}^{0}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{0}_{2}
such that
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}\circ\gr(\phi)
. Denote the set of isomorphism classes of filtered Poisson deformations of
A
by
\mathrm{PDef}(A)
.

Definition 4.1.2
.

•

A
filtered quantization
of
A
is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\theta)
consisting of

(i)

an associative algebra
\mathcal{A}
equipped with a complete and separated filtration by subspaces

\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{\leq i},\qquad...\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq-1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq 1}\subseteq...

such that

[\mathcal{A}_{\leq i},\mathcal{A}_{\leq j}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq i+j-d}\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{Z},

and

(ii)

an isomorphism of graded Poisson algebras

\theta:\gr(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}A,

where the Poisson bracket on
\gr(\mathcal{A})
is defined by

\{a+\mathcal{A}_{\leq i-1},b+\mathcal{A}_{\leq j-1}\}=[a,b]+\mathcal{A}_{\leq i+j-d-1},\qquad a\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq i},\ b\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq j}.

•

An isomorphism of filtered quantizations
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\theta_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{2},\theta_{2})
is an isomorphism of filtered algebras
\phi:\mathcal{A}_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}\circ\gr(\phi)
. Denote the set of isomorphism classes of quantizations of
A
by
\mathrm{Quant}(A)
.

Remark 4.1.3
.

In many cases we will consider, the grading on
A
is by nonnegative integers. In such cases, the filtrations considered in Definitions
4.1.2
and
4.1.1
are automatically complete and separated.

Now let
X
be a
graded Poisson variety
. By this, we will mean a normal quasi-projective variety with a rational
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action and a Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}:\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{O}_{X}
of degree
-d
. We will consider the so-called
conical topology
on
X
. The open subsets in this topology are the Zariski-open subsets
U\subset X
which are stable under
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. By a result of Sumihiro, every point in
X
admits a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-stable open affine neighborhood (see
[
PV94
, Thm 1.2]
). When viewed in the conical topology,
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a sheaf of graded Poisson algebras.

Definition 4.1.4
.

•

A
filtered quantization
of
X
is a pair
(\mathcal{D},\theta)
consisting of

(i)

a sheaf
\mathcal{D}
of associative algebras in the conical topology on
X
, equipped with a complete and separated filtration by subsheaves of vector spaces

\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{D}_{\leq i},\qquad...\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\leq-1}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\leq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\leq 1}\subseteq...

such that

[\mathcal{D}_{\leq i},\mathcal{D}_{\leq j}]\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\leq i+j-d},

and

(ii)

an isomorphism of sheaves of graded Poisson algebras

\theta:\gr(\mathcal{D})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}_{X}.

•

An isomorphism of filtered quantizations
(\mathcal{D}_{1},\theta_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{D}_{2},\theta_{2})
is an isomorphism of sheaves of filtered algebras
\phi:\mathcal{D}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{D}_{2}
such that
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}\circ\gr(\phi)
. Denote the set of isomorphism classes of filtered quantizations of
X
by
\mathrm{Quant}(X)
.

Filtered Poisson deformations of
X
can be defined in a similar way. Often, the isomorphism
\theta
is clear from the context, and will be omitted from the notation. However, the reader should keep in mind that a Poisson deformation
(\mathcal{A}^{0},\theta)
(resp. filtered quantization
(\mathcal{A},\theta)
) is
not
determined up to isomorphism by
\mathcal{A}^{0}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}
) alone.

Suppose
A=\mathbb{C}[X]
, where
X
is a graded
affine
Poisson variety. Of course,
A
is a graded Poisson algebra of degree
-d
. If
\mathcal{D}
is a filtered quantization of
X
, then
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{D})
is a filtered quantization of
A
. The correspondence
\mathcal{D}\mapsto\Gamma(X,\mathcal{D})
defines a bijection
\mathrm{Quant}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Quant}(A)
, see e.g.
[
Gin86
, Sec 1]
. The inverse bijection
\mathrm{Quant}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Quant}(X)
can be defined as follows. Given
\mathcal{A}\in\mathrm{Quant}(A)
, form the corresponding Rees algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}:=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{A}_{\leq i}\hbar^{i}.

Note that
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}/(\hbar-1)\simeq\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}/(\hbar)\simeq\gr(\mathcal{A})\simeq A
. For every homogeneous element
f\in A
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, choose a lift
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,i}:=\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}/(\hbar^{i})
of
f
. It is easy to check that the multiplicative system
\{\widetilde{f}_{i}^{n}\}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,i}
satisfies the Ore conditions (the key observation is that
\ad(\widetilde{f}_{i})^{i}=0
, which follows from the commutativity of
A
). Hence, there is a localization
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,i}[\widetilde{f}_{i}^{-1}]
, a graded
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]/(\hbar^{i})
-algebra. It is easy to check that this localization is independent of the lift. Consider the inverse limit
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}[f^{-1}]:=\lim_{i\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\infty}\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,i}[\widetilde{f}_{i}^{-1}]
(with respect to the natural homomorphisms
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,i+1}[\widetilde{f}_{i+1}^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,i}[\widetilde{f}_{i}^{-1}]
) in the category of graded algebras. Then
\mathcal{A}[f^{-1}]:=\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}[f^{-1}]/(\hbar-1)
is a filtered quantization of the graded Poisson algebra
A[f^{-1}]
. The algebras
\mathcal{A}[f^{-1}]
, as
f
runs over the homogeneous elements in
A
, form a sheaf on
X
, denoted by
\mathcal{D}
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{Quant}(X)
and
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{D})\simeq\mathcal{A}
. This sheaf is called the
microlocalization
of
\mathcal{A}
over
X
.

4.2.
Kleinian Singularities and the McKay correspondence

Kleinian singularities
are an important class of 2-dimensional graded Poisson varieties. In this section, we will review some basic facts about such varieties. For details and proofs, we refer the reader to
[
McK80
]
and
[
GV83
]
.

Let
\Gamma
be a nontrivial finite subgroup of
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
. Write
\{V_{1},...,V_{n}\}
for the nontrivial irreducible representations of
\Gamma
and
V
for the 2-dimensional tautological representation of
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2)
. For
i,j\in\{1,...,n\}
, define a nonnegative integer

m_{ij}:=\dim\Hom_{\Gamma}(V_{j},V_{i}\otimes V).

The
McKay graph
of
\Gamma
is the graph with vertices
\{V_{1},...,V_{n}\}
and
m_{ij}
(non-oriented) edges between
V_{i}
and
V_{j}
.

Theorem 4.2.1

(
[
McK80
]
, Props 3,4)
.

The McKay graph of
\Gamma
is a simply laced Dynkin diagram. The passage from
\Gamma
to its McKay graph defines a bijection

\{\text{nontrivial finite subgroups }\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2)\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\text{simple root systems of type }ADE\}.

With
\Gamma
as above, consider the quotient variety
\Sigma:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
. The variety
\Sigma
is called the
Kleinian singularity
corresponding to
\Gamma
.
The symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
induces a Poisson bracket on
\mathbb{C}[\Sigma]=\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]^{\Gamma}
of degree
-2
, making
\Sigma
a graded Poisson variety of the type described in Section
4.1
. Consider the mininal resolution
\rho:\mathfrak{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Sigma
. The exceptional divisor
\rho^{-1}(0)
is a union of components
\{C_{1},...,C_{m}\}
, each isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
, intersecting transversely, see e.g.
[
Slo80
, Sec 6.1]
. The
intersection graph
of
\mathfrak{S}
is the (undirected) graph with vertices
\{C_{1},...,C_{n}\}
and an edge between
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
if and only if
C_{i}\cap C_{j}\neq\emptyset
. The correspondence of Theorem
4.2.1
has a geometric interpretation, due to Gonzalez-Springberg and Verdier.

Theorem 4.2.2

(
[
GV83
]
, Thm 2.2)
.

The McKay graph of
\Gamma
is isomorphic to the intersection graph of
\mathfrak{S}
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the simple complex Lie algebra of type ADE corresponding to
\Sigma
via Theorem
4.2.1
. Choose a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, and let
\Delta,\Lambda\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be the root system and weight lattice, respectively. Choose simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}\}\subset\Delta
. By Theorems
4.2.1
and
4.2.2
, there are bijections

(4.2.1)

\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}\}\simeq\{C_{1},...,C_{n}\}\simeq\{V_{1},...,V_{n}\}

well-defined up to a diagram automorphism. Arrange the indices so that
\alpha_{i}
corresponds to
C_{i}
and
V_{i}
. The following proposition is an easy consequence of
[
GV83
, Thm 2.2]
.

Proposition 4.2.3
.

There is a group isomorphism

\sigma:\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pic(\mathfrak{S})

with the following property: if
\sigma(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}
, then

\langle\mathcal{L},C_{i}\rangle=\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle,\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

Remark 4.2.4
.

The map

c_{1}:\Pic(\mathfrak{S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(\mathfrak{S},\mathbb{Z})

which takes each line bundle
\mathcal{L}
to its first Chern class
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})
is an isomorphism. Indeed
\mathfrak{S}
comes equipped with a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action which contracts it onto the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution
\mathfrak{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Sigma
.
This gives an identification of
H^{2}(\mathfrak{S},\mathbb{Z})
with the second cohomology group of the exceptional divisor, which has basis indexed by the irreducible components. The claim that
c_{1}
is an isomorphism easily follows.

4.3.
Symplectic singularities

Let
X
be a normal Poisson variety.

Definition 4.3.1

(
[
Bea00
]
, Def 1.1)
.

We say that
X
has
symplectic singularities
if

(i)

the regular locus
X^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset X
is symplectic; denote the symplectic form by
\omega^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

(ii)

there is a resolution of singularities
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
such that
\rho^{*}(\omega^{\mathrm{reg}})
extends to a regular (not necessarily symplectic)
2
-form on
Y
.

If (ii) holds for one resolution, it holds for all others,
[
Bea00
, Sec 1.2]
. In particular, if a normal Poisson variety
X^{\prime}
admits a a birational projective Poisson morphism to
X
, then
X^{\prime}
has symplectic singularities as well.

Lemma 4.3.2

(
[
Bea00
]
, Prop 1.3)
.

Suppose
X
has symplectic singularities. Then
X
has rational singularities. That is, for any proper birational map
\eta:Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
the following are true:

(i)

\eta_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Z}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}_{X}
,

(ii)

R^{i}\eta_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Z}=0
for
i>0
.

In this paper, we will give special attention to so-called
conical symplectic singularities
.

Definition 4.3.3
.

A
conical symplectic singularity
is a normal graded Poisson variety
X
with symplectic singularities such that the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
X
contracts it to a point.

Note that a conical symplectic singularity
X
is in particular a graded Poisson variety of the type considered in Section
4.1
. Furthermore, the contracting
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action guarantees that
X
is affine.

Example 4.3.4
.

The following are examples of conical symplectic singularities

(i)

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2)
be a finite subgroup. Then the Kleinian singularity
\Sigma=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Bea00
, Prop 2.4]
. For
\rho
we take the minimal resolution
\mathfrak{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Sigma
.

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex reductive Lie algebra and let
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a nilpotent orbit. Then
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Bea00
, Sec 2.5]
.

(iii)

In the setting of
(ii)
, let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
be a connected finite étale cover. Then
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Los21
, Lem 2.5]
.

For an arbitrary variety
X
, define the subvarieties
X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},...
as follows:
X_{0}:=X
and
X_{k+1}:=X_{k}-X_{k}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. If
X
is Poisson, then all
X_{k}
are Poisson subvarieties of
X
.

Definition 4.3.5
.

We say that
X
has finitely many (symplectic) leaves if
X_{k}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a symplectic variety for all
k
. By a symplectic leaf of
X
we mean an irreducible (i.e. connected) component of
X_{k}^{\mathrm{reg}}
for some
k
.

The following lemma is elementary.

Lemma 4.3.6
.

Let
X
be a Poisson variety with finitely many leaves. Then the leaves are in bijection with prime Poisson ideals in
\mathbb{C}[X]
; to a sympletic leaf
\mathcal{L}\subset X
one assigns the ideal of all functions which vanish on
\mathcal{L}
. Furthermore, if
I\subset\mathbb{C}[X]
is an arbitrary Poisson ideal, the variety of zeroes
V(I)\subset X
is a union of symplectic leaves.

The following important result is Theorem 2.3 in
[
Kal06
]
.

Proposition 4.3.7
.

Suppose
X
has symplectic singularities. Then
X
has finitely many leaves.

4.4.
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalizations

Let
X
be a normal Poisson variety with symplectic singularities. Recall that a normal variety
Y
is
\mathbb{Q}
-
factorial
if every Weil divisor has a (nonzero) integer multiple which is Cartier. The following is a consequence of
[
BCHM10
]
(see
[
Los19
, Prop 2.1]
for a proof).

Proposition 4.4.1
.

There is a birational projective morphism
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
such that

(i)

Y
is an irreducible, normal, Poisson variety (in particular,
Y
has symplectic singularities).

(ii)

Y
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial.

(iii)

Y
has terminal singularities.

Remark 4.4.2
.

Modulo (i), (iii) is equivalent to the condition that the singular locus of
Y
is of codimension
\geq 4
, see
[
Nam01
, Main Thm]
. In practice, the latter condition is often easier to check.

The map
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
in the proposition above (or the variety
Y
itself, if the map is understood) is called a
\mathbb{Q}
-
factorial terminalization
. If
X
is conical, then
Y
admits a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action such that
\rho
is
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant, see
[
Nam08
, A.7]
.

Example 4.4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex reductive Lie algebra and let
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its nilpotent cone. By Example
4.3.4
(ii) (and the normality of
\mathcal{N})
,
X:=\mathcal{N}
is a conical symplectic singularity. For
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
we take the Springer resolution
T^{*}(G/B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
. In fact, this example can be generalized to all varieties of the form
X:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a nilpotent cover, see Section
7.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}_{1},...\mathfrak{L}_{t}\subset X
be the symplectic leaves of codimension 2 – there are finitely many by Proposition
4.3.7
. For any proper birational morphism
\rho:Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
, consider the open subset

Z^{2}:=\rho^{-1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}}\cup\mathfrak{L}_{1}\cup...\cup\mathfrak{L}_{t})\subset Z

Lemma 4.4.4
.

\codim(Z-Z^{2},Z)\geq 2
.

Proof.

If
\rho:Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization, a proof is contained in
[
Los16
, Prop 2.14]
. In general, let
\overline{\rho}:\overline{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}Z
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization of
Z
. Then
\rho\circ\overline{\rho}:\overline{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization of
X
. Note that
\overline{\rho}^{-1}(Z^{2})\subseteq(\rho\circ\overline{\rho})^{-1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}}\cup\mathfrak{L}_{1}\cup...\cup\mathfrak{L}_{t})
. Thus,

\codim(Z-Z^{2},Z)\geq\codim(\overline{Z}-\overline{\rho}^{-1}(Z^{2}),\overline{Z})\geq\codim(\overline{Z}-(\rho\circ\overline{\rho})^{-1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}}\cup\mathfrak{L}_{1}\cup...\cup\mathfrak{L}_{t}),\overline{Z})\geq 2

∎

Definition 4.4.5
.

Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity and
Y
a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization of
X
.
The
Namikawa space
associated to
X
is the complex vector space

\mathfrak{P}:=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C}).

We will see below that
\mathfrak{P}
depends only on
X
(and not on
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
), justifying the terminology. In Section
4.7
we will see that
\mathfrak{P}
plays a central role in the classification of Poisson deformations and filtered quantizations of
X
, and this is the main reason we consider it.

Note that
\mathfrak{P}
comes equipped with a natural
\mathbb{Q}
-form, namely
H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Q})
. So it makes sense to consider the rational and real Namikawa spaces
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathbb{R}}:=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{R})
. Consider the first Chern class map

c_{1}:\Pic(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Z}).

The following proposition provides an alternative characterization of
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Lemma 4.4.6
.

c_{1}
induces an isomorphism of vector spaces

(4.4.1)

\Pic(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Q}).

Proof.

Henceforth, we will use the superscript ‘an’ for complex analytic objects. For example,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}}_{Y^{\mathrm{reg}}}
will denote the sheaf of complex analytic functions on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
. The idea of the proof is as follows. We will first show that the the lemma holds if we replace the algebraic Picard group
\Pic(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})
with its analytic counterpart
\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})
– this is easy. Passing from
\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})
to
\Pic(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})
requires a technical argument. Namely, we will show that every analytic line bundle on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a multiple with a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant structure for the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
restricted from the contracting
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
Y
. An equivariant analytic line bundle on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
can be pushed forward to obtain a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant analytic coherent sheaf on
Y
. To complete the proof, we appeal to a version of the GAGA principle.

Step 1
. Recall that
\operatorname{Pic}^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an},\times})
. Consider the exponential exact sequence of sheaves on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an},\times}\rightarrow 0

and the corresponding long exact sequence in cohomology

H^{1}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\xrightarrow{c_{1}}H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{1}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}}).

In Step 2, we will show that
the first and the fourth terms vanish, and hence that
c_{1}
is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Pic}^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Z})
.

Step 2
. Next we prove that

(4.4.2)

H^{j}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})=0\text{ for }j=1,2.

The variety
Y
is singular symplectic, hence Cohen-Macaulay (singular symplectic implies Gorenstein by
[
Bea00
, Proposition 1.3]
. It is a standard fact that Gorenstein implies Cohen-Macaulay). This in particular implies that the stalks of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}}_{Y}
are Cohen-Macaulay. It follows that the cohomology with support
H^{i}_{Y^{\mathrm{reg}}}(Y,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})
is
0
for
i\leqslant 3
. Thus

(4.4.3)

H^{j}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{j}(Y,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}}),\text{ for }j\leqslant 2.

Since
Y
is singular symplectic,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}}
coincides with the dualizing sheaf
K^{\mathrm{an}}
. By the Grauert-Riemenschneider theorem,

R^{i}_{*}\rho K^{\mathrm{an}}=0
for
i>0
. Since
X
is affine and hence Stein, this implies that
H^{k}(Y,K^{\mathrm{an}})=0
for all
k>0
. This, together with the isomorphism
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}}\simeq K^{\mathrm{an}}
and
(
4.4.3
), implies
(
4.4.2
) and hence the isomorphism

c_{1}:\operatorname{Pic}^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Z})
.

Step 3
. Next we show that every analytic line bundle on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a multiple with a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant structure.
Pick a sufficiently large integer
n
and consider the complex analytic manifold
E^{n}:=\mathbb{C}^{n}-\{0\}

(to be understood as an approximation of
E(\mathbb{C}^{\times})
). This manifold has a free action of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
with quotient
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.

Consider the product
\mathbb{Y}:=Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\times E^{n}
. This too admits a free action of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, i.e. the diagonal one, and the quotient variety
\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a fiber bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
with fiber
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Step 4
. Let
\pi
denote the projection
\mathbb{Y}\twoheadrightarrow Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
. We claim that every analytic line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathbb{Y}
is isomorphic to the pullback under
\pi
of a line bundle on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Since
H^{i}(Y^{reg},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})=0
for
i=1,2
(see Step 2) and
H^{i}(E^{n},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})=0
for
0<i<n-1
, we have

H^{i}(\mathbb{Y},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})=0
for
i=1,2
. Hence, the exponential sequence for
\mathbb{Y}
induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Pic}^{\mathrm{an}}(\mathbb{Y})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
. By the Künneth formula,
\pi^{*}
induces an isomorphism

H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
. Since the exponential sequence is functorial with respect to pullbacks, we see that
\pi^{*}
is an isomorphism

(4.4.4)

\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\simeq\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(\mathbb{Y}).

An inverse for (
4.4.4
) can be
constructed as follows. Let
\iota
denote the inclusion
\mathbb{Y}\hookrightarrow Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Thanks to (
4.4.4
), we see that

\iota_{*}\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Restricting
\iota_{*}\mathcal{L}
to
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\{0\}
defines an inverse for
\pi^{*}
.

Step 5
. We have
H^{i}(\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})=H^{i}(\mathbb{Y},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{an}})^{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
. By Step 4, the right hand side vanishes.
Therefore
c_{1}:\operatorname{Pic}^{\mathrm{an}}(\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times})\rightarrow H^{2}(\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times},\mathbb{Z})
is an isomorphism. Now we compute the base change of the target to
\mathbb{C}
.

By
[
Los16
, Lemma 2.15]

H^{1}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=0
. Also
H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{C})=0
. Using the Serre spectral sequence for the fiber bundle
\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, we see that

H^{2}(\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times},\mathbb{C})\simeq H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})\oplus H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{C})
. Equivalently, the following sequence is exact

(4.4.5)

0\rightarrow\mathbb{C}=H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow 0.

Here the first map is the pullback under
\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
and the second is the pullback under the fiber inclusion
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Step 6
. Consider the equivariant Picard group
\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})
. Forgetting equivariance, we get a group homomorphism

\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})

We claim that the cokernel is torsion.

Since the first Chern class map commutes with pullbacks from (
4.4.5
), the cokernel of the homomorphism

(4.4.6)

\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times})\rightarrow\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})

is torsion. Furthermore
\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(\mathbb{Y}/\mathbb{C}^{\times})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathbb{Y})
.
By Step 4,
\iota_{*}\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
for all line bundles
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathbb{Y}
. It follow that there is an isomorphism
\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathbb{Y})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})
given by push-forward. Under this identification, (
4.4.6
) becomes the pullback map under restriction to
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\{0\}
and forgetting equivariance. In particular, the cokernel of

\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\rightarrow\Pic^{\mathrm{an}}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})
is torsion. Equivalently, every line bundle on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
has a multiple which admits a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant structure.

Step 7
. Next, we prove that every
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant analytic line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
comes from an algebraic line bundle. Let
\iota
denote the inclusion
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow Y
. Since
\operatorname{codim}_{Y}(Y^{\mathrm{sing}})\geqslant 3
, the push-forward
\mathcal{F}:=\iota_{*}\mathcal{L}
is an analytic coherent sheaf, see
[
Siu71
, Theorem 5]
. It comes with a natural
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant structure. As usual, let
\rho
denote the projective morphism
Y\rightarrow X
. In Steps 8-9, we will show that the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to
Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
is the analytification of an algebraic coherent sheaf. We will now explain how this implies the claim of the lemma.

If
\dim Y=2
(equivalently, if
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is the minimal resolution of a Kleinian singularity), the claim of the lemma follows from Remark
4.2.4
. Thus, we can assume
\dim Y\geqslant 4
. In this case
\rho^{-1}(0)
has codimension at least
2
, see Lemma
4.4.4
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}
denote an algebraic coherent sheaf on
Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
with
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{an}}\simeq\mathcal{F}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}
denote the restriction of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
to
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}-\rho^{-1}(0)
so that the restriction of
\mathcal{L}
to
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}-\rho^{-1}(0)
coincides with the analytification of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
.
Write
\iota_{1}
for the inclusion
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}-\rho^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
. By Step 2 of the proof of
[
LB21
, Proposition 3.2]
,
\mathcal{L}
, which coincides with the analytic pushforward of its restriction to
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}-\rho^{-1}(0)
, coincides with the analytification of
\iota_{1*}\mathcal{L}_{1}
. It follows that
\iota_{1*}\mathcal{L}_{1}
is a line bundle. So
\mathcal{L}
is the analytification of an algebraic line bundle, as asserted.

It remains to show that the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to
Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
is the analytification of an algebraic coherent sheaf. This will be accomplished using the GAGA principle in Steps 8-9.

Step 8
. Consider a more general situation: let
\rho:Y\rightarrow X
a projective morphism onto an affine variety
X
. Suppose that
\rho^{*}:\mathbb{C}[X]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[Y]
is an isomorphism. Further, assume that
X,Y
are equipped with
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-actions such that
\mathbb{C}[X]
is positively graded and
\rho
is
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant. In particular, there is a point
0\in X
. We claim that every
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant analytic coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
is the analytification of
an algebraic
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant coherent sheaf.

Assume first that
\mathbb{C}[X]
is generated by degree
1
elements. We claim that there is a unique
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant algebraic coherent sheaf on
Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
whose analytification is the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
.

Since
\mathbb{C}[X]
is generated by degree
1
elements, the action of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
on
X-\{0\}
is free with quotient
\operatorname{Proj}(\mathbb{C}[X])
. We can form the GIT quotient of
Y
by the action of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}

(with the trivial line bundle). This quotient is
(Y-\rho^{-1}(0))/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. The latter variety is projective over
(X-\{0\})/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, hence is projective. Form the analytic coherent sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
on

(Y-\rho^{-1}(0))/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
obtained from
\mathcal{F}|_{Y-\rho^{-1}(0)}
by equivariant descent. By the GAGA principle, it comes from a unique algebraic coherent sheaf
on
(Y-\rho^{-1}(0))/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, to be denoted by
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}
denote the pullback of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
to

Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
. The analytification of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}
. The uniqueness (of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and hence of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
) again follows from the GAGA principle: the analytification functor on a projective variety is a category equivalence.

Step 9
.
Finally, we will prove the lemma in the general case, i.e. when
\mathbb{C}[X]
need not be generated by elements of degree
1
.
There is
e\geqslant 1
such that the subalgebra
\mathbb{C}[X]_{(e)}:=\bigoplus_{i\geqslant 0}\mathbb{C}[X]_{ie}
(where
\mathbb{C}[X]_{ie}

denotes the graded component of degree
ie
) is generated by elements of degree
e
. Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the subgroup of
e
th roots of unity. Consider the quotients
X/\Gamma,Y/\Gamma
. Let
\rho_{(e)}:Y/\Gamma\rightarrow X/\Gamma

be the natural morphism and
\varpi:Y\rightarrow Y/\Gamma
the quotient morphism. Consider the sheaf
\varpi_{*}\mathcal{F}
. It splits as a direct sum
\bigoplus\mathcal{F}(i)
, where
\mathcal{F}(i)
is the eigensheaf for the action of
\Gamma
with eigenvalue
t\mapsto t^{i},t\in\Gamma,i=0,\ldots,e-1
. The sheaf
\mathcal{F}(i)
is
\mathbb{C}^{\times}/\Gamma
-equivariant—we twist the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on

\mathcal{F}(i)
by
t\mapsto t^{-i}
. The action of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}/\Gamma
on
X/\Gamma
satisfies the conditions of Step 8. So we can find a unique
\mathbb{C}^{\times}/\Gamma
-equivariant algebraic coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}_{1}(i)
on
Y/\Gamma-\rho_{(e)}^{-1}(0)

whose analytification is
\mathcal{F}(i)
. Form the sheaf
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{e-1}\mathcal{F}_{1}(i)
on
Y/\Gamma-\rho_{(e)}^{-1}(0)
. Note that the analytification of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is the restriction of
\varpi_{*}\mathcal{F}
. By the functoriality of the construction,

\mathcal{F}_{1}
is a sheaf of
\varpi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules. So we can regard it as a sheaf on
Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
. The analytification is
still the restriction of
\mathcal{F}

to
Y-\rho^{-1}(0)
.

∎

If we choose a different
\mathbb{Q}
-terminalization
Y_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
, there is a canonical isomorphism
\Pic(Y_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}})\simeq\Pic(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})
, see
[
BLPW16
, Prop 2.18]
. In particular, in view of Lemma
4.4.6
, the Namikawa space
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is independent of the choice of
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
.

4.5.
Structure of Namikawa space

Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity, and choose a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
as in Section
4.4
. Following
[
Nam11
]
and
[
Los16
]
, we will provide a description of
\mathfrak{P}
in terms of the geometry of
X
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}_{1},...,\mathfrak{L}_{t}\subset X
be the symplectic leaves of codimension 2. For each
\mathfrak{L}_{k}
, the formal slice to
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
is identified with the formal neighborhood at
0
in a Kleinian singularity
\Sigma_{k}=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma_{k}
. We will call
\Sigma_{k}
the ‘singularity’ of the leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
. Let
\mathfrak{g}_{k}
be the corresponding simple complex Lie algebra of type ADE (cf. Theorem
4.2.1
). Fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{k}
, and let
\Delta_{k}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
,
\Lambda_{k}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
, and
W_{k}
be the root system, weight lattice, and Weyl group, respectively. Let
\rho_{k}:\mathfrak{S}_{k}\rightarrow\Sigma_{k}
be the minimal resolution. Recall, see Remark
4.2.4
, that
\rho_{k}^{-1}(0)
is homotopically equivalent to
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
due to the contracting
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on the latter. In particular, there is a natural identification
H^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{k},\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{2}(\rho_{k}^{-1}(0),\mathbb{Z})
. Now choose a point
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{k}
. Since
\rho:Y\rightarrow X
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization, it gives a symplectic resolution over codimension
2
leaves. Hence there is an identification
\rho^{-1}(x)\simeq\rho_{k}^{-1}(0)
. The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
acts by monodromy on the irreducible components
\{C_{i}\}
of
\rho_{k}^{-1}(0)
, and this action preserves the intersection graph of
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
. Thus,
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
acts by diagram automorphisms on
\Delta_{k}
—acting on the simple roots via the bijection (
4.2.1
)—and hence also on
\Delta_{k}
,
\Lambda_{k}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
, and
W_{k}
. The
partial Namikawa space
for
\mathfrak{L}_{k}
is the space of monodromy invariants

\mathfrak{P}_{k}:=(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}.

Since
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization,
\rho^{-1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})\subset Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
. The embedding
\rho_{k}^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
gives rise to a map on cohomology

\mathfrak{P}:=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{k},\mathbb{C})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}\simeq\mathfrak{P}_{k}.

This map is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Also, define

\mathfrak{P}_{0}:=H^{2}(X^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C}).

The embedding
X^{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
gives rise to a map

\mathfrak{P}=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(X^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{P}_{0},

also defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proposition 4.5.1

(
[
Los16
]
, Lem 2.8)
.

The maps
\mathfrak{P}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}_{k}
defined above induce a linear isomorphism

(4.5.1)

\mathfrak{P}\simeq\bigoplus_{k=0}^{t}\mathfrak{P}_{k},\qquad\lambda\mapsto(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{t}).

We will now define the
Namikawa Weyl group
associated to
X
. For each codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
, consider the subgroup of monodromy invariants
W_{k}^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}\subset W_{k}
. Note that there is a natural action of
W_{k}^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}
on
\mathfrak{P}_{k}=(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}
. The Namikawa Weyl group associated to
X
is the product

W:=\prod_{k=1}^{t}W_{k}^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}.

W
acts on
\mathfrak{P}
via the isomorphism (
4.5.1
) (the action on
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
is trivial).

4.6.
Finite covers of conical symplectic singularities

Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity. In this section, we will define the notion of a
finite cover
of
X
. Let
p^{\prime}:\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X^{\mathrm{reg}}
be a finite étale cover of the regular locus
X^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset X
. Rescaling if necessary, we can arrange so that the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
lifts to
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
. Consider the composition
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\overset{p^{\prime}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}X^{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow X
and its Stein factorization
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{reg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Note that
\widetilde{X}
is affine and
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
embeds into
\widetilde{X}
as an open subvariety. Since
\operatorname{codim}(X^{\mathrm{sing}},X)\geqslant 2
and
p:\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is finite, we have that
\codim(\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}^{\prime},\widetilde{X})\geq 2
. Thus the algebra
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}^{\prime}]
is finitely generated and

\widetilde{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}^{\prime}])
. In particular, the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
extends to
\widetilde{X}
. In fact,
\widetilde{X}
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Los21
, Lemma 2.5]
. A map
p:\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
obtained in this fashion is called a
finite cover
of
X
. We say that
p
is
Galois
if its restriction to
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
is Galois.

Lemma 4.6.1
.

Let
p:\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
be a finite cover and
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{X}
a symplectic leaf of codimension
d
. Then there is a symplectic leaf
\mathfrak{L}\subset X
of codimension
d
such that
p(\overline{\mathfrak{L}}^{\prime})=\overline{\mathfrak{L}}
.

Proof.

The assignment
\mathfrak{L}\mapsto I(\overline{\mathfrak{L}})
defines a bijection between the set of symplectic leaves in
X
(resp.
\widetilde{X}
) and the set of prime Poisson ideals in
\mathbb{C}[X]
(resp.
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}]
),
Lemma
4.3.6
. If
I
is a prime Poisson ideal in
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}]
, then
I\cap\mathbb{C}[X]
is a prime Poisson ideal in
\mathbb{C}[X]
. Thus,
p(\overline{\mathfrak{L}}^{\prime})
is the closure of a symplectic leaf in
X
, say
\mathfrak{L}
. Since
p
is finite, it preserves codimension. Thus,
\mathfrak{L}\subset X
is of codimension
d
.
∎

Next, we relate the dimension 2 singularities in
X
and
\widetilde{X}
. Let
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
be a codimension 2 leaf and
\Sigma_{k}=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma_{k}
the corresponding Kleinian singularity. Let
\Gamma
denote the algebraic fundamantal group
\pi^{\mathrm{alg}}_{1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})
of
X^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset X
. By
[
Gro71
, Cor 5.2]
,
\Gamma
is the profinite completion of the usual fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})
. And, by
[
Nam17
, Main Thm]
,
\Gamma
is finite. Thus,
\Gamma
is the maximal finite quotient of
\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})
. There is a natural map
\Sigma_{k}^{\wedge}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
, where we write
\Sigma_{k}^{\wedge}

for the spectrum of the completion

\mathbb{C}[\Sigma_{k}]^{\wedge_{0}}
of

\mathbb{C}[\Sigma_{k}]
at
0
. Since
\Sigma_{k}^{\wedge,\mathrm{reg}}
maps to
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
, there is an induced homomorphism

(4.6.1)

\phi_{k}:\Gamma_{k}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{alg}}(\Sigma_{k}^{\wedge,\mathrm{reg}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{alg}}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})=\Gamma.

Choose a point
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{k}
and let
p^{-1}(x)=\{\widetilde{x}_{j}\}
. Write
X^{\wedge x}
(resp.
\widetilde{X}^{\wedge\widetilde{x}_{j}}
) for the completion of
X
(resp.
\widetilde{X}
) at
x
(resp.
\widetilde{x}_{j}
), i.e.
X^{\wedge_{x}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[X]^{\wedge_{x}})
. There is a Cartesian diagram of schemes

(4.6.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{j}\widetilde{X}^{\wedge\widetilde{x}_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\wedge x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

Now suppose
\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is Galois with Galois group
\Pi
so that
\Pi
acts faithfully on
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}/\Pi\xrightarrow{\sim}X
. Choose a preimage
\widetilde{x}_{j}
, and let
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{k}\subset\widetilde{X}
be the connected component of
\widetilde{x}_{j}
in
p^{-1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
. The formal slice to
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{k}
at
\widetilde{x}_{j}
is isomorphic to the completion at
0
of a Kleinian singularity
\Sigma_{k}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}
(with, possibly,
\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}=1
). Note that
\Pi
acts on the fiber product
\bigsqcup_{j}\widetilde{X}^{\wedge\widetilde{x}_{j}}
. This action is transitive on the set
\{\widetilde{x}_{j}\}
. Consider the induced homomorphism

\Gamma_{k}\overset{\phi_{k}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Perm}\{\widetilde{x}_{j}\},

where
\mathrm{Perm}\{\widetilde{x}_{j}\}
denotes the permutation group. By construction,
\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}
is the kernel of this map. In particular,
\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}
is normal in
\Gamma_{k}
, and independent of the preimage
\widetilde{x}_{j}
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
denote the algebraic fundamental group of
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
(since the cover
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X^{reg}
is Galois,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is the kernel of the homomorphism
\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pi
). Similarly to (
4.6.1
), there are group homomorphisms
\phi_{k}^{\prime}:\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Gamma^{\prime}
. The diagram of schemes (
4.6.2
) gives rise to a diagram of groups

(4.6.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{k}}

4.7.
Deformations and quantizations of symplectic singularities

Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity and choose a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
as in Section
4.4
. Recall the Namikawa space
\mathfrak{P}=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})
. Below in this section we will see that this vector space parameterizes both Poisson deformations and filtered quantizations of
Y
.

The following proposition was obtained in
[
Los16
]
but is a direct consequence of results of Namikawa (
[
Nam08
]
).

Proposition 4.7.1

(
[
Los16
]
, Proposition 2.6)
.

There is a graded Poisson scheme
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}
over
\mathfrak{P}
such that

(i)

The fiber of
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}
over
0
is
Y
.

(ii)

Suppose
B
is a conical affine scheme,
Z
is a graded Poisson scheme over
B
, and (i) holds for the morphism
Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}B
. Then there is a unique
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant morphism
B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{P}
with the following property: there is an isomorphism of graded Poisson schemes
Z\simeq Y_{\mathrm{univ}}\times_{\mathfrak{P}}B
over
B
which is the identity over
0\in B
.

The scheme
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}
in Proposition
4.7.1
is called the
universal graded Poisson deformation
of
Y
.

Remark 4.7.2
.

Below, we will need a geometric interpretation of the unique morphism
B\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
. We provide one in the special case when
B
is a vector space with
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action given by
t\mapsto t^{-d}
, as on
\mathfrak{P}
so that the morphism
B\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}

is linear. Let
B^{\wedge},\mathfrak{P}^{\wedge}
denote the formal neighborhoods of
0
in the corresponding vector spaces. Let
Z^{\wedge},Y_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\wedge}
denote the formal neighborhoods of
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
in
Z,Y_{\mathrm{univ}}
. These are formal deformations of
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
over
B^{\wedge},\mathfrak{P}^{\wedge}
. The de Rham cohomology group
H^{2}_{dR}(Z^{\wedge}/B^{\wedge})
decomposes as a tensor product
H^{2}_{dR}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\otimes\mathbb{C}[B^{\wedge}]
thanks to the Gauss-Manin connection. In particular, the class of the relative symplectic form on
Z^{\wedge}/B^{\wedge}
can be viewed as
a map
B^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\wedge}
.
Because of the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-actions, this map comes from a linear map
B\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
. It was shown in
[
KV02
, Theorem 3.6]
that the map
B^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\wedge}
is the unique such map inducing an isomorphism
Z^{\wedge}\xrightarrow{\sim}B^{\wedge}\times_{\mathfrak{P}^{\wedge}}Y_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\wedge}
of formal Poisson deformations of
Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
. A connection between the Namikawa and Kaledin-Verbitsky approaches is explained in
[
Los16
, Remark 2.7]
. In particular, the map
B\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
described in this remark is the same as the one in Proposition
4.7.1
.

Next, we state an analog of Proposition
4.7.1
for filtered quantizations. For any graded smooth symplectic variety
V
, there is a (non-commutative)
period map

\mathrm{Per}:\mathrm{Quant}(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(V,\mathbb{C}),

see
[
BK04
, Section 4]
,
[
Los12
, Section 2.3]
.

Proposition 4.7.3

(
[
Los16
]
, Proposition 3.1(1))
.

The maps

\mathrm{Quant}(Y)\overset{|_{Y^{\mathrm{reg}}}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\mathrm{Quant}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\overset{\mathrm{Per}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}

are bijections.

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
, write
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}
for the corresponding filtered quantization of
Y
. There is also
a universal version of
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}
.

Proposition 4.7.4

(
[
Los16
]
, Cor 3.2(2))
.

There is a sheaf
\mathcal{D}^{Y_{\mathrm{univ}}}
of associative
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P}]
-algebras on
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}
such that, for each
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
, the filtered quantization
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}
of
Y
is obtained as

\mathcal{D}^{Y_{\mathrm{univ}}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P}]}\mathbb{C}
, where the homomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
corresponds to
\lambda
.

Next, we explain the relation between Poisson deformations (resp. quantizations) of
Y
and Poisson deformations (resp. quantizations) of
X
. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
, set
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}:=\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})
. Let
Y_{\lambda}
denote the fiber of
Y^{\mathrm{univ}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
over
\lambda
and let
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{C}[Y_{\lambda}]
.

Theorem 4.7.5
.

The following are true:

(i)

For every
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
, the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{0}
is a filtered Poisson deformation of
\mathbb{C}[X]
.

(ii)

Every filtered Poisson deformation of
\mathbb{C}[X]
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
.

(iii)

For every
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}
, we have
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\lambda}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
if and only if
\lambda^{\prime}\in W\cdot\lambda
.

Hence, the map
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\lambda}
induces a bijection

\mathfrak{P}/W\simeq\mathrm{PDef}(X),\qquad W\cdot\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\lambda}.

Proof.

These results are essentially due to Namikawa,
[
Nam20a
]
. (i) is a consequence of

[
Los16
, Prop 2.9(1)]
. (ii) and (iii) follow from
[
Los16
, Prop 2.12, Cor 2.13]
.
∎

Theorem 4.7.6

(
[
Los16
]
, Prop 3.3, Thm 3.4)
.

Direct analogs of (i)-(iii) of Theorem
4.7.5
hold for filtered quantizations. In particular, there is a bijection

\mathfrak{P}/W\simeq\mathrm{Quant}(X),\qquad W\cdot\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}.

Next we discuss the universal graded deformation of
X
. The algebra
\mathbb{C}[Y_{\mathrm{univ}}]
is a graded deformation of
\mathbb{C}[Y]=\mathbb{C}[X]
over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P}]
. The algebra
\mathbb{C}[Y_{\mathrm{univ}}]
carries a
W
-action which is the identity on
\mathbb{C}[Y]
and makes the homomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[Y_{\mathrm{univ}}]
equivariant.
These two claims constitute

[
Los16
, Prop 2.9]
. Similarly to the case of the
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalizations, all Poisson deformations
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\lambda}
can be recovered as the fibers of a certain Poisson scheme over
\mathfrak{P}/W
.

Proposition 4.7.7

(
[
Los16
]
, Prop 2.12, Cor 2.13)
.

There is a graded affine Poisson scheme
X_{\mathrm{univ}}
over
\mathfrak{P}/W
such that:

(i)

The fiber of
X_{\mathrm{univ}}
over
0
is
X
.

(ii)

Suppose
B
is a conical affine scheme,
Z
is a graded Poisson scheme over
B
, and (i) holds for the morphism
Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}B
. Then there is a uniquely defined
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant morphism
B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{P}/W
with the following property: there is an isomorphism of graded Poisson schemes
Z\xrightarrow{\sim}X_{\mathrm{univ}}\times_{\mathfrak{P}/W}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}Z
over
B
which is the identity over
0\in B
.

(iii)

We have
\mathbb{C}[X_{\mathrm{univ}}]=\mathbb{C}[Y_{\mathrm{univ}}]^{W}
.

The scheme
X_{\mathrm{univ}}
in Proposition
4.7.7
is called the
universal Poisson deformation
of
X
. Note that (iii) yields a projective morphism
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}\rightarrow X_{\mathrm{univ}}
.

Let
X_{\lambda}
denote the fiber of
W\lambda
in
X_{\mathrm{univ}}
. The morphism
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}\rightarrow X_{\mathrm{univ}}
restricts to
Y_{\lambda}\rightarrow X_{\lambda}
.
It follows from the proof of
[
Los16
, Proposition 2.9]
that

Y_{\mathrm{univ}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}\times_{\mathfrak{P}/W}X_{\mathrm{univ}}\rightarrow X_{\mathrm{univ}}

is the Stein factorization. In particular,
Y_{\lambda}\rightarrow X_{\lambda}
is a projective morphism with connected fibers between normal varieties of the same dimension. Hence it is birational and
\mathbb{C}[X_{\lambda}]\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}[Y_{\lambda}]
.

In the present paper, filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities will play the leading role—Poisson deformations enter only as a tool for understanding quantizations. Below, we
highlight an important example of Theorem
4.7.6
.

Example 4.7.8
.

Let
G
be a complex simple algebraic group and let
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its nilpotent cone. Choose a Borel subgroup
B\subset G
and a maximal torus
H\subset B
. As noted in Example
4.4.3
,
X:=\mathcal{N}
is a conical symplectic singularity. For
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
, we take the Springer resolution
\rho:T^{*}(G/B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
. There is a natural identification

\mathfrak{P}:=H^{2}(T^{*}(G/B),\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{*}.

The regular locus of
X
is the principal nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\subset X
and there is a single codimension 2 leaf, namely the subregular orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset X
. If
\mathfrak{g}
is simply laced, the singularity of this leaf is of the same type as
\mathfrak{g}
. Otherwise, this singularity corresponds to the
unfolding
of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
(e.g. if
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
G_{2}
, then the singularity is of type
D_{4}
). In the latter case,
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
acts by the group of automorphisms of the simply laced Dynkin diagram which folds into
\mathfrak{g}
. In either case,
W
is identified with the (Lie-theoretic) Weyl group for
\mathfrak{g}
. A convenient reference for these facts is
[
Slo80
, Sec 8.3-8.4]
.

In this example, the objects
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}
,
X_{\mathrm{univ}}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
can be easily described. The universal graded Poisson deformation
Y_{\mathrm{univ}}
is the homogeneous vector bundle
G\times^{B}\mathfrak{n}^{\perp}
with its natural projection to
\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, where
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathfrak{b}
denotes the nilpotent radical. The variety
X_{\mathrm{univ}}
is
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and the map

X_{\mathrm{univ}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}/W
is the quotient morphism.

Finally, the quantization
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
of
\mathbb{C}[X]
with quantization parameter
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is the corresponding
central reduction
of
U(\mathfrak{g})
. More precisely, the
W
-orbit of
\lambda
defines a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda}\subset\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})
by means of the Harish-Chandra isomorphism, and
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\simeq U(\mathfrak{g})/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda}U(\mathfrak{g})
.

Fix a quantization parameter

\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{t})\in\bigoplus_{k=0}^{t}\mathfrak{P}_{k}\simeq\mathfrak{P}.

We will explain how to (partially) recover
\lambda
from the quantization
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
.

For a codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
, consider the filtered quantization
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma_{k}}_{\lambda_{k}}
of
\mathbb{C}[\Sigma_{k}]
corresponding to the quantization parameter
\lambda_{k}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
. Then
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma_{k}}_{\lambda_{k}}
are related as follows. Choose a point
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{k}
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\hbar}^{\wedge}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda_{k},\hbar}^{\wedge}
) denote the formal completion of the Rees algebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda_{k}}^{\Sigma_{k}}
) at the ideal defined by
x
(resp.
0
), and let
\mathbb{A}_{\hbar}(V)
denote the homogeneous Weyl algebra associated to the symplectic vector space
V:=T_{x}\mathfrak{L}_{k}
. Then there is a
\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
-algebra isomorphism

(4.7.1)

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\hbar}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathbb{A}_{\hbar}(V)^{\wedge}\ \widehat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]}\ \mathcal{A}_{\lambda_{k},\hbar}^{\wedge},

see, e.g.,
[
Los16
, (3.2)]
. Here, and elsewhere, we write
\widehat{\otimes}
for the completed tensor product. Modulo
\hbar
, (
4.7.1
) gives rise to an isomorphism

(4.7.2)

\mathbb{C}[X]^{\wedge}\simeq\mathbb{C}[V]^{\wedge}\ \widehat{\otimes}\ \mathbb{C}[\Sigma_{k}]^{\wedge}.

If (
4.7.2
) is fixed,
W_{k}\lambda_{k}
is uniquely determined by (
4.7.1
), see
[
Los16
, Proposition 3.6]
.

4.8.
Quantizations of Kleinian singularities

We will now elaborate on the classification of filtered quantizations in the case of Kleinian singularities.
On the one hand, this is an interesting special case, where the quantizations can be constructed algebraically. On the other hand, thanks to (
4.7.1
), understanding the case of Kleinian singularities is essential for understanding the general case.

Let
\Sigma=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
be a Kleinian singularity, and fix the notation of Section
4.2
, e.g.
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{h}
, and so on. By Remark
4.2.4
, there is a natural identification
\mathfrak{P}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
W
is identified with the Weyl group for
\mathfrak{g}
. Thus by Theorem
4.7.6
, there is a bijection

\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Quant}(\Sigma),\qquad W\cdot\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}.

For Kleinian singularities, there is an explicit algebraic construction of filtered quantizations due to Crawley-Boevey and Holland (
[
CH98
]
). For each element
c\in\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]^{\Gamma}
of the form

c=1+\sum_{\gamma\neq 1}c_{\gamma}\gamma

there is an associated filtered quantization
eH_{c}e
of
\mathbb{C}[\Sigma]
, constructed as follows. First, form the algebra

H_{c}:=\mathbb{C}\langle x,y\rangle\#\Gamma/(xy-yx-c).

The grading on
\mathbb{C}\langle x,y\rangle\#\Gamma
induces a filtration on
H_{c}
. Let
e\in\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
