. There are two irreducible Harish-Chandra bimodules annihilated by
I_{0}
—they are the irreducible constituents of the metaplectic representation. Both are unitary. These bimodules are among the most familiar examples of unipotent bimodules which are not special unipotent.

(v)

Let
G=\mathrm{SL}(2n)
and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be the universal cover of the so-called ‘model’ nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}_{(2^{n})}
. We will see below (see Proposition
8.2.8
) that
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{\rho}{2})
. This ideal plays a central role in the determination of the unitary dual of the universal cover of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
(see
[
Hua90
]
).

Example 6.2.2
.

Let
G=\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
and let
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}_{(2^{n})}
be the ‘model’ nilpotent orbit. In
[
McG94
]
, McGovern attaches to
\mathbb{O}
several so-called
q-unipotent
ideals (all are
weakly unipotent
in the sense of
[
Vog84
]
). One of these ideals is the maximal ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{\rho}{2})
(it is completely prime, like all of McGovern’s ideals). We note that if
n\geq 2
, this ideal is
not
unipotent in the sense of Definition
6.0.1
. This is evident from the infinitesimal character calculations in Section
8.2
(see Proposition
8.2.8
). Unitarity considerations justify its exclusion. For example, an
atlas
computation shows that the spherical Harish-Chandra bimodule
U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{\rho}{2})
is non-unitary for
2\leq n\leq 7
(for
n>7
, the unitarity calculation cannot be completed in a reasonable amount of time).

6.3.
Motivation

In this section, we will provide some evidence in favor of Definition
6.0.1
.

6.3.1.
Unitarity

The most basic requirement of unipotent bimodules is that they are unitary (we will recall what this means in Section
10.1
). If
\mathcal{B}
is an irreducible unitary Harish-Chandra bimodule, then by a classical result of Vogan (see
[
Vog86
, Prop 7.12]
), the left and right annihilators of
\mathcal{B}
are completely prime ideals. Thus, a minimal requirement of all unipotent ideals is that they are completely prime.

The set of completely prime primitive ideals in
U(\mathfrak{g})
includes all primitive ideals of
\mathcal{W}
-dimension 1,
see Corollary
3.5.6
. Conversely, every completely prime primitive ideal with integral infinitesimal character is of
\mathcal{W}
-dimension 1 (with the possible exception of primitive ideals attached to a single orbit in type
E_{8}
), see
[
Los10a
]
,
[
Los15
]
. At non-integral infinitesimal character, there are completely prime primitive ideals with
\operatorname{\mathcal{W}-\dim}(I)>1
. For example, if
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
and
n\geq 2
, the maximal ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\rho/2)
is completely prime, whereas

\operatorname{\mathcal{W}-\dim}(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\rho/2))=2^{\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor},

see
[
Pre10
]
,
[
LP21
, Proposition 4.1]
. However, as noted in Example
6.2.2
, the Harish-Chandra bimodules
U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\mathrm{max}}(\rho/2)
are non-unitary in all cases we can check. These examples suggest the following conjecture.

Conjecture 6.3.1
.

The left and right annihilators of
unitary Harish-Chandra bimodules have
\mathcal{W}
-dimension
1
.

In view of this conjecture, and the examples supporting it, we restrict our attention to primitive ideals of
\mathcal{W}
-dimension 1. This set includes all ideals of the form
I_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
, see Proposition
6.1.2
. In classical types, all primitive ideals of
\mathcal{W}
-dimension 1 are of this form, see
[
Top21
, Thm 1.2]
. In exceptional types, there are exceptions (a few will be encountered below). Nonetheless, ideals of the form
I_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
constitute a particularly nice class of primitive ideals of
\mathcal{W}
-dimension
1
.

6.3.2.
Orbit method

The considerations above suggest that all unipotent ideals are of the form
I_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
.
The orbit method philosophy suggests that one should further restrict to
\lambda=0
.

Definition 6.3.2

(
[
Vog90
]
, Def 2.1)
.

A
Dixmier algebra
for
G
is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
consisting of an associative algebra
\mathcal{A}
and an algebra homomorphism
\varphi:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
, regarded as a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule via
\varphi
, is Harish-Chandra (cf. Definition
3.4.1
). An isomorphism of Dixmier algebras
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\varphi_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{2},\varphi_{2})
is an isomorphism of algebras
\phi:\mathcal{A}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{2}
which intertwines
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
.

One version of the orbit method, articulated in
[
Vog90
]
, is the following. For each
G
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}
of a coadjoint orbit
\mathbb{O}^{1}
(not necessarily nilpotent), there should be an associated Dixmier algebra, denoted
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1})
. As a representation of
G
,
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1})
should coincide with the ring of regular functions
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}]
. Finally, the passage from
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}
to
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1})
should define an injective correspondence

\mathrm{Dix}:\{G\text{-eqvt covers of coadjoint orbits}\}\hookrightarrow\{\text{Dixmier algebras for }G\}.

This existence of this injection, with the property described above, is known as the
Dixmier conjecture
, see
[
Vog90
, Conj 2.3]
. In the terminology of
[
Vog90
]
, a
unipotent Dixmier algebra
is one which corresponds to a
nilpotent
cover under this (not-yet-defined) correspondence. A
unipotent ideal
is the kernel in
U(\mathfrak{g})
of the co-moment map for a unipotent Dixmier algebra.

In Section
7.8
, we will give a definition of
\mathrm{Dix}
, extending a result of the first-named author, see
[
Los16
, Thm 5.3]
. Our map has the following property: If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a nilpotent cover, then
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is the canonical quantization
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
(regarded as a Dixmier algebra via the co-moment map
\Phi^{\widetilde{X}}_{0}:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
). Thus, Definition
6.0.1
is consistent with the orbit method philosophy.

6.3.3.
Maximality

It is generally believed that unipotent ideals should be maximal (see Desideratum (3) from Section
1.3
and the references therein). In Appendix
B
, we will show that for linear classical groups, the ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is always maximal. This result is extended to arbitrary groups in the paper
[
MM23
]
.

Now we show that the maximality of
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is equivalent to the simplicity of
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
. The latter is a general expectation of canonical quantizations of conical symplectic singularities, see Section
5.5
.

Proposition 6.3.3
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
is simple.

(2)

I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

To simplify notation, let
I:=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
and
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
. We first prove (1)
\Rightarrow
(2). Suppose
I
is not maximal. Let
\mathcal{W}
be the
W
-algebra associated to
\mathbb{O}
and let
R
be the reductive part of the centralizer of
e\in\mathbb{O}
. Consider the functors
\bullet_{\dagger}:\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W})
and
\bullet^{\dagger}:\HC^{R}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{G}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
constructed in Section
3.5
. Note that
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
is a completely reducible

R
-equivariant
\mathcal{W}
-bimodule (in fact, it is the direct sum of
1
-dimensional
\mathcal{W}
-bimodules, Lemma

6.1.1
). Since
U(\mathfrak{g})/I\subset\mathcal{A}
is an inclusion of Harish-Chandra bimodules, and
\bullet_{\dagger}
is exact (Proposition
3.5.1
),
(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)_{\dagger}
is an
R
-stable subbimodule of
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
.

Consider the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=[(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)_{\dagger}]^{\dagger}
. Recall that

\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
, see Lemma
6.1.1
(iii). Thus
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
embeds into

\mathcal{A}
as a subalgebra and as a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule direct summand. Moreover, the injective map
U(\mathfrak{g})/I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
factors through the natural map
U(\mathfrak{g})/I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}([U(\mathfrak{g})/I]_{\dagger})^{\dagger}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. It follows that the latter map is injective, and thus by
3.5.1
(iv) the bimodule
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is an extension of
U(\mathfrak{g})/I
by a bimodule supported on
\partial\mathbb{O}
. Note that for every ideal
\widetilde{I}\subset U(\mathfrak{g})

properly containing
I
we have
\mathcal{V}(U(\mathfrak{g})/\widetilde{I})\subsetneq\mathcal{V}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
, since
I
is a prime ideal, see (iii) of Proposition
3.1.1
.
Since
U(\mathfrak{g})/I
has finite length, see Lemma
3.4.2
, there is a minimal ideal
\widetilde{I}\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
properly containing
I
.

We claim that
\widetilde{I}/I
is a 2-sided ideal in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Let us show that it is a right ideal (the proof that it is a left ideal is analogous). The associated variety
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}/[\widetilde{I}/I])
of the
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}/(\widetilde{I}/I)
is a proper subvariety of
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
. Hence
\mathcal{V}(\widetilde{I}/I)=\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.
If
\mathcal{B}:=(\widetilde{I}/I)\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}/[\widetilde{I}/I])
is nonzero, then
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{B})\subsetneq\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. By the tensor-Hom adjunction, the simple
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule
\widetilde{I}/I
embeds into
\Hom_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}/[\widetilde{I}/I],\mathcal{B})
, in fact, into the locally finite part, and the associated variety of the latter is contained in the associated variety of
\mathcal{B}
. This contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{V}(\widetilde{I}/I)=\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. It follows that
\mathcal{B}=\{0\}
. The right multiplication in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
gives a map
\widetilde{I}/I\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{g})}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, since
\mathcal{B}=\{0\}
, we get

[\widetilde{I}/I]\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{I}/I
. Thus
\widetilde{I}/I
is a nonzero right (and hence two-sided) ideal in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

Since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a direct bimodule summand of
\mathcal{A}
, we have that
\mathcal{A}/[\mathcal{A}(\widetilde{I}/I)]\neq\{0\}
. Let
{I}^{\prime}
be the left annihilator of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\widetilde{I}/I)
. Note that

\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\widetilde{I}/I))\subset\mathcal{V}(U(\mathfrak{g})/\widetilde{I})\subsetneq\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A})
. So
I^{\prime}
is a two-sided ideal in
\mathcal{A}
, different from
\mathcal{A}
. Since
\mathcal{A}/I^{\prime}
is a
HC bimodule over
U(\mathfrak{g})/\widetilde{I}
we see that
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}/I^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{V}(U(\mathfrak{g})/\widetilde{I})
.
On the other hand,
\mathcal{V}(U(\mathfrak{g})/\widetilde{I})\subsetneq\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A})
. It follows that
I^{\prime}\neq\{0\}
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is simple, we get a contradiction. This proves (1)
\Rightarrow
(2).

Next we prove (2)
\Rightarrow
(1). Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is not simple. Then there is a nonzero prime ideal
\widetilde{I}\subset\mathcal{A}
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is a Noetherian domain, the GK dimension of the quotient
\mathcal{A}/\widetilde{I}
is less than that of
\mathcal{A}
, and hence the GK dimension of the image of
U(\mathfrak{g})/I
in
\mathcal{A}/\widetilde{I}

is less than that of
U(\mathfrak{g})/I
. Since
I
is maximal,
U(\mathfrak{g})/I

has no such algebra quotient, a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

6.3.4.
Applicability to real reductive groups

In this subsection, we will discuss the applicability of Definition
6.0.2
to the more general setting of real reductive Lie groups. This issue is quite subtle and deserving of careful consideration. It is further explored in
[
MM23
]
and
[
DM
]
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}
be a real reductive Lie group. For this informal discussion, the precise meaning of ‘real reductive Lie group’ will not matter enormously. It should be broad enough to include non-linear covering groups, such as
\mathrm{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, but narrow enough so that the theory of Harish-Chandra modules is applicable. In particular,
G_{\mathbb{R}}
should have finitely many connected components and the identity component should be a finite cover of an algebraic group. Let
\mathfrak{g}
denote the complexification of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
and
K
the complexification of a maximal compact subgroup
K_{\mathbb{R}}\subset G_{\mathbb{R}}
. A
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-
module
is a finitely-generated
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module with with a compatible rational
K
-action. A
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module is
admissible
if each irreducible
K
-representation appears with finite multiplicity. Under suitable conditions on
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, there is a bijection between the set of irreducible admissible
G_{\mathbb{R}}
-representations and the set of irreducible admissible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-modules, see
[
Vog81
, Chp 0.3]
for an overview. We note that if
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is complex, then a
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module is the same thing as a
G_{\mathbb{R}}
-equivariant Harish-Chandra bimodule for the (complex) Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
. As explained in Section
1
, the set
\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G_{\mathbb{R}})
of irreducible unitary
G_{\mathbb{R}}
-representations should contain a finite set of ‘building blocks’
\mathrm{Unip}(G_{\mathbb{R}})\subset\mathrm{Irr}_{u}(G_{\mathbb{R}})
called ‘unipotent representations’. If
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is complex, then
\mathrm{Unip}(G_{\mathbb{R}})
should be the set defined in Definition
6.0.2
. Our problem is to formulate a natural generalization of Definition
6.0.2
for arbitrary
G_{\mathbb{R}}
.

If
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is algebraic, there is a notion of a
special unipotent representation
, due to Adams, Barbasch, and Vogan. The definition in
[
ABV92
, Sec 27]
is stated in the language of
L
-groups and Arthur parameters, but is equivalent to the following (see
[
ABV92
, Cor 27.13]
).

Definition 6.3.4

(
[
ABV92
]
)
.

Suppose
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is an algebraic group and let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
be a nilpotent orbit for the Langlands dual Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. A
special unipotent representation
attached to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
\mathcal{B}
such that
\Ann(\mathcal{B})=I_{\mathrm{max}}(h^{\vee}/2)
.

The following conjecture is essentially due to Arthur.

Conjecture 6.3.5

(
[
Art89
]
)
.

Suppose
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is an algebraic group. Then all special unipotent representations of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
are unitary.

We summarize the cases in which Conjecture
6.3.5
is known

•

G_{\mathbb{R}}
is a split real form of a linear classical group (
[
Art13
]
,
[
BMSZ20
]
).

•

G_{\mathbb{R}}
is a complex classical group, regarded as a real group by restriction of scalars (
[
Bar89
]
).

•

G_{\mathbb{R}}
is a real form of a simple exceptional group (
[
AMLV
]
).

Notably, if we allow
G_{\mathbb{R}}
to be a non-linear group, Conjecture
6.3.5
is false.

Example 6.3.6
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathrm{Mp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. Hence,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(4)
and
K
is the ‘square-root of determinant’ cover of
GL(2)

K=\left\{(g,z)\in GL(2)\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\mid\det(g)=z^{2}\right\}

Let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
be the subregular nilpotent orbit for
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{so}(5)
. Then in standard coordinates on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, we have
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}=(1,0)
. There are two degenerate principal series representations of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
which are annihilated by the ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})
. They are irreducible by
[
KR90
, Thm 1]
. In the same (standard) coordinates, the
K
-types are as follows:

2\mathbb{Z}\times 2\mathbb{Z}+(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})\qquad\text{or}\qquad 2\mathbb{Z}\times 2\mathbb{Z}-(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}).

By an easy application of the Dirac inequality, we see that neither representation is unitary. We are grateful to Jing-Song Huang for suggesting this approach.

We will show in Section
9
that the set of unipotent ideals includes (as a proper subset) all special unipotent ideals. This suggests the following generalization of Definition
6.3.4
.

‘Definition’ 6.3.7
.

Suppose
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is an algebraic group and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a finite cover of a nilpotent co-adjoint
\Ad(\mathfrak{g})
-orbit. A
unipotent representation
of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
attached to
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathrm{Ann}(\mathcal{B})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
.

We use quotation marks to indicate that this is
not
a reasonable definition—if we take the ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
to be non-special, there can be non-unitary
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-modules which are annihilated by
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
even if
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is algebraic. This problem is already apparent for
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.

Example 6.3.8
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Hence,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(2)
and
K=SO(2)
. The unipotent ideal attached to the (universal) 2-fold cover of the principal nilpotent
\Ad(\mathfrak{g})
-orbit is the maximal ideal in
U(\mathfrak{g})
of infinitesimal character
\frac{\rho}{2}
, see (v) of Example
6.2.1
. There are two irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-modules annihilated by this ideal: one spherical and one non-spherical. The spherical representation is unitary (it is the midpoint of a unitary complementary series). The non-spherical representation, however, is
not
unitary. In particular, it should not be regarded as a unipotent representation of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
.

We believe that ‘Definition’
6.3.7
can be fixed in every case by replacing
G_{\mathbb{R}}
with an appropriate non-linear covering group. This covering group should depend in a predictable way on the infinitesimal character of
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. In Example
6.3.8
, the infinitesimal character of
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is half-integral. This suggests that we should look for genuine representations of the (non-linear) 2-fold covering group
\mathrm{Mp}(2,\mathbb{R})
. There are two genuine irreducible representations of
\mathrm{Mp}(2,\mathbb{R})
annihilated by the ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
, namely the irreducible constituents of the oscillator representation. Both are unitary, and (we believe) worthy of the label ‘unipotent’.

Remark 6.3.9
.

Further evidence for this idea can be found in
[
BMSZ20
]
. There, the authors define a class of maximal ideals for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
called ‘metaplectic special unipotent’. One can check that all of their ideals are unipotent in our sense, compare to Section
9
. They conjecture, but do not prove, that all genuine irreducible representations of
\mathrm{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
annihilated by a metaplectic special unipotent ideal are unitary.

However, if we assume that
\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid, these subtleties seem to vanish. In a forthcoming paper
[
DM
]
, the second-named author and Dougal Davis will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 6.3.10
.

Suppose
\mathbb{O}
is a birationally rigid nilpotent
\Ad(\mathfrak{g})
-orbit. Then every irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
\mathcal{B}
with
\Ann(\mathcal{B})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is unitary.

Example 6.3.11
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}
be the split real form of the (unique) simple group of type
E_{8}
and consider the nilpotent orbits

\mathbb{O}_{1}=2A_{2}+2A_{1},\qquad\mathbb{O}_{2}=A_{4}+A_{3}.

Both orbits are rigid, see
[
DE09
]
. We will explain in Section
8
how the ideals
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
can be computed—in these cases we have

I_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=I_{\mathrm{max}}(\rho/3),\qquad I_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{2})=I_{\mathrm{max}}(\rho/5).

An
atlas
computation shows that in each case, there is a unique irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module annihilated by
I_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{i})
, and it is unitary.

Example 6.3.12
.

Let
G_{\mathbb{R}}
be the non-linear group
\mathrm{Mp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
(for
n\geq 2
) and let
\mathbb{O}
be the minimal orbit. As noted in Example
6.2.1
(iv),
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is the Joseph ideal. There are two irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-modules annihilated by this ideal—they are the irreducible constituents of the oscillator representation. It is a classical fact that both are unitary.

Theorem
6.3.10
suggests that if
\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid, ‘Definition’
6.3.7
is correct. We propose the following.

Definition 6.3.13
.

Suppose
\mathbb{O}
is a birationally rigid nilpotent
\Ad(\mathfrak{g})
-orbit. A
unipotent representation
of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
attached to
\mathbb{O}
is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathrm{Ann}(\mathcal{B})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
. Write
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}}(G_{\mathbb{R}})
for the set of (equivalences classes of) such representations.

As further vindication of Definition
6.3.13
, we offer the following remark. It is shown in
[
LY23
]
that the set
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}}(G_{\mathbb{R}})
admits a geometric parameterization (analogous to our parameterization of unipotent bimodules, see Theorem
6.6.2
below). More precisely, they prove that for
G_{\mathbb{R}}
algebraic,
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}}(G_{\mathbb{R}})
is in bijection with pairs
(\mathbb{O}_{K},\mathcal{L})
, where
\mathbb{O}_{K}
is a
K
-orbit in
\mathbb{O}\cap(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
and
\mathcal{L}
is an irreducible twisted local system on
\mathbb{O}_{K}

with half-canonical twist.

6.4.
Equivariant bimodules for Hamiltonian quantizations of nilpotent covers

In this section we will establish some preliminary facts about Harish-Chandra bimodules for quantizations of nilpotent covers, specializing (and modifying) the general results and constructions of Sections
4.13
,
4.14
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover and let
(\mathcal{A},\Phi):=(\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{X}}_{\lambda},\Phi^{\widetilde{X}}_{\lambda})
be a Hamiltonian quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. On any Harish-Chandra bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\HC(\mathcal{A})
, there is an adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}
, defined by

\ad:\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\End(\mathcal{B}),\qquad\ad(\xi)(b)=\Phi(\xi)b-b\Phi(\xi),\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{g},b\in\mathcal{B}.

Definition 6.4.1
.

A Harish-Chandra bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\HC(\mathcal{A})
is
G
-
equivariant
if
\ad
integrates to a rational action
\Ad
of
G
. Denote the full subcategory of
G
-equivariant Harish-Chandra bimodules by
\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})\subset\HC(\mathcal{A})
and write
\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})
for the quotient category
\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})/\HC^{G}_{\partial}(\mathcal{A})
.

Let
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. By (
4.13.2
), there is an equivalence of categories

\bullet_{\dagger}:\overline{\HC}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma/\Gamma(\lambda)\modd

In this section, we will provide an analogous description of the category
\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})
. Let
\Omega=\pi_{1}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
and let
\Omega(\lambda)
be the image of
\Gamma(\lambda)
under the quotient map
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Omega
.

Proposition 6.4.2
.

The functor
\bullet_{\dagger}:\overline{\HC}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma/\Gamma(\lambda)\modd
restricts to an equivalence

\bullet_{\dagger}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Omega/\Omega(\lambda)\modd

Proof.

First, note that the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})
, and hence of
Z(G)^{\circ}
, on any bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})
is trivial. Replacing
G
with
G/Z(G)^{\circ}
if necessary, we can assume that
G
is semisimple. Let
G^{\mathrm{sc}}
denote the universal cover of
G
, and
K
the kernel of the quotient map
G^{\mathrm{sc}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G
. An object in
\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda})
is a bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\overline{\HC}(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda})
on which the action of
K
is trivial. Since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G
-equivariant cover, we see that
K\subset G^{\mathrm{sc}}_{x}
for all
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. So
K
maps to
\Gamma
with cokernel equal to
\Omega
.

We claim that the
K
-action on
\mathcal{B}\in\HC(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda})
is trivial if and only if the
K
-action on
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}
is trivial.
Clearly,
K
acts trivially on
\mathcal{B}
if and only if it acts trivially on
\gr\mathcal{B}
. By (
4.13.1
), we have
(\gr\mathcal{B})|_{X^{\mathrm{reg}}}\simeq(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}})^{\Gamma}
. Since
\Gamma
acts freely on
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}}
,
K
acts trivially on
(\gr\mathcal{B})|_{X^{\mathrm{reg}}}
if and only if it acts trivially on
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}
. This completes the proof.
∎

The next lemma will be useful for computing
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}
when
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})
. Since the image of
\mathfrak{g}
in
\mathcal{A}
lives in degree
\leqslant d
, every good filtration on
\mathcal{B}
is stable under
\ad(\mathfrak{g})
, and hence
\Ad(G)
. In particular,
\gr(\mathcal{B})
is a
G
-equivariant
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}]
-module and the
G
-action is uniquely recovered from the Poisson structure.

Lemma 6.4.3
.

For any
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})
(and any good filtration), there is an isomorphism of
G
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}

(6.4.1)

(\gr\mathcal{B})|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}\simeq(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{\mathbb{O}}})^{\Omega}.

This isomorphism uniquely determines the
\Omega
-module
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}
.

Proof.

We can assume in the proof that
G
is semisimple and simply connected and hence that
\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})=\HC(\mathcal{A})
. Recall that for a smooth symplectic variety, a Poisson coherent sheaf is the same thing as a local system, see Step 3 in the proof of
[
Los17
, Lemma 3.9]
.

By (
4.13.1
) (and using the notation therein) there is a local system isomorphism

(6.4.2)

(\gr\mathcal{B})|_{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}}\simeq(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}^{\prime}})^{\Gamma}.

On both sides of (
6.4.2
), the
\mathfrak{g}
-action is recovered from the local system structure. It follows that the isomorphism (
6.4.2
) is
\mathfrak{g}
- and hence
G
-equivariant.

Since
\operatorname{codim}(\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}-\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{reg}})\geqslant 2
, (
6.4.2
) is equivalent to a
G
-equivariant local system isomorphism

(6.4.3)

(\gr\mathcal{B})|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}\simeq(\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{\mathbb{O}}})^{\Gamma}.

This implies (
6.4.1
).
It remains to show that (
6.4.1
) determines
\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}
uniquely. For this, it suffices to show that the forgetful functor from the category of
G
-equivariant local systems on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
to the category of
G
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a full embedding. Choose
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. Via restriction to
x
, these categories are equivalent to
G_{x}/G_{x}^{\circ}\operatorname{-mod}
and
G_{x}\operatorname{-mod}
, respectively. The forgetful functor is the pullback under
G_{x}\twoheadrightarrow G_{x}/G_{x}^{\circ}
, which is indeed a full embedding. This completes the proof.
∎

We will also need a
G
-equivariant version of Corollary
4.14.4
. Let
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant Galois cover and let
\Pi:=\Aut_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})
. Note that the
\Pi
-action on
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
is by
G
-equivariant Hamiltonian automorphisms. Suppose
\mathcal{A}^{\widecheck{X}}
is a
\Pi
-equivariant Hamiltonian quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
such that
\mathcal{A}\simeq(\mathcal{A}^{\widecheck{X}})^{\Pi}
as Hamiltonian quantizations. In view of Proposition
6.4.2
, the following result is completely analogous to Corollary
4.14.4
, and is proved in a similar fashion.

Corollary 6.4.4
.

Suppose
\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}^{\widecheck{X}})\simeq\Vect
. Then
\bullet_{\dagger}
induces an equivalence of monoidal categories

\bullet_{\dagger}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pi\modd.

The inverse equivalence,
\bullet^{\dagger}
, is the functor of
isotypic components

V^{\dagger}\simeq(\mathcal{A}^{\widecheck{X}}\otimes V)^{\Pi},\qquad V\in\Pi\modd.

Our next task is to relate the restriction and extension functors of Section

3.5
to those of
Section
4.13
.
Let
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{X}}_{\lambda},I:=I_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
and
\underline{J}:=I_{\dagger}\subset\mathcal{W}
. Consider the full subcategory of
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))

(6.4.4)

\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I):=\{\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\mid I\subseteq\mathrm{LAnn}(\mathcal{B}),\ I\subseteq\mathrm{RAnn}(\mathcal{B})\}

and the Serre subcategory

(6.4.5)

\HC^{G}_{\partial}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I):=\{\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\mid I\subsetneq\mathrm{LAnn}(\mathcal{B}),\ I\subsetneq\mathrm{RAnn}(\mathcal{B})\}\subset\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\}.

Note that
\HC_{\partial}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)=\HC^{G}_{\partial\mathbb{O}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\cap\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
, this follows from (iii) of Proposition
3.1.1
. Form the quotient category

(6.4.6)

\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I):=\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)/\HC^{G}_{\partial}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I).

Every bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})
can be regarded as a
G
-equivariant Harish-Chandra
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule via the co-moment map
\Phi:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}
. The resulting bimodule is contained in the subcategory
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\subset\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. This defines a forgetful functor

\Phi^{*}:\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I).

This functor takes
\HC^{G}_{\partial}(\mathcal{A})
to
\HC^{G}_{\partial\mathbb{O}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
and thus descends to a functor (still denoted by
\Phi^{*}
)

\Phi^{*}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I).

On the other hand, there is a restriction functor

\bullet_{\dagger}:\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W}),

see Section
3.5
. By
3.5.1
, this functor gives rise to a full embedding
\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\hookrightarrow\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})
. To avoid notational confusion, we will denote this embedding by
\bullet_{\dagger^{\prime}}
for the remainder of this subsection. The next proposition shows that
\bullet_{\dagger}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Omega\modd
and
\bullet_{\dagger^{\prime}}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})
are compatible in a certain precise sense.

Choose
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
as in Lemma
6.1.1
. Note that
\Omega=R_{x}/R^{\circ}
. By Lemma
6.1.1
(ii), we can choose an
\Omega
-stable ideal
J\subset\mathcal{W}
of codimension 1 such that
\underline{J}
is the intersection of the
R
-conjugates of
J
, and furthermore

\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}\simeq\bigoplus_{r\in R/R_{x}}\mathcal{W}/rJ.

The filtered algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
comes equipped with a natural
R
-action as well as an
R
-equivariant algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{W}/\underline{J}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
.

A representation
V
of
\Omega
is the same thing as an
R_{x}/R^{\circ}
-equivariant module for
\mathcal{W}/J
. Consequently, there is a uniquely defined
R
-equivariant
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
-module
\mathsf{B}_{V}
with fiber
V
over the summand
\mathcal{W}/J
. We can view
\mathsf{B}_{V}
as an object in
\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})
.

Lemma 6.4.5
.

The following diagram of functors commutes (up to isomorphism)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\modd}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{\dagger^{\prime}}}

Proof.

We can assume in the proof that
G
is semisimple and simply connected: the general case is obtained from this one by passing to full subcategories. In particular, the
G
-equivariance condition for a Harish-Chandra
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule is vacuous, and
\Omega=\Gamma
. Also all categories involved are semisimple, so it is enough to establish an isomorphism on the level of objects.

Recall, (
4.13.2
), that
\bullet_{\dagger}
defines an equivalence
\overline{\HC}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma/\Gamma(\lambda)\operatorname{-mod}
.
Define
\breve{X}
and
\breve{\mathcal{A}}
(for
\widetilde{X}
and
\mathcal{A}
) as in the discussion preceding Lemma
4.14.2
. The functor
V\mapsto(\breve{\mathcal{A}}\otimes V)^{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to
\bullet^{\dagger}
by Lemma
4.14.2
(i) and so is a quasi-inverse equivalence for
\bullet_{\dagger}
, see (
4.13.2
). Thus, it suffices to exhibit a functorial isomorphism (we omit
\Phi^{*}
from the notation)

(6.4.7)

[(\breve{\mathcal{A}}\otimes V)^{\Gamma}]_{\dagger^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{B}_{V}.

Since the
\Gamma
-action on
\breve{\mathcal{A}}
fixes the image of the quantum comoment map,
we have a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism
(\breve{\mathcal{A}}\otimes V)_{\dagger^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\breve{\mathcal{A}}_{\dagger^{\prime}}\otimes V
of objects in
\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})
.
This gives rise to an isomorphism

(6.4.8)

[(\breve{\mathcal{A}}\otimes V)^{\Gamma}]_{\dagger^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\breve{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{\dagger}^{\prime}}\otimes V)^{\Gamma}.

While
\breve{\mathcal{A}}
is not, in general, a quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\breve{X}]
, it quantizes an algebra whose normalization is
\mathbb{C}[\breve{X}]
, see (ii) of Lemma
4.14.2
. So (i) and (ii) of Lemma
6.1.1
hold for
\breve{\mathcal{A}}
nonetheless. Thus

\breve{\mathcal{A}}_{\dagger^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigoplus_{r\in R/\breve{R}}\mathcal{W}/rJ,

where we write
\breve{R}
for the preimage in
R
of
\Gamma(\lambda)\subset R/R^{\circ}
. From this description it is easy to see that the right hand side of (
6.4.8
) coincides with
\mathsf{B}_{V}
.

∎

6.5.
Classification of unipotent ideals

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
and
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
be
G
-equivariant covers of a common nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
. As usual, let
\widetilde{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
and
\widecheck{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}])
. If
f:\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
is a morphism of
G
-equivariant covers, there is an induced morphism of spectra
\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{X}
. The latter morphism is a finite
G
-equivariant cover, which is étale over the subset
\widecheck{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\widecheck{X}
by Lemma
4.6.1
. Recall, Definition
1.5.2
, that this morphism is
almost étale
if it is étale over the locus

\widecheck{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cup\bigcup_{k}\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset\widecheck{X}.

Define a binary relation
\succeq
on the set of
G
-equivariant nilpotent covers as follows

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\succeq\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\iff\exists\ f:\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\text{ such that }\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{X}\text{ is an almost \'{e}tale cover}

Definition 6.5.1
.

Let
\sim
be the equivalence relation on the isomorphism classes of
G
-equivariant nilpotent covers defined by taking the symmetric closure of
\succeq
. Denote the equivalence class of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
by
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
.

Below, we will show that unipotent ideals are parameterized by equivalence classes of covers. Our main result requires a bit of preparation.

Lemma 6.5.2
.

Suppose
G
is simple. Then
\widetilde{X}
satisfies condition (
5.0.1
) except in the following case:
G
is the (unique) simple group of type
E_{8}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is the principal nilpotent orbit.

Proof.

If
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G
-equivariant cover, then the subgroups
\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2)
corresponding to the codimension 2 leaves in
\widehat{X}
are contained in the subgroups
\Gamma_{k}\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2)
for
\widetilde{X}
, see (
4.6.3
). Thus, we can reduce to the case when
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is an orbit. The singularities corresponding to the codimension 2 leaves in the closures of nilpotent orbits were described in the classical cases by Kraft and Procesi (
[
KP81
, Main Thm]
,
[
KP82
, Thm 2]
) and in the exceptional cases by Fu et.al. (
[
FJLS15
, Sec 13]
). The lemma follows by inspection of the references above.
∎

We note that it possible to give a more conceptual proof of Lemma
6.5.2
, at least in exceptional types. The proof requires some machinery which is developed in Section
7.2
. We refer the reader to Remark
7.2.6
.

Lemma 6.5.3
.

Let
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
be an equivalence class of
G
-equivariant nilpotent covers. Then

(i)

[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
contains a unique maximal element
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
.

(ii)

\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is Galois over every cover in
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
.

(iii)

There is an equivalence of categories
\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widehat{X}})\simeq\Vect
.

Proof.

For (i), it suffices to show that
\widetilde{X}
admits a unique maximal almost étale
G
-equivariant cover
\widehat{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
. For
G
simply connected, this is a special case of Proposition
5.4.3
. The general case is proved in the same way. For similar reasons,
\widehat{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
is Galois, proving (ii).

For (iii), note that
G
decomposes as a product
G=H\times E_{8}\times...\times E_{8}
, where
H
is a reductive group containing no simple factors of type
E_{8}
and
E_{8}
is the (unique) simple group of type
E_{8}
. Indeed, every connected reductive group admits a central isogeny from a product of simple groups and a torus, and the simply connected group of type
E_{8}
is adjoint. Thus, it suffices to prove (iii) separately for
G=H
and
G=E_{8}
.

First suppose
G=H
. Then
\widehat{X}
satisfies property (
5.0.1
) by Lemma
6.5.2
. Let
\Omega=\pi_{1}^{G}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}),\Gamma=\pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})
. Since
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is maximal in its equivalence class,
\Gamma=\Gamma(0)
, see Proposition
5.4.3
and Remark
5.4.4
. Thus
\Omega=\Omega(0)
and by Proposition
6.4.2
there is an equivalence

\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widehat{X}})\simeq\Omega/\Omega(0)\simeq\Vect,

proving (iii) in this case.
Next suppose
G=E_{8}
. If
\mathbb{O}
is the principal nilpotent orbit, then
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=1
. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}=\mathbb{O}
and
\Gamma=1
. By the results of Section
4.13
, there is an embedding
\overline{\HC}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widehat{X}})\hookrightarrow\Gamma\modd\simeq\Vect
. (iii) follows at once. If
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is not the principal orbit, then
\widehat{X}
satisfies property (
5.0.1
), see again Lemma
6.5.2
. So we can argue as in the case of
G=H
.
∎

Proposition 6.5.4
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
,
\dot{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
be
G
-equivariant covers. Then

[\dot{\mathbb{O}}]=[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]\iff I_{0}(\dot{\mathbb{O}})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}).

Moreover, if
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is maximal in its equivalence class, and
\Pi:=\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
, then there is a
G
-equivariant algebra isomorphism

(6.5.1)

[(U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}))_{\dagger}]^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}.

Proof.

Note that every
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover is a homogeneous space for the universal cover of the derived subgroup of
G
. Thus, we can reduce to the case when
G
is semisimple and simply connected. The proof has several steps.

Step 1
. First we prove the easy implication

[\dot{\mathbb{O}}]=[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]\Rightarrow I_{0}(\dot{\mathbb{O}})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}).

By Lemma
6.5.3
, we can assume
\dot{\mathbb{O}}
is the maximal cover in
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. Hence, there is a Galois cover
\dot{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
such that the induced map
\dot{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\dot{\mathbb{O}}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])=\widetilde{X}
is almost étale. By Proposition
5.4.5
, there is a filtered algebra embedding
i:\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\dot{X}}
, and by Lemma
4.11.2
,
i\circ\Phi_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}=\Phi_{0}^{\dot{X}}
. Thus

I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\ker{(\Phi_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})}=\ker{(i\circ\Phi_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})}=\ker{(\Phi_{0}^{\dot{X}})}=I_{0}(\dot{\mathbb{O}}),

as desired.

Step 2
. In Steps 3-7, we will prove the hard implication
I_{0}(\dot{\mathbb{O}})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\Rightarrow[\dot{\mathbb{O}}]=[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. By Step 1, it suffices to show that every
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, maximal in its equivalence class, is determined by the ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. Our proof is somewhat indirect. We will first show that
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
determines a pair
(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}},\widecheck{\mathcal{A}})
consisting of a nilpotent cover
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
, covered by
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, and a filtered quantization
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
. The quantization
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
is constructed so that
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
coincides with kernel of the (unique) quantum co-moment map
U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
and
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
is the smallest cover of
\mathbb{O}
admitting a quantization with this property. Set
\widecheck{X}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}])
. The pair
(\widecheck{X},\widecheck{\mathcal{A}})
determines a unique Galois cover
\breve{\mathbb{O}}
of
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
(see the discussion preceding Lemma
4.14.2
) such that the image of
\overline{\HC}(\widecheck{\mathcal{A}})
in
\pi_{1}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})\modd
consists precisely of representations with trivial restriction to
\pi_{1}(\breve{\mathbb{O}})
. To prove that

I_{0}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\Rightarrow[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]=[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
we will show that
\breve{\mathbb{O}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
.

Step 3
.
Let
R
denote the reductive part of the centralizer of
e\in\mathbb{O}
, and let
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
.
Recall the functors
\bullet_{\dagger}:\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W})
and
\bullet^{\dagger}:\HC^{R}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
and the maps
\bullet_{\dagger}:\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W}),\bullet^{{\ddagger}}:\mathrm{Prim}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Prim}_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
from Section
3.5
.
By (iv) of Lemma
6.1.1
, applied to
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
, there is a
\Gamma
-invariant ideal
J\subset\mathcal{W}
of codimension 1 such that
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=J^{\ddagger}
. Consider the finite index subgroup
\operatorname{Stab}_{R}(J)\subset R

and let
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
be the cover corresponding to
\operatorname{Stab}_{R}(J)/R^{\circ}\subset\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})
. Thus,
\pi_{1}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})\simeq\Stab_{R}(J)/R^{\circ}\subseteq R/R^{\circ}\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})
. Since
J
is
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
-stable, we get the inclusion
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subseteq\pi_{1}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}})
. Thus
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a cover of
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
.

Step 4
.
Recall from Section
3.5
that
R^{\circ}
preserves
J
. Let
\underline{J}
denote the intersection of the
R
-conjugates of
J
. This is an
R
-stable ideal of finite codimension. Consider the Dixmier algebra
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}:=(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}
. We claim that
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
is a filtered quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
. This will be proved in steps 5-6.

Step 5
.
By Lemma
3.5.5
,
\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
embeds into
\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
as a
G
-stable subalgebra. This embedding intertwines the natural homomorphisms
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
, and hence the extension
\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
is finite.
By Proposition (v) of
3.5.1
,
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}_{\dagger}\simeq\mathcal{W}/\underline{J}
. The dimension of the right hand side is
|R/\operatorname{Stab}_{R}(J)|
. Thus
\dim(\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}_{\dagger})
coincides with the degree of the covering map
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\rightarrow\mathbb{O}
. In particular, the induced map
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Spec(\gr(\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}))
is birational. A finite birational morphism onto a normal variety is an isomorphism. Thus,
\gr(\widecheck{\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
is equivalent to the normality of
\Spec(\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}})
. This will be established in Step 6.

Step 6
.
By Step 5,
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
embeds as an open
G
-orbit in
\operatorname{Spec}(\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}})
with boundary codimension
\geq 2
. Thus,
\Spec(\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}})
is normal if
\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
is Cohen-Macaulay.
Note that the homomorphism
\mathcal{W}\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})_{\dagger}
factors through
\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}/\underline{J}
.
Also
\mathcal{W}/\underline{J}
, regarded as an object in
\HC_{\mathrm{fin}}^{R}(\mathcal{W})
, embeds as a direct summand in

(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})_{\dagger}
.
Therefore,
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{W}/\underline{J})^{\dagger}
, regarded as a filtered
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule, embeds as a direct summand in
[(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})_{\dagger}]^{\dagger}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
(so that
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
viewed as a filtered bimodule decomposes into the direct sum of
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
and another filtered bimodule). The last isomorphism follows from Lemma
6.1.1
(iii). Passing to associated graded bimodules, we deduce that
\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
, regarded as a
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
-module, embeds as a direct summand into
\gr\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\simeq\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. The latter, and hence the former, is a Cohen-Macaulay module. So
\gr\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
is a Cohen-Macaulay algebra. As noted above, this implies that
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
is a filtered quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}]
.

Step 7
.
Form
\breve{\mathbb{O}}
and
\breve{\mathcal{A}}
as in the discussion preceding Lemma
4.14.2
(for
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}])

and its quantization
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
).
The construction in Step 6 shows that
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
is a cover of
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
in the sense of Definition
4.14.1
. Also, since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is maximal in its equivalence class, we have

\HC(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})\simeq\operatorname{Vect}
, see (iii) of Lemma
6.5.3
. Thus by Corollary
4.14.4
, we have that
\breve{\mathbb{O}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\breve{\mathcal{A}}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
. This proves the implication
I_{0}(\dot{\mathbb{O}})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\Rightarrow[\dot{\mathbb{O}}]=[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
.

Step 8
. It remains to prove (
6.5.1
).
By Step 7,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\breve{\mathbb{O}}
. By Proposition
4.14.3
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a Galois cover of
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
. Let
\Pi^{\prime}\subset\Pi
be the Galois group of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
. By Proposition
4.14.3
,
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi^{\prime}}
. In particular, there is an inclusion

(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
intertwining the quantum comoment maps. Consider the chain of inclusions

(6.5.2)

U(\mathfrak{g})/I\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}\hookrightarrow\widecheck{\mathcal{A}},

where
I:=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. Recall that the functor
\bullet_{\dagger}
is exact by (i) of Proposition
3.5.1
. Applying
\bullet_{\dagger}
to (
6.5.2
) we obtain another chain of inclusions

(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}_{\dagger}.

By the construction of
\widecheck{\mathcal{A}}
in Step 4, the composition of these inclusions is an isomorphism. On the other hand, by (i) of Lemma
6.1.1
, we have

\dim(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}_{\dagger}=\operatorname{deg}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}/\Pi),\qquad\dim\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}=\operatorname{deg}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}),

where ‘deg’ denotes the degree of a finite cover of
\mathbb{O}
. We conclude that
\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi
. This proves (
6.5.1
).

∎

The following is an immediate consequence of Proposition
6.5.4
.

Theorem 6.5.5
.

Let
\mathbb{O}
be a fixed nilpotent
G
-orbit. The passage from
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
to
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
defines a bijection between the following two sets

•

Equivalence classes of
G
-equivariant covers
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
.

•

Unipotent ideals
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
with
V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
.

Corollary 6.5.6
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is maximal in its equivalence class and let
\Pi:=\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. If
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a maximal ideal, then the quantum comoment map
U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
induces an algebra algebra isomorphism

U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}.

Proof.

Lemma
3.5.2
applied to
\mathcal{B}=U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

implies
U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\xrightarrow{\sim}([U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})]_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
. On the other hand,
([U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})]_{\dagger})^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}
by (
6.5.1
). This completes the proof.
∎

6.6.
Classification of unipotent bimodules

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover, maximal in its equivalence class, and let
I:=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. Recall the category
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
and its quotient
\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
defined in (
6.4.4
) and (
6.4.6
), respectively. Note that the irreducible objects in
\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
are in bijection with
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
— this is a consequence of Definition
6.0.2
together with (iii) of Proposition
3.1.1
.

Let
\Pi:=\Aut_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
, and
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}:=\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}/{\Pi}
. By Proposition
5.1.1
, the
\Pi
-action on
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
lifts to the canonical quantization
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
. Let
\widecheck{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}])
and choose
\delta\in\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{\widecheck{X}}
so that

\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}}:=(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}.

An object
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}})
can be regarded as a
G
-equivariant Harish-Chandra bimodule for
U(\mathfrak{g})/I
via the co-moment map
\Phi:=\Phi_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}}:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}}
. As explained in Section
6.4
, this gives rise to a forgetful functor between the corresponding quotient categories

(6.6.1)

\Phi^{*}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I).

Lemma 6.6.1
.

The functor (
6.6.1
) is an equivalence of monoidal categories.

Proof.

The quantization
\mathcal{A}^{\widecheck{X}}_{\delta}
coincides with the Dixmier algebra
\mathcal{A}
constructed in Step 4 of the proof of Proposition
6.5.4
. This follows from the equality
\breve{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
established in Step 7 of that proof and the definition of
\breve{\mathcal{A}}
preceding Lemma
4.14.2
. By the construction of
\mathcal{A}
we have
(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)_{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
. By (iii) of Proposition

3.5.1
, the cokernel of the inclusion
U(\mathfrak{g})/I\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
is supported on
\partial\mathbb{O}
.
This shows that the functor
\Phi^{*}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
is well-defined. By the construction, it is monoidal.

Next we construct a functor
\Psi:\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\rightarrow\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A})
.
For
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
, consider the microlocalization
\mathcal{B}_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}}
of
\mathcal{B}
to
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}\subset\widecheck{X}
. Set
\Psi(\mathcal{B}):=\Gamma(\mathcal{B}_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}})
. Of course,
\Psi
descends to a functor (still denoted by
\Psi
)

\Psi:\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})

We claim that
\Phi^{*}:\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
and
\Psi:\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A})
are quasi-inverse equivalences. This follows at once from two observations. First,
\Phi^{*}
commutes with microlocalization to
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
. Second, for
\mathcal{B}\in\HC(\mathcal{A})
or
\HC(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
, the kernel and cokernel of the natural map
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{B}_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}})
are supported on
\partial\mathbb{O}
. Indeed, for any good filtration on
\mathcal{B}
, there is an embedding
\gr\Gamma(\mathcal{B}_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}})\hookrightarrow\Gamma(\gr\mathcal{B}|_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}})
and the natural homomorphism
\gr\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\Gamma(\gr\mathcal{B}|_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}})

factors as the composition

\gr\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\gr\Gamma(\mathcal{B}_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}})\hookrightarrow\Gamma(\gr\mathcal{B}|_{\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}})
.
∎

The next result provides a classification of
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
, compare to Theorem
1.5.4
.

Theorem 6.6.2
.

There is an equivalence of monoidal categories

\Pi\modd\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I),\qquad V\mapsto\Phi^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\otimes V)^{\Pi}.

In particular, there is a bijection

\{\text{irreducible representations of }\Pi\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G).

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is maximal in its equivalence class,
\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})\simeq\Vect
by Lemma
6.5.3
. Thus, by Corollary
6.4.4
, there is an equivalence of monoidal categories

(6.6.2)

\Pi\modd\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{\HC}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\widecheck{X}}),\qquad V\mapsto(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\otimes V)^{\Pi}.

We obtain the desired equivalence
\Pi\modd\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
by composing (
6.6.2
) with the equivalence of Lemma
6.6.1
.
∎

Corollary 6.6.3
.

Suppose
I
is maximal. Then there is an equivalence of monoidal categories

\Pi\modd\xrightarrow{\sim}\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I),\qquad V\mapsto\Phi^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\otimes V)^{\Pi}.

In particular, every bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
is of the form
(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\otimes V)^{\Pi}
for a uniquely defined irreducible
V\in\Pi\modd
.

Proof.

Since
I
is maximal,
\HC^{G}_{\partial}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)=0
, see (
6.4.5
). Thus, the quotient functor
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\HC}^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)
is an equivalence. Now the corollary follows from Theorem
6.6.2
.
∎

6.7.
A proof of a conjecture of Vogan

Recall from Section
3.4
that every Harish-Chandra bimodule
\mathcal{B}\in\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
can be regarded as a
G
-representation via the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}
. If
\mathcal{B}
is unipotent, then this
G
-representation is of a very special form.

Proposition 6.7.1
.

Assume
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a maximal ideal. Let
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
and let
e\in\mathbb{O}
. Then there is a finite-dimensional representation
\chi
of
G_{e}/G_{e}^{\circ}
such that

\mathcal{B}\simeq_{G}\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{G_{e}}\chi.

Proof.

Assume
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is maximal in its equivalence class, and let
\Pi=\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. By Corollary
6.6.3
, there is a uniquely defined irreducible
V\in\Pi\modd
such that
\mathcal{B}\simeq\Phi^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\otimes V)^{\Pi}
. The filtration on
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
induces a good filtration on
\mathcal{B}
, and there is a natural isomorphism of graded
G
-equivariant
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
-modules

(6.7.1)

\gr(\mathcal{B})\simeq\gr(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}\otimes V)^{\Pi}\simeq(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]\otimes V)^{\Pi}.

Fix
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
as in Section
6.6
. Choose
x\in\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
over
e
and
y\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
over
x
. Thus,
G_{y}
is a normal subgroup of finite-index in
G_{x}
and
\Pi\simeq G_{x}/G_{y}
. Regard
V
as an irreducible representation of
G_{x}
, trivial on
G_{y}
, and form the
G
-equivariant vector bundle
\mathcal{V}:=G\times^{G_{x}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/G_{x}\simeq\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}
. Then
(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]\otimes V)^{\Pi}
can be identified with
\Gamma(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}},\mathcal{V})
. As
G
-representations

\Gamma(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}},\mathcal{V})\simeq_{G}\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{G_{x}}V\simeq_{G}\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G}_{G_{e}}(\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G_{e}}_{G_{x}}V).

Now set
\chi=\mathrm{AlgInd}^{G_{e}}_{G_{x}}V
to complete the proof.
∎

Remark 6.7.2
.

In Section
9
, we will show that all special unipotent ideals
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee})
are unipotent. Thus, the conclusion of Proposition
6.7.1
holds for all special unipotent bimodules. In particular, Proposition
6.7.1
provides an affirmative answer to a conjecture of Vogan (
[
Vog91
, Conj 12.1]
) in the case of complex groups. Special cases of this conjecture were previously established by
[
Mas18
]
,
[
Bar17
]
, and
[
Won18
]
.

Proposition
6.7.1
is the ‘complex’ case of a more general conjecture about irreducible representations of real reductive Lie groups, see
[
Vog91
, Sec 12]
. We conclude this section with a discussion of this more general conjecture. A proof will appear in a forthcoming paper of the second-named author and Dougal Davis
[
DM
]
.

Let
K
be a symmetric subgroup of
G
, i.e. the fixed point locus of a an algebraic involution. Suppose
\mathcal{B}
is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module such that
V(\mathrm{Ann}(\mathcal{B}))=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Then the associated variety of
\mathcal{B}
is a union of components of
\overline{\mathbb{O}}\cap(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
, and each such component is the closure of a
K
-orbit on
\mathbb{O}\cap(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
, see
[
Vog91
, Section 4]
. Now suppose that the associated variety of
\mathcal{B}
contains a
K
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{K}\subset\mathbb{O}\cap(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
such that

(6.7.2)

\codim(\partial\mathbb{O}_{K},\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{K})\geq 2.

Then the associated variety of
\mathcal{B}
is
equal
to the closure of
\mathbb{O}_{K}
, see
[
Vog91
, Theorem 4.6]
(we note that Condition
6.7.2
is automatic if
\codim(\partial\mathbb{O},\overline{\mathbb{O}})\geq 4
).

Finally suppose that
\Ann(\mathcal{B})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
. In this situation, one can associate to
\mathcal{B}
a certain
K
-equivariant vector bundle
\mathcal{L}_{K}
on
\mathbb{O}_{K}
, see
[
Vog91
, Sec 2]
. In fact, this vector bundle has the additional structure of a twisted local system (with ‘twist’ corresponding to a square root of the canonical bundle on
\mathbb{O}_{K}
, see
[
Vog91
, Thm 8.7]
). In this setting, Vogan’s conjecture is as follows.

Conjecture 6.7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module such that
\Ann(\mathcal{B})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
. Suppose that the associated variety of
\mathcal{B}
is
\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{K}
, where
\mathbb{O}_{K}
is a
K
-orbit on
\mathbb{O}\cap(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
satisfying Condition
6.7.2
. Then there is an isomorphism of
K
-representations

\mathcal{B}\simeq_{K}\Gamma(\mathbb{O}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{B}})

The second-named author and Dougal Davis will provide a proof of this conjecture in a forthcoming paper
[
DM
]
.

7.
Parabolic Induction of Hamiltonian Quantizations

Let
G
be a connected reductive algebraic group. In Section
6
, we defined, for each
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, a completely prime primitive ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
, Definition
6.0.1
, and a finite set of irreducible Harish-Chandra bimodules
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
, Definition
6.0.2
. Recall that in Definition
6.5.1
we have defined an equivalence relation on the set of nilpotent covers.

We proved that the set
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
is in natural bijection with irreducible representations of the (finite) automorphism group of the maximal cover in the equivalence class
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
, see Theorem
6.6.2
, and that all bimodules in
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
are, as
G
-representations, of the form conjectured by Vogan, see Proposition
6.7.1
. However, three properties of unipotent ideals and bimodules were left more or less unaddressed, namely:

•

All unipotent bimodules are unitary.

•

All unipotent ideals are maximal.

•

All special unipotent bimodules are unipotent.

Compare to Desiderata (1),(3), and (6) from Section
1.3
. We will verify these properties in Sections
8
-
10
. To do so, we will need a method for computing the infinitesimal characters of unipotent ideals. This method will be developed in Section
8
. In this section, we establish some preliminary facts about filtered quantizations of nilpotent covers which are needed for these computations.

We will summarize the method here in order to motivate what follows. Assume for simplicity that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid, see Section
2.4
, and maximal in its equivalence class (it is not particularly difficult to reduce the computation to this special case and we will do so in Section
8.1
). These assumptions guarantee that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is the universal
G
-equivariant cover of
\mathbb{O}
. Taking
\Aut_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
-invariants in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
, we get a Hamiltonian quantization
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}^{X}
of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
corresponding to a special parameter
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{P}^{X}
constructed in Example
5.3.2
. Moreover,
I_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{O})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. Choose a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a birationally rigid
L
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{L}
such that
\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\mathbb{O}_{L}
. Since
\mathbb{O}_{L}
is birationally rigid,
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}_{L}]
admits a unique filtered quantization,
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{X_{L}}
. In Section
7.3
, we will define the notion of parabolic induction for Hamiltonian quantizations. For every ‘induction parameter’
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
, we construct an ‘induced’ quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
, denoted
\Ind^{G}_{L}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{X_{L}}
. Every Hamiltonian quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
arises in this fashion. More precisely, there is a linear isomorphism
\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{X}
such that
\Ind^{G}_{L}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{X_{L}}
coincides with the Hamiltonian quantization
\mathcal{A}^{X}_{\eta(\lambda)}
. Given an induction parameter
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
and the infinitesimal character of
I_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{L})
, it is easy to determine the infinitesimal character of
I_{\eta(\lambda)}(\mathbb{O})
. So we need to determine the element
\eta^{-1}(\epsilon)\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
. Computing
\eta
is a nontrivial problem, to which the bulk of this section is devoted. In Sections
7.5
-
7.7
, we solve this problem under certain conditions on
\mathbb{O}
. This reduces the problem of computing unipotent infinitesimal characters to the case of birationally rigid orbits. By a similar argument, one can further reduce to the case of rigid nilpotent orbits. For such orbits, we can compute the infinitesimal characters using the results of McGovern (
[
McG94
]
) and Premet (
[
Pre13
]
).

7.1.
Partial resolutions of nilpotent covers

Choose a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and an
L
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
. Fix a parabolic subgroup
P\subset G
with Levi decomposition
P=LN
, and form the
G
-equivariant fiber bundle

\pi:\widetilde{Y}:=G\times^{P}(\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P,

see Section
2.4
. There is a proper map
\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, which factors through a partial resolution
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
.

In this preparatory section, we will compute the Picard group and class group of
\widetilde{Y}
. We begin with a general observation.

Lemma 7.1.1
.

Let
V
be a normal affine cone. Then
\Pic(V)=0
.

Proof.

Choose a line bundle
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(V)
. Since
V
is normal,
\mathcal{L}
can be made
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant, see e.g.
[
KKLV89
, Proposition 2.4]
. Hence,
\mathcal{L}
has the structure of a graded
\mathbb{C}[V]
-module. Let
0\in V
be the fixed point for the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action and consider the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subset\mathbb{C}[V]
. Since
\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle, the fiber
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{L}/\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathcal{L}
is one-dimensional. Choose a nonzero element
\overline{s}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
. This element lifts to a section
s\in\mathcal{L}
which generates
\mathcal{L}
as a
\mathbb{C}[V]
-module
by the graded version of Nakayama’s lemma. The action map defines a surjection
\mathbb{C}[V]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}
, which is manifestly injective, since
\mathcal{L}
is torsion-free.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{X}(L)
denote the character lattice of
L
. If
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(L)
, write
\mathcal{L}_{G/P}(\chi)
for the
G
-equivariant line bundle on
G/P
with fiber
\chi
at
[P]\in G/P
.

Proposition 7.1.2
.

The following are true:

(i)

The map
\pi^{*}:\Pic(G/P)\rightarrow\Pic(\widetilde{Y})
is an isomorphism. If
G
is semisimple and simply connected, there is a further isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{G/P}:\mathfrak{X}(L)\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pic(G/P)
.

(ii)

There is a short exact sequence

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\widetilde{Y})\overset{\mathrm{div}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Cl(\widetilde{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}0,

where
\Cl
denote the class group, and
\mathrm{div}
is the natural map from line bundles to divisors.

Proof.

First, we prove (ii). Let
C\subset G/P
be the open Bruhat cell and let
\widetilde{C}=\pi^{-1}(C)
. There is an
N^{-}
-invariant isomorphism

(7.1.1)

\widetilde{C}\simeq N^{-}\times\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}

Taking class groups, we get an identification
\Cl(\widetilde{C})\simeq\Cl(\widetilde{X}_{L})
. Since
\codim(\widetilde{X}_{L}-\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L},\widetilde{X}_{L})\geq 2
,
\Cl(\widetilde{X}_{L})\simeq\Cl(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
and since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is smooth,
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})\simeq\Pic(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
. Thus,
\Cl(\widetilde{C})\simeq\Pic(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
.

Since
\widetilde{C}
is an open subset of
\widetilde{Y}
there is a surjective map
\mathrm{res}:\Cl(\widetilde{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Cl(\widetilde{C})
. We will show that the following sequence is exact

(7.1.2)

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\widetilde{Y})\overset{\mathrm{div}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Cl(\widetilde{Y})\overset{\mathrm{res}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Cl(\widetilde{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}0.

First, note that
\mathrm{div}:\Pic(\widetilde{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Cl(\widetilde{Y})
is injective, since
\widetilde{Y}
is normal. Next, observe that the composition

\Pic(\widetilde{Y})\overset{\mathrm{div}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Cl(\widetilde{Y})\overset{\mathrm{res}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Cl(\widetilde{C})

coincides with the composition

\Pic(\widetilde{Y})\overset{\mathrm{res}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Pic(\widetilde{C})\overset{\mathrm{div}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Cl(\widetilde{C}),

By (
7.1.1
),
\Pic(\widetilde{C})\simeq\Pic(\widetilde{X}_{L})
, and by Lemma
7.1.1
,
\Pic(\widetilde{X}_{L})\simeq 0
. Thus,
\Pic(\widetilde{C})\simeq 0
and
\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{div})\subseteq\ker(\mathrm{res})
.

Next we prove the reverse inclusion:
\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{div})\supseteq\ker(\mathrm{res})
. Let
D=G/P-C
and
\widetilde{D}=\pi^{-1}(D)=\widetilde{Y}-\widetilde{C}
. Denote the irreducible components of
D
by
D_{1},...,D_{r}
. Note that each
D_{i}
is of codimension 1 and the set
\{D_{1},...,D_{r}\}
is parameterized by simple roots for
\mathfrak{g}
which are not contained in
\mathfrak{l}
, see
[
Bor69
, Thm 21.14]
. For each
D_{i}
, let
\widetilde{D}_{i}=\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
. Since
\pi:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P
is a fiber bundle with irreducible fibers,
\widetilde{D}_{1},...,\widetilde{D}_{r}
are the irreducible components of
\widetilde{D}
. If
[E]\in\ker(\mathrm{res})
, then
[E]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[\widetilde{D}_{i}]
for integers
c_{1},...,c_{r}
. Hence

[E]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[\widetilde{D}_{i}]=\mathrm{div}\left(\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[D_{i}])\right),

where
\mathcal{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[D_{i}]))\in\Pic(G/P)
is the line bundle corresponding to the class
\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[D_{i}]\in\Cl(G/P)
. In particular,
\ker(\mathrm{res})\subseteq\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{div})
. Thus, we have shown that (
7.1.2
) is exact. Using the isomorphism
\Cl(\widetilde{C})\simeq\Pic(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
described in the first paragraph of the proof, we get a short exact sequence

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\widetilde{Y})\overset{\mathrm{div}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\Cl(\widetilde{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}0,

as desired.

Next, we prove (i). For
G
semisimple, the isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{G/P}:\mathfrak{X}(L)\simeq\Pic(G/P)
is standard, see e.g.
[
KKV89
, Prop 3.2]
. It remains to show that
\pi^{*}:\Pic(G/P)\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pic(\widetilde{Y})
.

Restricting along the zero section
G/P\subset\widetilde{Y}
defines a left inverse for
\pi^{*}
. In particular,
\pi^{*}
is injective. To prove it is surjective, choose a line bundle
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(\widetilde{Y})
. By the proof of the inclusion
\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{div})\supseteq\ker(\mathrm{res})
above,

\mathrm{div}(\mathcal{L})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[\widetilde{D}_{i}]=\mathrm{div}\left(\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[D_{i}])\right).

Since
\mathrm{div}
is injective, this implies
\mathcal{L}\simeq\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}[D_{i}])
. Thus,
\pi^{*}
is surjective, as desired. This completes the proof.
∎

We say that a line bundle
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(\widetilde{Y})
is
relatively ample
if it is ample with respect to the projective morphism
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
. Write
\Pic^{a}(\widetilde{Y})\subset\Pic(\widetilde{Y})
for the semigroup of relatively ample line bundles and
\mathfrak{X}(L)^{>0}\subset\mathfrak{X}(L)
for the semigroup of (strictly)
P
-dominant weights.

Proposition 7.1.3
.

The isomorphism
\pi^{*}:\Pic(G/P)\simeq\Pic(\widetilde{Y})
of Proposition
7.1.2
restricts to a semigroup isomorphism
\Pic^{a}(G/P)\simeq\Pic^{a}(\widetilde{Y})
. If
G
is semisimple and simply connected, there is a further isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{G/P}:\mathfrak{X}(L)^{>0}\simeq\Pic^{a}(G/P)
.

Proof.

First, observe that there is a finite morphism

\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G\times^{P}\widetilde{X}\simeq G/P\times\widetilde{X},

and
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
coincides with the composition
\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P\times\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
(where the second map is the projection). If
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(G/P)
is ample, then
\pi^{*}\mathcal{L}
coincides with the pullback to
\widetilde{Y}
of the relatively ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}\in\Pic(G/P\times\widetilde{X})
. Since
\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P\times\widetilde{X}
is finite, this pullback is relatively ample.

Conversely, suppose that
\pi^{*}\mathcal{L}
is relatively ample. The zero section
G/P\subset\widetilde{Y}
is the preimage under
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
of
0\in\widetilde{X}
. Hence,
\mathcal{L}\simeq(\pi^{*}\mathcal{L})|_{G/P}
is ample.

Now suppose
G
is semisimple and simply connected. It is a standard fact that for a character
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(L)
, the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{G/P}(\chi)
is ample if and only if
\chi
is strictly dominant for
P
. Thus,
\mathcal{L}_{G/P}:\mathfrak{X}(L)\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pic(G/P)
restricts to an isomorphism

\mathfrak{X}(L)^{>0}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pic^{a}(G/P)
, as asserted.
∎

Fix the notation of Section
7.1
, i.e.
P=LN
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
,
\widetilde{X}_{L}
,
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
,
\pi:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P
, and so on. Let
\mu_{L}:\widetilde{X}_{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{l}^{*}
denote the moment map. For the remainder of this subsection, we will also assume:

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}\text{ is birationally rigid}.

Let
N_{G}(L)
denote the normalizer in
G
of
L
. Note that
N_{G}(L)
acts on
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])
via the co-adjoint action
\Ad^{*}
. Consider the group

N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}):=\{(n,\sigma)\in N_{G}(L)\times\Aut(\widetilde{X}_{L})\mid\Ad^{*}(n)\circ\mu_{L}=\mu_{L}\circ\sigma\}.

We can regard
L
as a normal subgroup of
N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
via the natural embedding

L\hookrightarrow N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}),\qquad l\mapsto(l,l).

We define

\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}:=N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})/L.

Since both
N_{G}(L)/L
and
\Aut(\widetilde{X}_{L})
are finite, so is
\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}
.

Let
\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{X}}:=\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{Y}_{univ}]
.

7.2.
\mathbb{Q}
-terminalizations of nilpotent covers

Fix the notation of Section
7.1
, i.e.
P=LN
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
,
\widetilde{X}_{L}
,
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
,
\pi:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P
, and so on. For the remainder of this subsection, we will also assume:

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}\text{ is birationally rigid}.

Under this assumption, we will see below that the partial resolution
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization. In the case when
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is an orbit, this was proved by the first-named author (see
[
Los16
, Cor 4.6]
). For universal covers in classical types, a similar result was obtained by Namikawa (see
[
Nam20
]
). The result below is due to the third-named author (see
[
Mat20
, Cor 4.3]
).

Theorem 7.2.1
.

The partial resolution
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization.

Theorem
7.2.1
will allow us to provide purely Lie-theoretic descriptions of the Namikawa space
\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}
and Namikawa Weyl group
W^{\widetilde{X}}
attached to
\widetilde{X}
(see Section
4.5
for definitions). These descriptions are as follows. Let
\underline{\pi}
denote the restriction of
\pi:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P
to
\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and consider the pullback map on cohomology
\underline{\pi}^{*}:H^{2}(G/P,\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}
. Note that
H^{2}(G/P,\mathbb{C})
is identified with
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])
. Consider the composition

(7.2.1)

\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])\simeq H^{2}(G/P,\mathbb{C})\overset{\underline{\pi}^{*}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}H^{2}(\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}},

We will see below that this map is an isomorphism.

Next, we will describe
W^{\widetilde{X}}
in terms of the data
(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
. Let
\Ad^{*}
denote the co-adjoint action of
N_{G}(L)
on
\mathfrak{l}^{*}
and let
\mu_{L}:\widetilde{X}_{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{l}^{*}
denote the moment map. Consider the group

N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}):=\{(\eta,\zeta)\in N_{G}(L)\times\Aut(\widetilde{X}_{L})\,|\,\Ad^{*}(\eta)\circ\mu_{L}=\mu_{L}\circ\zeta\}.

We can regard
L
as a normal subgroup of
N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
via the natural embedding

L\hookrightarrow N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}),\qquad l\mapsto(l,l).

Consider the quotient group

\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}:=N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})/L.

Since both
N_{G}(L)/L
and
\Aut(\widetilde{X}_{L})
are finite, so is
\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}
acts on
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])
via the natural projection
\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}N_{G}(L)/L
.

There is a natural homomorphism

\kappa:\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Aut_{G}(\widetilde{X}).

For nilpotent orbits,
\kappa
was defined in Step (ii) of the proof of
[
Los16
, Prop 4.7]
. For nilpotent covers, the definition is analogous. We note that
\kappa
is surjective by
[
Nam20a
, Proposition 13]
.

Proposition 7.2.2
.

The following are true:

(i)

The map
\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

W^{\widetilde{X}}
is identified with the normal subgroup

\ker{\kappa}\subset\widetilde{W}^{\widetilde{X}}

with its natural action on
