, Sec 13]
,
\mathbb{O}_{M,2}=\Ind^{M}_{A_{4}+A_{1}}\{0\}
. Hence,
\Ind^{G}_{M}A_{4}+A_{1}=\Ind^{G}_{A_{4}+A_{1}}\{0\}=E_{6}(a_{1})+A_{1}
. So
M=M_{1}
by Lemma
7.5.10
.

Computation of
\eta_{1}
. Since
\mathbb{O}
admits a birationally rigid cover and
\Sigma_{1}\simeq A_{1}
, the hypotheses of Proposition
7.7.4
are satisfied. Thus

\eta_{1}(\tau_{1}(1))=c_{1}\omega_{1}(1),\qquad c_{1}=2|\Pic(\mathbb{O}_{M_{1}})||\Pic(\mathbb{O})|^{-1}.

Note that
\Pic(\mathbb{O})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})_{\mathrm{ab}}\simeq(S_{3})_{\mathrm{ab}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\Pic(\mathbb{O}_{M_{1}})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{M_{1}})_{\mathrm{ab}}\simeq 1
. Thus,
c_{1}=2(1)/2=1
. By definition,
\tau_{1}(1)
is the dominant generator of the free abelian group
\mathfrak{X}(M)
, i.e.
\tau_{1}(1)=\varpi_{8}
. Hence

(7.7.14)

\eta_{1}(\varpi_{8})=\omega_{1}(1).

Computation of
M_{2}
. For our given choice of
L
,
M_{2}
is non-standard. So the computation of
M_{2}
is more involved than it was for
M_{1}
. Let
L^{\prime}
denote the standard Levi subgroup (of Lie type
A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1}
) corresponding to the simple roots
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8}
. In
E_{8}
, a Levi subgroup is determined up to conjugacy by the isomorphism type of its Lie algebra. So
L
and
L^{\prime}
are
G
-conjugate. Furthermore,
atlas
provides us with a Weyl group element
w\in W
conjugating
L^{\prime}
onto
L
.

Let
K^{\prime}
denote the standard Levi subgroup (of Lie type
D_{5}+A_{2}
) corresponding to the simple roots
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8}
and let
K=wK^{\prime}
. Note that
L^{\prime}\subset K^{\prime}
and therefore
L\subset K
. Using Lemma
7.5.10
, we will show that
K=M_{2}
. Note that

\mathbb{O}_{K}=\Ind^{D_{5}+A_{2}}_{D_{3}+A_{1}+A_{2}}\{0\}=\mathbb{O}_{(3^{2},1^{4})}\times\{0\}

(here
D_{3}+A_{1}+A_{2}
embeds in
D_{5}+A_{2}
, with
D_{3}+A_{1}\subset D_{5}
). By
[
KP82
, Table 1]
,
\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{K}
is normal in codimension 2. There is one boundary orbit
\mathbb{O}_{K,1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{K}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{K,1}=\mathbb{O}_{(3,2^{2},1)}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
A_{1}
. By
[
DE09
, Sec 4]
,
\Ind^{G}_{K}\mathbb{O}_{(3,2^{2},1^{3})}=A_{7}
. So
K=M_{2}
by Lemma
7.5.10
.

Computation of
\eta_{2}
. Since
\dim(\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{m}_{2}))=1
and
\dim(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{*})=2
,
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{2})
acts on
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{*}
by the nontrivial diagram automorphism. In particular,
\mathfrak{P}_{2}^{X}=(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{*})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{2})}
is spanned by the element
\omega_{1}(2)+\omega_{2}(2)
. Since the monodromy action is nontrivial, neither Proposition
7.7.1
nor Proposition
7.7.4
can be straightforwardly applied. We will adapt the argument of Proposition
7.7.1
to show that

\eta_{2}(\tau_{1}(2))=\omega_{1}(2)+\omega_{2}(2).

Note that
X_{M_{2}}
has a single codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}\subset X_{M_{2}}
, and the corresponding singularity
\Sigma\subset X
is of type
A_{1}
. Let
Z_{M_{2}}
denote the cotangent bundle of the partial flag variety for
M_{2}
corresponding to
L
—it is a
\mathbb{Q}
-terminalization (in fact, a symplectic resolution) of
X_{M_{2}}
. Furthermore, there is a natural identification
\mathfrak{P}_{2}^{X}\simeq H^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{k},\mathbb{C})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L})}
which takes
\omega_{1}(2)+\omega_{2}(2)\in\mathfrak{P}_{2}^{X}
to
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{k},\mathbb{C})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L})}
. Thus, it suffices to show that the identification
\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})\simeq H^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{k},\mathbb{C})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L})}
induced by the restriction map
\Pic(Z_{M_{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\mathfrak{S}_{k})
takes
\tau_{1}(2)
to
\omega_{1}(2)+\omega_{2}(2)
. By Proposition
7.1.2
, we have
\Pic(Z_{M_{2}})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
(with
\overline{\pi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{G/Q}(\tau_{1}(2))\mapsfrom 1
). Let
E
denote the exceptional divisor of
\overline{\rho}:Z_{M_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X_{M_{2}}
. Since
X_{M_{2}}
has only one singularity (of type
A_{2}
) and the monodromy action is nontrivial,
E
is irreducible, and the mapping
1\mapsto[E]
induces a short exact sequence as in Lemma
7.7.5

\mathbb{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Cl(Z_{M_{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\mathbb{O}_{M_{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}0.

Since
\Pic(\mathbb{O}_{M_{2}})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, and
[E]
is anti-dominant, we have
[E]=\mathrm{div}(\mathcal{L}_{Z_{M_{2}}}(-2\tau_{1}(2)))
.

Note that
E_{\Sigma}:=E\cap\mathfrak{S}_{k}
is the union of the two irreducible components of the exceptional divisor of
\mathfrak{S}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Sigma
. For simple roots
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{sl}(3)}^{*}\simeq H^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{k},\mathbb{C})
, we have
\langle[E_{\Sigma}],\alpha_{i}\rangle=-2+1=-1
, and therefore
[E_{\Sigma}]=-\omega_{1}(2)-\omega_{2}(2)
. Arguing as in the proof of
7.7.1
, we deduce that
\eta_{2}(\tau_{1}(2))=\omega_{1}(2)+\omega_{2}(2)
, as asserted.

By definition,
\tau_{1}(2)
is the dominant generator of the free abelian group
\mathfrak{X}(M_{2})
. Thus,in the notation of the previous step,
\tau_{1}(2)=w\varpi_{6}
. Using
atlas
we compute
\tau_{1}(2)=\varpi_{4}-3\varpi_{8}
. Hence

(7.7.15)

\eta_{2}(\varpi_{4}-3\varpi_{8})=\omega_{1}(2)+\omega_{2}(2).

7.8.
A proof of the Dixmier conjecture

In this section, we will prove the following result, resolving a conjecture of Vogan (see
[
Vog90
, Conj 2.3]
).

Theorem 7.8.1
.

Suppose
G
is semisimple.
The following are true:

(1)

There is an injective correspondence

\mathrm{Dix}:\{G\text{-eqvt covers of coadjoint orbits}\}\hookrightarrow\{\text{Dixmier algebras for }G\}

such that for any cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}
, there is an isomorphism of
G
-representations

\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1})\simeq_{G}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}].

(2)

The image of
\mathrm{Dix}
is the set of Dixmier algebras which arise as filtered quantizations of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
runs over the set of
G
-equivariant nilpotent covers.

(3)

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
nilpotent
cover, then
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
(where the latter is regarded as a Dixmier algebra via the uniquely defined co-moment map
\Phi_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
).

Note that Theorem
7.8.1
generalizes
[
Los16
, Thm 5.3(1)]
, and is proved by similar methods.

Proof.

We will establish bijections between the following four sets.

(a)

The set of
G
-equivariant covers of coadjoint
G
-orbits (up to isomorphisms of
G
-equivariant covers).

(b)

The set of
G
-equivariant filtered Poisson deformations of algebras
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
(up to
G
-equivariant isomorphisms of filtered Poisson algebras).

(c)

The set of
G
-equivariant filtered quantizations of algebras

\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
(up to
G
-equivariant isomorphisms of filtered algebras).

(d)

The set of
G
-equivariant filtered quantizations of algebras
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
(up to Dixmier algebra isomorphisms).

A bijection between (b) and (c) is constructed in
[
Los16
, Rmk 3.24]
: for
\widetilde{X}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
both sets are classified by
\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}/N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
and the bijection preserves the parameter. In particular,
\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{X}}_{0}
corresponds to the trivial deformation
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
.

A bijection between (c) and (d) is as follows.
Every
G
-equivariant filtered quantization
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
admits a unique quantum co-moment map
\Phi:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}
, see Lemma
4.11.2
. Note that
\Phi
turns
\mathcal{A}
into a Dixmier algebra for
G
. This defines a (tautologically) surjective map from (c) to (d). To prove that this map is injective we will show that the filtration on
\mathcal{A}
is uniquely recovered up to a
G
-equivariant algebra isomorphism. Consider the functor
\bullet_{\dagger}:\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{R}(\mathcal{W})
associated to the orbit
\mathbb{O}
covered by
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, see Section

3.5
. By Lemma
6.1.1
,
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
is concentrated in degree
0
and there is a
G
-equivariant filtered algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})^{\dagger}
. So the filtration is recovered uniquely. This shows that the map from (c) to (d) is injective.

It remains to establish a bijection between (a) and (b). To go from (b) to (a), we take a filtered Poisson deformation
\mathcal{A}^{0}
of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
and form its spectrum
\widetilde{X}^{1}
. The group
G
acts on
\widetilde{X}^{1}
in a Hamiltonian fashion. The moment map is unique because
G
is semisimple. The
G
-variety
\widetilde{X}^{1}
contains a (unique) open
G
-orbit since
\widetilde{X}
does, and the moment map realizes this open orbit as a cover of a coadjoint orbit. We map
\mathcal{A}^{0}
to this open orbit. Taking the disjoint union over all possible
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
, we get a map from (b) to (a).

To prove that this map is a bijection
we will need a parameterization of
G
-equivariant covers of coadjoint orbits. Generalizing
[
Los16
, Definition 1.2]
, we define the notion of a birationally minimal induction datum for a cover of a coadjoint orbit. This is a triple
(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L},\xi)
consisting of a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
, an
L
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
, and an element
\xi\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
. To each such triple, we can associate a
G
-equivariant cover of a coadjoint orbit, namely the open
G
-orbit in the variety
G\times^{P}(\{\xi\}\times\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})
, where
\widetilde{X}_{L}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}])
. This defines a bijection between the set of all
G
-equivariant covers of coadjoint orbits and the set of all birationally minimal induction data up to conjugation by
G
. Indeed, it suffices to show that two birationally minimal induction data for
\mathbb{O}^{1}
are
G
-conjugate. This easily reduces to the case when
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}
is a nilpotent cover. In this case, the conjugacy follows from (iii) of Proposition
2.4.1
.

Now let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}
be a
G
-equivariant cover with birationally minimal induction datum

(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L},\xi)
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
, and let
\mathsf{Cov}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
denote the set of covers
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}

which give rise to
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
via this construction. We will show that

(*)

the map from (b) to (a) defines a bijection between the set of
G
-equivariant Poisson deformations of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
and

\mathsf{Cov}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
.

Recall, Theorem
4.7.5
, that the set
\mathrm{PDef}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
of Poisson deformations of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
up to isomorphisms of Poisson deformations is in natural bijection with
\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}/W^{\widetilde{X}}
. By Remark
4.10.2
, every such deformation has a natural
G
-action. By the same remark, the group of graded
G
-equivariant automorphisms of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
acts on
\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}/W^{\widetilde{X}}
.
And the filtered Poisson deformations up to
G
-equivariant isomorphism of filtered Poisson algebras are classified by orbits of this group action.

By Proposition
7.2.2
, there is a natural isomorphism
\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}\simeq\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
. The Galois group
\Pi
of the cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\rightarrow\mathbb{O}
is naturally identified with the
group of
G
-equivariant graded Poisson automorphisms of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
, and, by (ii) of Proposition
7.2.2
, the Weyl group
W^{\widetilde{X}}
is the kernel of the natural epimorphism
N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})\twoheadrightarrow\Pi
. So the filtered Poisson deformations up to filtered
G
-equivariant Poisson isomorphism are parameterized by
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})/N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
. The deformation corresponding to the orbit of
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
is recovered as
\mathbb{C}[G\times^{P}(\{\xi\}\times\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})]
, this follows from (iii) of Proposition
7.2.2
. The open
G
-orbit therein coincides with the cover birationally induced from
(\mathfrak{l},\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L},\xi)
because the natural morphism

G\times^{P}(\{\xi\}\times\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{C}[G\times^{P}(\{\xi\}\times\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})])

is an isomorphism over the open orbit. To finish the proof of (*) note that the
G
-conjugacy classes of birationally minimal induction data with
\mathfrak{l},\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
fixed are in bijection with
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})/N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
.
Under the identifications of both sets with
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})/N_{G}(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
the map from (b) to (a) is the identity.

This proves claim (2) of the proposition. Claim (3) follows because the bijection between (b) and (c) sends
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
to
\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{X}}_{0}
. For (1), it remains to establish an isomorphism
\operatorname{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1})\simeq_{G}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}]
.
Since
\mathrm{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1})
is a filtered quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
, there is an isomorphism of
G
-representations

(7.8.1)

\operatorname{Dix}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1})\simeq_{G}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}].

The variety

\Spec(\mathbb{C}[G\times^{P}(\{\xi\}\times\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})])
is normal and, since it admits a finite
G
-equivariant map to a coadjoint orbit closure, contains finitely many orbits, all of even dimension. Since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}
is an open orbit, we see that
\mathbb{C}[G\times^{P}(\{\xi\}\times\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})]=\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}]
. The left hand side is a filtered Poisson deformation of
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
.
So
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{1}]\simeq_{G}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
. Combined with (
7.8.1
) this completes the proof of (1).
∎

8.
Infinitesimal characters of unipotent ideals

Let
G
be a connected reductive algebraic group and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Choose
\lambda\in\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{\widetilde{X}}
and consider the corresponding ideal
I_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. By Proposition
6.1.2
(i),
I_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is primitive. Define

\gamma_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}):=\text{infinitesimal character of }I_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W.

In this section, we will outline a general strategy for computing the
unipotent infinitesimal character

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. It is based on the computation of the map
\eta
in
Section
7
. This strategy is implemented in Section
8.2
in the case of linear classical groups. In Sections
8.3
and
8.4
, we discuss the applicability of this strategy for spin and exceptional groups.

8.1.
Unipotent infinitesimal characters

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Choose a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a birationally rigid
L
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
. Let
M\subset G
be a Levi subgroup containing
L
and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}=\mathrm{Bind}^{M}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
. Suppose
\gamma
is an infinitesimal character for
\mathfrak{m}
, regarded as an element of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W_{M}
. Since
W_{M}
is a subgroup of
W
,
\gamma
defines an element in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W
, and hence an infinitesimal character for
\mathfrak{g}
.

Proposition 8.1.1
.

Let
\beta\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
. There is an equality in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W

\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}).

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
7.3.2
we can assume that
G
is semisimple. Choose a parabolic subgroup
Q\subset G
with Levi subgroup
M
and a Borel subgroup
B\subset G
contained in
Q
. The Borel subgroup
B
determines a system of positive roots in
M
and in
G
. Let
\rho(\mathfrak{m})
denote the half-sum of the positive roots in
\mathfrak{m}
and choose an anti-dominant representative for the infinitesimal character
\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
. Consider the sheaf
\mathfrak{D}_{Q/B}^{\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})+\rho(\mathfrak{m})}
of twisted differential operators on
Q/B
with twist
\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})+\rho(\mathfrak{m})
. Taking global sections, we get an algebra isomorphism

\Gamma(Q/B,\mathfrak{D}_{Q/B}^{\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})+\rho(\mathfrak{m})})\simeq U(\mathfrak{m})/(I_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})\cap\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{m}))=:\mathcal{U}_{M},

see
[
HTT08
, Theorem 11.2.2]
. Furthermore,
\Phi_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}_{M}}:U(\mathfrak{m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}_{M}}
factors through an algebra homomorphism
\Phi_{M}:\mathcal{U}_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}_{M}}
. We can repeat the construction of
Section

7.3
to produce an algebra
\Ind_{M}^{G}\mathcal{U}_{M}
.
By Proposition
7.3.3
applied to
\widetilde{X}_{M}:=\mathcal{N}_{M}
, we have

\Ind_{M}^{G}\mathcal{U}_{M}\simeq\Gamma(G/B,\mathfrak{D}_{G/B}^{\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})+\rho(\mathfrak{g})}),

Let
\Psi:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{U}_{M}
denote the quantum co-moment map. Note that
\ker{\Psi}
has infinitesimal character
\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
, see again
[
HTT08
, Theorem 11.2.2]
. Furthermore,
\Phi_{M}
gives rise to an algebra homomorphism
\Phi:\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{U}_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}_{M}}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}}
, where the isomorphism
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}_{M}}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}}
follows from
7.3.3
. By Lemma
4.11.2
,
\Phi_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}}=\Phi\circ\Psi
. In particular,
\ker{\Phi_{\beta}^{\widetilde{X}}}=I_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
has infinitesimal character
\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
.
∎

Remark 8.1.2
.

There is a classical notion of parabolic induction for 2-sided ideals in enveloping algebras (see e.g.
[
Dix74
, Chapter 5]
). It is not difficult to show that
I_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
coincides with induced ideal
\Ind^{G}_{M}I_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
. Since parabolic induction preserves infinitesimal character, this is a stronger version of Proposition
8.1.1
. Since we will not use this fact, we omit the proof.

Now choose a Levi subgroup
K\subset L
and a birationally rigid
K
-orbit such that
\mathbb{O}_{L}=\Ind^{L}_{K}\mathbb{O}_{K}
(the induction does not need to be birational). Let
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L}=\mathrm{Bind}^{L}_{K}\mathbb{O}_{K}
and
\widecheck{X}_{L}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L}])
. Note that the universal
L
-equivariant cover
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
of
\mathbb{O}_{L}
is a Galois cover of
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
. By Corollary
7.6.1
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is 2-leafless, and thus
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is 2-leafless by Lemma
7.6.6
. Consider the barycenter parameter
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{P}^{\widecheck{X}_{L}}
(for the Galois cover
\widehat{X}_{L}\twoheadrightarrow\widecheck{X}_{L}
) defined in Example
5.3.2
and let
\delta=\eta^{-1}(\epsilon)\in\mathfrak{X}([\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}]\cap\mathfrak{k})
. We can view
\delta
as an element of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{k})
via the direct sum decomposition
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{k})=\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})\oplus\mathfrak{X}([\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}]\cap\mathfrak{k})
.

Suppose
\gamma
is an infinitesimal character for
\mathfrak{k}
, regarded as an element of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W_{K}
. Then the infinitesimal character
\gamma+\delta\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W
is defined in the usual way: choose a representative for
\gamma
in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and form the sum
\gamma+\delta\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. Since
W_{K}\subset W
and
\delta
is a fixed point for the
W_{K}
-action,
\gamma+\delta
is well-defined modulo
W
, and independent of the choice of representative. The unipotent infinitesimal character
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
can now be computed as follows.

Proposition 8.1.3
.

There is an equality in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W

(8.1.1)

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\delta.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.1.1
, there is an equality

(8.1.2)

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}).

Note that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is equivalent to
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
, since both covers are 2-leafless. By Proposition
6.5.4
,

I_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
, and therefore
\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
. Let
\Pi=\pi_{1}^{L}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
. By Proposition
5.3.1
, there is an isomorphism of Hamiltonian quantizations
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}^{\widecheck{X}_{L}}\simeq(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widehat{X}_{L}})^{\Pi}
. Hence,

I_{\delta}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=I_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}),

and therefore

(8.1.3)

\gamma_{\delta}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}).

By Proposition
8.1.1

(8.1.4)

\gamma_{\delta}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=\gamma_{\delta}(\mathbb{O}_{K})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\delta.

Combining (
8.1.2
), (
8.1.3
) and (
8.1.4
) gives
(
8.1.1
).
∎

Using Proposition
8.1.3
, we can reduce the calculation of
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
to the case of
rigid
nilpotent orbits. For such orbits, we will appeal to the following proposition.

Proposition 8.1.4
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}
is simple. Let
\mathbb{O}
be a birationally rigid nilpotent orbit, which is not one of the following:

\mathfrak{g}

G_{2}

F_{4}

E_{7}

E_{8}

E_{8}

E_{8}

\mathbb{O}

\widetilde{A}_{1}

\widetilde{A}_{2}+A_{1}

(A_{1}+A_{3})^{\prime}

A_{3}+A_{1}

D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{2}

A_{5}+A_{1}

Then there is a unique primitive ideal
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
such that

(i)

V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
.

(ii)

m_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I)=1
.

Furthermore,
I=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.1.2
,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
satisfies (i) and (ii) above. The uniqueness claim is
[
Los18
, Lemma 4.6]
.
∎

Remark 8.1.5
.

There is a geometric characterization of the six orbits appearing in Proposition
8.1.4
: they are exactly the rigid orbits
\mathbb{O}
such that
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
is not normal in codimension
2
. This can be deduced from the incidence tables in
[
FJLS15
]
.

8.2.
Unipotent infinitesimal characters: linear classical groups

Let
G
be linear classical. In this section, we will compute the unipotent infinitesimal characters attached to
G
-equivariant nilpotent covers. We will follow the recipe outlined in Section
8.1
.

8.2.1.
Birationally rigid orbits

First, assume
\mathbb{O}
is a birationally rigid orbit. We will compute
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})
using Proposition
8.1.4
and the results of McGovern (
[
McG94
]
). It will be convenient to define some combinatorial operations.

Definition 8.2.1
.

Suppose
q
is a partition of
n
. Define
\rho(q)\in(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z})^{n}
by appending

(\frac{q_{i}-1}{2},\frac{q_{i}-3}{2},...,\frac{3-q_{i}}{2},\frac{1-q_{i}}{2})

for each
i\geq 1
.

Define
\rho^{+}(q)\in(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}
by appending the
positive
elements of the sequence

(\frac{q_{i}-1}{2},\frac{q_{i}-3}{2},...,\frac{3-q_{i}}{2},\frac{1-q_{i}}{2})

for each
i\geq 1
, and then
0
’s as needed so that
|\rho^{+}(q)|=\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
.

For example, if
q=(4,3^{2},1)
, then

\rho(q)=(\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{3}{2},1,0,-1,1,0,-1,0)\quad\text{and}\quad\rho^{+}(q)=(\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},1,1,0).

Definition 8.2.2
.

Suppose
q
is a partition. Define
f_{B}(q)
as follows: for every odd
i
with
q_{i}\geq q_{i+1}+2
, move one box down from
q_{i}
to
q_{i+1}
. Define
f_{C}(q)
as follows: for every even
i
with
q_{i}\geq q_{i+1}+2
, move one box from
q_{i}
to
q_{i+1}
and then add a single box to
q_{1}
. If
q=(0)
is the trivial partition, define
f_{C}(q)=(1)
.

For example, if
q=(7^{2},4^{3},2,1^{2})
, then

f_{B}(q)=(7^{2},4^{2},3^{2},1^{2})\quad\text{and}\quad f_{C}(q)=(8,6,5,4^{2},2,1^{2}).

Proposition 8.2.3
.

Suppose
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a birationally rigid nilpotent orbit corresponding to a partition
p
. Then
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is a maximal ideal, and
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is as follows

(i)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
, then
\mathbb{O}=\{0\}
and
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})=\rho
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(n)
, then

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})=\rho^{+}(f_{B}(p^{t})).

(iii)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, then

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})=\rho^{+}(f_{C}(p^{t})).

Proof.

We will show that for each infinitesimal character
\gamma
listed above, the maximal ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma)
satisfies the properties

(8.2.1)

V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))=\overline{\mathbb{O}},\qquad m_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))=1.

Then by Proposition
8.1.4
, we have
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma)=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

If
\gamma=\rho
, then
I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma)
is the augmentation ideal, and (
8.2.1
) is trivial. Thus, we can assume
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(n)
or
\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
.

In
[
McG94
, Sec 4]
, McGovern attaches a finite collection of infinitesimal characters
Q(\mathbb{O})
to every nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. For each
\gamma\in Q(\mathbb{O})
, he proves that

V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))=\overline{\mathbb{O}}

(see
[
McG94
, Thm 5.1]
). For the infinitesimal characters above, it is easy to check that
\gamma\in Q(\mathbb{O})
. For a proof of this containment (and a description of
Q(\mathbb{O})
), we refer the reader to Proposition
B.2.2
. McGovern also gives a formula for
m_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))
, see
[
McG94
, Thm 5.14]
. For the infinitesimal characters above, it is easy to compute that
m_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))=1
. We leave the straightforward details to the reader.
∎

Remark 8.2.4
.

From the formulas above, it is clear that all unipotent infinitesimal characters attached to rigid orbits in classical types are ‘extremal’ in the sense of
[
Bar89
, Def 10.3]
.

Example 8.2.5
.

Let
G=\mathrm{SO}(9)
. Using Proposition
7.6.3
, we see that there are 4 birationally rigid orbits, corresponding to the partitions

(3,2^{2},1^{2}),\ (2^{4},1),\ (2^{2},1^{5}),\ (1^{9}).

The corresponding infinitesimal characters are given in the table below

\mathbb{O}

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})

(3,2^{2},1^{2})

(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})

(2^{4},1)

(2,\frac{3}{2},1,\frac{1}{2})

(2^{2},1^{5})

(\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2},1,\frac{1}{2})

(1^{9})

(\frac{7}{2},\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2})

If
\mathbb{O}
is both birationally rigid and special, then
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})
has an even simpler description.

Lemma 8.2.6
.

Suppose
\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid and special. Then there is a unique nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
satisfying
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})=\mathbb{O}
(defined by
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O})
, for example). Furthermore,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})=I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}})
.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
satisfies
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})=\mathbb{O}
, then by
[
McG94
, Cor. 5.19]

m_{\overline{\mathbb{O}}}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}))=1.

Thus,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})=I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}})
by
8.1.4
. By the Dynkin classification of nilpotent orbits,
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is uniquely determined by
\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}
. Hence,
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is the unique nilpotent
G^{\vee}
-orbit satisfying
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})=\mathbb{O}
.
∎

8.2.2.
Birationally rigid covers

Next, assume
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a birationally rigid cover of
\mathbb{O}
. As usual, let
X=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
. Choose a Levi subgroup
K\subset G
and a birationally rigid nilpotent
K
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{K}
such that
\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{K}\mathbb{O}_{K}
. The possible orbits
\mathbb{O}
and pairs
(K,\mathbb{O}_{K})
are described in Proposition
7.6.10
. Consider the barycenter parameter
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{P}^{X}
and let
\delta=\eta^{-1}(\epsilon)\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{k})
. By Proposition
8.1.3
, there is an equality in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W

(8.2.2)

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\delta.

Since
\mathbb{O}_{K}
is birationally rigid,
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})
can be computed using Proposition
8.2.3
. The element
\delta
was computed in Corollaries
7.7.3
and
7.7.6
. Below, we will give a formula for
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
involving only the partition for
\mathbb{O}
(this is possible since all birationally rigid covers
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
are equivalent, see Proposition
6.5.4
).

Definition 8.2.7
.

Suppose
q
is a partition. Let
x(q)\subset q
be the subpartition consisting of multiplicity-1 parts and let
y(q)\subset q
be the subpartition consisting of multiplicty-2 parts.

Suppose
y
is a partition such that every part has multiplicity 2. Define a partition
g(y)
(of the same size as
y
) by replacing every pair
(y_{i},y_{i})
with
(y_{i}+1,y_{i}-1)
.

For example, if
q=(6^{2},5,3^{3},1)
, then
x(q)=(5,1)
and
y(q)=(6^{2})
. If
y=(5^{2},4^{2},1^{2})
, then
g(y)=(6,5^{2},3,2)
.

Proposition 8.2.8
.

Let
p
be the partition corresponding to
\mathbb{O}
. Form the partitions
x=x(p^{t})
and
y=y(p^{t})
. Then
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is given by the following formulas

(i)

If
G=\mathrm{SL}(n)
, then
p=(d^{m})
and

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=(\frac{n-1}{2d},\frac{n-3}{2d},...,\frac{1-n}{2d})=\frac{\rho}{d}.

(ii)

If
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)
or
\mathrm{SO}(2n)
, then

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{B}(x)),

where
\rho^{+}
and
f_{B}
are as in Definitions
8.2.1
and
8.2.2
, respectively.

(iii)

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, then

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{C}(x)).

Proof.

First, suppose
G=\mathrm{SL}(n)
. By Proposition
8.1.3

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{0}(\{0\})+\delta.

By Corollary
7.7.3
we have

\displaystyle\delta=(\underbrace{\frac{d-1}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-1}{2d}}_{m},\underbrace{\frac{d-3}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-3}{2d}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{1-d}{2d},\ldots,\frac{1-d}{2d}}_{m}).

Combining, we get

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

\displaystyle=(\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m})

\displaystyle+(\underbrace{\frac{d-1}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-1}{2d}}_{m},\underbrace{\frac{d-3}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-3}{2d}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{1-d}{2d},\ldots,\frac{1-d}{2d}}_{m})

which coincides up to permutation with
\rho/d
.

Next, suppose
G=\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
. By Proposition
7.6.10
, we have

K=\prod_{k\in S_{2}(p)}\mathrm{GL}(k)\times\mathrm{Sp}(2n-2|S_{2}(p)|),\qquad\mathbb{O}_{K}=\{0\}\times...\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{O}_{p\#S_{2}(p)}.

By the conditions on
p
in Proposition
7.6.7
, we have
p^{t}=x\cup y
. Note that
i\in y
if and only if
i\in S_{2}(p)
. Hence,
y=S_{2}(p)\cup S_{2}(p)
. On the other hand,
i\in x
if and only if
i
is a column in
p
of multiplicity 1. Hence,
x=(p\#S_{2}(p))^{t}
. Now by Proposition
8.2.3

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})=(\rho(S_{2}(p)),\rho^{+}(f_{C}(x))).

By Corollary
7.7.6

\delta=\frac{1}{2}(\underbrace{1,1,...,1}_{|S_{2}(p)|},\underbrace{0,...,0}_{n-|S_{2}(p)|}).

Hence

(8.2.3)

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\delta=(\rho(S_{2}(p)),\rho^{+}(f_{C}(x)))+(\underbrace{\frac{1}{2},...,\frac{1}{2}}_{|S_{2}(p)|},\underbrace{0,...,0}_{n-|S_{2}(p)|}).

Note that
\rho(S_{2}(p))+(\frac{1}{2},...,\frac{1}{2})
coincides with
\rho^{+}(g(y))
up to permutation and negation of entries. Hence, the right hand side of (
8.2.3
) is
W
-conjguate to
(\rho^{+}(g(y)),\rho^{+}(f_{C}(x)))=\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{C}(x))
. The computation for
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)
is analogous.

If
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n)
and
\mathbb{O}
is not of the form
\mathbb{O}_{(4^{2m},3,1)}
, then by the same argument as above

(8.2.4)

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=(\rho(S_{2}(p)),\rho^{+}(f_{B}(x)))+(\underbrace{\frac{1}{2},...,\frac{1}{2}}_{|S_{2}(p)|},\underbrace{0,...,0}_{n-|S_{2}(p)|}).

By construction, all columns in
p\#S_{2}(p)
are distinct. Hence, all rows in
x=(p\#S_{2}(p))^{t}
are distinct. If
x_{1}
is odd, then
f_{B}(x)_{1}=x_{1}
. Otherwise,
f_{B}(x)_{1}=x_{1}-1
. In either case,
f_{B}(x)
contains at least one odd part, and therefore
\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{B}(x))
contains at least one entry equal to
0
. Hence the
W
-orbit of
\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{B}(x))
consists of all
n
-tuples obtained by arbitrary permutations and sign changes. Arguing as above, we see that
\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{B}(x))
coincides with the right hand side of (
8.2.4
) up to permutations and sign changes.

Finally, suppose
G=\mathrm{SO}(8m+4)
and
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}_{(4^{2m}31)}
. By Proposition
7.6.10
, we have

K=\mathrm{GL}(2m+1)\times\mathrm{GL}(2m+1),\qquad\mathbb{O}_{K}=\{0\}\times\{0\}.

By Corollary
7.7.6
, we have

\delta=\frac{1}{2}(\underbrace{1,...,1}_{2m+1},\underbrace{0,...,0}_{2m+1}).

Hence

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\delta=(\rho(2m+1),\rho(2m+1))+\frac{1}{2}(\underbrace{1,...,1}_{2m+1},\underbrace{0,...,0}_{2m+1}).

This weight contains (exactly) one entry equal to
0
, and is therefore
W
-conjugate to
\rho^{+}(2m+2,2m+1,2m+1,2m)
. On the other hand,
p^{t}=(2m+2,2m+1,2m+1,2m)
and therefore
f_{B}(x)\cup g(y)=(2m+2,2m+1,2m+1,2m)
. Thus,
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\rho^{+}(f_{B}(x)\cup g(y))
, as asserted. This completes the proof.
∎

Example 8.2.9
.

Let
G=\mathrm{SO}(9)
. The orbits admitting birationally rigid covers are given by the partitions

(5,3,1),(3,2^{2},1^{2}),(3,1^{6}),(2^{4},1),(2^{2},1^{5}),(1^{9}).

Only
(5,3,1)
and
(3,1^{6})
are not birationally rigid (see Example
8.2.5
). The orbit
(5,3,1)
admits two (equivalent) birationally rigid covers, of degrees 2 and 4. The orbit
(3,1^{6})
admits a single birationally rigid cover, of degree 2. The corresponding infinitesimal characters are
(1,\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},0)
and
(\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
, respectively.

8.2.3.
Arbitrary covers

Finally, let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be an arbitrary
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Choose a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a birationally rigid
L
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
.

If
G=\mathrm{SL}(n)
, then
L
is of the form

L=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{2})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})),

and

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}=\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{1})}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{2})}\times...\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{t})},

where each
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{i})}
is a birationally rigid
\mathrm{SL}(a_{i})
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Hence by Proposition
8.1.1

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=(\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{1})}),\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{2})}),...,\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{t})}))).

Each
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{SL}(a_{i})})
can be computed using Proposition
8.2.8
.

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
or
\mathrm{SO}(n)
, then
L
is of the form

L=\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})\times G(m),

and

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}=\{0\}\times...\{0\}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G(m)},

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G(m)}
is a birationally rigid
G(m)
-equivariant nilpotent cover. If
m\neq 0
, then

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=(\rho(a_{1}),\rho(a_{2}),...,\rho(a_{t}),\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G(m)})).

Otherwise,
\mathbb{O}
is very even. If
G=\operatorname{Sp}(2n)
or
\operatorname{SO}(2n+1)
, then

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=(\rho(a_{1}),...,\rho(a_{t}))

If
G=\operatorname{SO}(2n)
, then

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=(\rho(a_{1}),...,\rho(a_{t}))\quad\text{or}\quad\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=(\rho(a_{1}),...,\rho(a_{t}))\text{ with last entry negated},

depending on the numeral attached to
\mathbb{O}
.

8.3.
Unipotent infinitesimal characters: spin groups

In this section, we modify the approach outlined in Section
8.2
to compute the unipotent infinitesimal characters for spin-equivariant covers. Let
G=\mathrm{Spin}(n)
, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a (
G
-equivariant) nilpotent cover, maximal in its equivalence class. By Proposition
8.1.1
, the computation of
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
reduces to the case of birationally rigid covers. Thus, we can (and will) assume that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid. In particular, since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is assumed to be maximal,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is the the universal (
G
-equivariant) cover of
\mathbb{O}
, see Lemma
7.6.6
.

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is
\mathrm{SO}(n)
-equivariant, then
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
can be computed using the results of Section
8.2
. So we can also assume that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is
not

\mathrm{SO}(n)
-equivariant. Thus,
\mathbb{O}
is is as described in Proposition
7.6.11
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a 2-fold cover of the universal
\mathrm{SO}(n)
-equivariant cover
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
of
\mathbb{O}
. Let

L=\prod_{i\in S_{4}(p)}\mathrm{GL}(i)\times\mathrm{SO}(n-2|S_{4}(p)|),\qquad\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}=\{0\}\times...\times\{0\}\times\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{p\#S_{4}(p)}.

where
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{p\#S_{4}(p)}
denotes the universal
\mathrm{SO}(n-2|S_{4}(p)|)
-equivariant cover of
\mathbb{O}_{p\#S_{4}(p)}
. By Proposition
7.6.10
,
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is birationally rigid and
\mathrm{Bind}^{\mathrm{SO}(n)}_{L}\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}=\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
8.1.3
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L})+\delta,

where
\delta\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
is the element corresponding to the barycenter parameter
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{X}^{\widehat{X}}
. The element
\delta
was computed in Corollary
7.7.7
:

\displaystyle\delta=(\underbrace{\frac{1}{4},...,\frac{1}{4}}_{|S_{4}(p)|},0,...,0).

Combining, we get

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

\displaystyle=(\rho(S_{4}(p)),\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{p\#S_{4}(p)}))+(\underbrace{\frac{1}{4},...,\frac{1}{4}}_{|S_{4}(p)|},0,...,0).

The infinitesimal character
\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{p\#S_{4}(p)})
, and hence
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
, can be computed using the results of Section
8.2
.

Example 8.3.1
.

Let
G=\mathrm{Spin}(6)
,
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}_{(5,1)}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be the universal cover of
\mathbb{O}
. By Proposition
7.6.11
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid. We have

S_{4}(p)=(1),\qquad p\#S_{4}(p)=(3,1).

Hence,

L=\mathrm{GL}(1)\times\mathrm{SO}(4),\qquad\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}=\{0\}\times\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{(3,1)}.

By Proposition
8.2.8
, we have
\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{(3,1)})=(\frac{1}{2},0)
. So

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L})+\delta=(0,\frac{1}{2},0)+(\frac{1}{4},0,0)=(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2},0),

which is
W
-conjugate to
\rho/4
. Note that there is an isomorphism
\mathrm{Spin}(6)\simeq\mathrm{SL}(4)
. And indeed, the unipotent infinitesimal character attached to the universal cover of the principal orbit in
\mathfrak{sl}(4)
is
\rho/4
, see Proposition
8.2.8
(i).

8.4.
Unipotent infinitesimal characters: exceptional groups

Let
G
be a simple exceptional group, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Following the recipe outlined in Section
8.1
one can, in principle, compute the infinitesimal character of
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. Here, we will sketch the procedure and give some examples. In
[
MM23
]
, we carry out this procedure in all cases to produce a complete list of unipotent infinitesimal characters in exceptional types.

(1)

Find a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a birationally rigid cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
.

(2)

Find a Levi subgroup
K\subset L
and a rigid orbit
\mathbb{O}_{K}
such that
\mathbb{O}_{L}=\Ind^{L}_{K}\mathbb{O}_{K}
(for this, one can use the results of
[
DE09
]
).

(3)

Compute
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})
. For the six ‘bad’ orbits in Proposition
8.1.4
, this computation is carried out in Appendix
C
. In all other cases, there is a unique multiplicity 1 primitive ideal with
V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{K}
, computed by Premet in
[
Pre13
, Secs 3-5]
, which must coincide with
I_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})
, see Proposition
8.1.4
. For the reader’s convenience, a full list of unipotent infinitesimal characters attached to rigid orbits in exceptional types is provided in Table
1
after Appendix
C
.

(4)

Compute the barycenter parameter
\delta\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{k})
. Except in 4 cases, all dimension 2 singularities in
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}_{L}])
are of type
A_{1}
(see Proposition
7.6.16
), and hence
\delta
can be computed as in Example
7.7.9
.

(5)

Use Proposition
8.1.3
and steps (3) and (4) to compute
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\delta
.

Example 8.4.1
.

Let
G=G_{2}
and let
\mathbb{O}=G_{2}(a_{1})
. In this example, we will compute the unipotent infinitesimal characters associated to every cover of
\mathbb{O}
.

Since
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})\simeq S_{3}
,
\mathbb{O}
admits 3 non-isomorphic covers (apart from
\mathbb{O}
itself):

•

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2}
: Galois 2-fold cover,
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2})\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{3}
.

•

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3}
: non-Galois 3-fold cover,
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3})\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

•

\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
: Galois 6-fold (universal) cover,
\pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})\simeq 1
.

Recall from Example
7.7.8
that there is a unique codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{1}\subset\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
and the corresponding singularity is of type
A_{1}
. This
A_{1}
singularity cannot be smoothened under the 3-fold cover
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
. Hence,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3}
is birationally induced. Apart from
T
and
G
, there are two non-conjugate Levi subgroups of
G
, corresponding to
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
. Denote them by
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, respectively. As explained in Example
7.7.8
,
\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L_{2}}\{0\}
. So
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L_{1}}\{0\}
. The remaining two covers of
\mathbb{O}
, namely
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
, are birationally rigid, and hence equivalent.

We first compute the infinitesimal characters
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})
and
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3})
. By Proposition
8.1.1
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathfrak{l}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3})

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathfrak{l}_{1})=\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{1}

So that our answers are consistent with
atlas
conventions, we will write these expressions in the basis
\{\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2}\}
of fundamental weights (see, e.g.,
[
Bou02
, Plates I-IX]
for the change-of-basis matrix)

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3})

\displaystyle=\varpi_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{2}

In these coordinates, it is clear that neither weight is dominant. We use
atlas
to compute their dominant representatives

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})=\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{2},\qquad\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3})=\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{1}

For
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2}
or
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
, we will compute
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
using Proposition
8.1.3
. By equation (
7.7.12
) of Example
7.7.8
,

\delta=\eta_{1}^{-1}(\epsilon_{1})=\eta_{1}^{-1}(\frac{1}{2}\omega_{1}(1))=\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{1}.

Now by Proposition
8.1.3

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{L_{2}})+\delta=\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{1}=\varpi_{1}.

We record all of these computations in the table below.

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}

(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})

(K,\mathbb{O}_{K})

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})

\delta

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

\mathbb{O}

(L_{2},\{0\})

(L_{2},\{0\})

\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}

0

\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{2}

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2}

(G_{2},\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2})

(L_{2},\{0\})

\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}

\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{1}

\varpi_{1}

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3}

(L_{1},\{0\})

(L_{1},\{0\})

\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{1}

0

\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{1}

\widehat{\mathbb{O}}

(G_{2},\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2})

(L_{2},\{0\})

\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}

\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{1}

\varpi_{1}

We note that all three infinitesimal characters
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
are special unipotent (cf. Definition
1.2.1
). This is not an accident (see Section
9
for a general statement and explanation).

If
\mathbb{O}_{L}
is one of the 4 exceptions listed in Proposition
7.6.16
, then Proposition
7.7.4
is not, on its own, sufficient for computing the barycenter parameter
\delta\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{k})
. In these cases, one can apply (a modification of) Proposition
7.7.1
, as illustrated below.

Example 8.4.2
.

Let
G=E_{8}
,
\mathbb{O}=E_{8}(b_{6})
, and let
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
be the universal (6-fold)
G
-equivariant cover. We will compute the unipotent infinitesimal character
\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})
.

Label the simple roots as follows

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{4}
\alpha_{5}
\alpha_{6}
\alpha_{7}
\alpha_{8}

and write
\varpi_{1},...,\varpi_{8}
for the corresponding fundamental weights. By Proposition
7.6.16
,
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid. Hence,
(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})=(G,\widehat{\mathbb{O}})
. On the other hand,
\mathbb{O}
is birationally induced from
(K,\mathbb{O}_{K})=(A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1},\{0\})
. Choose
K
corresponding to the simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}
. Then

(8.4.1)

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})=\rho(\mathfrak{k})=\alpha_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\frac{3}{2}\alpha_{5}+2\alpha_{6}+\frac{3}{2}\alpha_{7}.

Converting once again into fundamental weight coordinates, we get

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})=\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}+\varpi_{3}-3\varpi_{4}+\varpi_{5}+\varpi_{6}+\varpi_{7}-\frac{3}{2}\varpi_{8}.

By equations (
7.7.14
) and (
7.7.15
) of Example
7.7.10

\eta_{1}^{-1}(\epsilon_{1})=\frac{1}{2}\eta_{1}^{-1}(\omega_{1}(1))=\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{8},\qquad\eta_{2}^{-1}(\epsilon_{2})=\frac{1}{3}\eta_{2}^{-1}(\omega_{1}(2)+\omega_{2}(2))=\frac{1}{3}\varpi_{4}-\varpi_{8}.

Now by Proposition
8.1.3

\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\delta=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}_{K})+\eta_{1}^{-1}(\epsilon_{1})+\eta_{2}^{-1}(\epsilon_{2})=\varpi_{1}+\varpi_{2}+\varpi_{3}-\frac{8}{3}\varpi_{4}+\varpi_{5}+\varpi_{6}+\varpi_{7}-2\varpi_{8}.

Conjugating by
W
to obtain a dominant representative

\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})=\frac{1}{3}\varpi_{1}+\frac{1}{3}\varpi_{2}+\frac{1}{3}\varpi_{6}+\frac{1}{3}\varpi_{8}

As a sanity check, we conclude by showing that the unipotent ideal
I_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})
is maximal. We will do so using Proposition
3.3.1
. Fix the notation of Section
8.5
. Using
atlas
we compute reductive subgroups
L^{\vee}_{\gamma}
and
L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}
of
G^{\vee}
. We see that
L^{\vee}_{\gamma}
is of type
E_{6}+A_{2}
and
L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}
is of type
A_{3}+A_{1}
, embedded in the
E_{6}
factor of
L^{\vee}_{\gamma}
. Thus,
\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee}=\Ind^{E_{6}+A_{2}}_{A_{1}+A_{3}}=D_{4}(a_{1})\times(3)
, see
[
DE09
, Sec 3]
and
\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee})=D_{4}(a_{1})\times\{0\}
. Now it is easy to see that

\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})=20=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})

Thus
I_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})
is maximal by Proposition
3.3.1
.

8.5.
Maximality of unipotent ideals

In Appendix
B
, we prove the following result.

Theorem 8.5.1
.

Suppose that
G
is linear classical, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a maximal ideal.

The idea of the proof is as follows. Let
\gamma:=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
and define subgroups

L_{\gamma,0}^{\vee}\subset L_{\gamma}^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}

as in Section
3.3
. Let

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}:=\mathsf{D}(\Ind^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}}\{0\}),

a nilpotent orbit for
L_{\gamma}
, the dual group of
L_{\gamma}^{\vee}
. By Proposition
3.3.1
,
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is maximal if and only if

\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})

Checking this condition amounts to some standard (but tedious) casework, which is carried out in Sections
B.1
-
B.4
.

In
[
MM23
]
, we extend Theorem
8.5.1
to arbitrary
G
.

9.
A refinement of BVLS duality

Let
G
be a connected reductive algebraic group. Recall from Section
3.2
there is an order-reversing map

\mathsf{D}:\{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset\mathcal{N}^{\vee}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\mathbb{O}\subset\mathcal{N}\},\qquad\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}):=\text{open orbit in }V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}})).

called BVLS duality. In this section, for each nilpotent
G^{\vee}
-orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
, we will construct an equivalence class of covers
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
of
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
such that

I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}))=I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}).

In particular, this shows that every special unipotent ideal is unipotent.

The assignment
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
defines an injective map

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}:\{\text{nilpotent orbits for }G^{\vee}\}\hookrightarrow\{\text{equivalence classes of nilpotent covers for }G\}

which we call
refined BVLS duality
. This map is constructed in Sections
9.1
-
9.2
. Some motivation for our construction is provided in Section
9.3
.

9.1.
Distinguished orbits and their duals

Proposition 9.1.1
.

Let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
be a distinguished nilpotent
G^{\vee}
-orbit. Let
\mathbb{O}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
and
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
the universal
G
-equivariant cover. Then

(i)

\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid.

(ii)

\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}=\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})
.

Our proof of this proposition is a case-by-case computation, and will occupy the remainder of this section.

Classical types

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
, then Proposition
9.1.1
is trivial—only the principal orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is distinguished, its dual orbit is
\mathbb{O}:=\{0\}
, and

\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}=\rho=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O}).

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1),\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
or
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, and let
q
be the partition corresponding to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
. By Corollary
2.6.6
,
q
is of the following form

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, and

q=(2k_{1},2k_{2},...,2k_{m}),\qquad k_{1}>k_{2}>...>k_{m}>0.

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
, and

q=(2k_{1}-1,2k_{2}-1,...,2k_{m}-1),\qquad k_{1}>k_{2}>...>k_{m}>0,\ m\text{ is odd}.

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, and

q=(2k_{1}-1,2k_{2}-1,...,2k_{m}-1),\qquad k_{1}>k_{2}>...>k_{m}>0,\ m\text{ is even}.

Let
p
be the partition corresponding to
\mathbb{O}
. By Proposition
3.2.4
,

(9.1.1)

p=((m+1)^{a_{m+1}},m^{a_{m}},...,1^{a_{1}}),

where the multiplicities
a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{m}
are as follows.

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
and
m
is odd, then

a_{i}=\begin{cases}0&i=m+1\\
2k_{m}+1&i=m\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})+2&i\text{ odd, }i\neq m\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})-2&i\text{ even, }i\neq m+1.\\
\end{cases}

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
and
m
is even, then

a_{i}=\begin{cases}1&i=m+1\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})+2&i\text{ odd, }i\neq m+1\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})-2&i\text{ even}.\\
\end{cases}

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, then

a_{i}=\begin{cases}0&i=m+1\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})-2&i\text{ odd}\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})+2&i\text{ even, }i\neq m+1.\\
\end{cases}

•

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, then

a_{i}=\begin{cases}0&i=m+1\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})+2&i\text{ odd, }i\neq m+1\\
2(k_{i}-k_{i+1})-2&i\text{ even}.\\
\end{cases}

We leave the (straightforward) verification to the reader. By Corollary
7.6.1
it is enough to show that

(a)

\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is
2
-leafless;

(b)

H^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})=0
.

For (a) by
Lemma

7.6.6
we may assume that
G
is adjoint group, and let
G^{cl}
be the corresponding classical group (i.e.
SO(n)
or
Sp(n)
). One can check using
[
CM93
, Cor 6.1.6]
that in each case
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})\simeq\pi_{1}^{G^{cl}}(\mathbb{O})
. Proposition
7.6.7
implies (a). To prove (b) note that for orbits
\mathbb{O}
in classical types, the reductive part
\mathfrak{r}
of the centralizer of
e\in\mathbb{O}
is given in
[
CM93
, Thm 6.1.3]
. If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
or
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
(resp.
\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
), then
\mathfrak{r}
is semisimple if and only if the corresponding partition contains no odd (resp. even) parts of multiplicity 2. For the partitions above, it is easy to check that this condition is satisfied. Thus,
H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})=0
by Lemma
7.2.7
. This completes the proof of (i) in classical types.

We now proceed to proving (ii). First, assume
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
. Let
x=x(p^{t})
and
y=y(p^{t})
, cf. Definition
8.2.7
. Then by Proposition
8.2.3

(9.1.2)

\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})=\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{C}(x)),

where
f_{C}(x)
and
g(y)
are the partitions constructed in Definitions
8.2.2
and
8.2.7
and
\rho^{+}
is the infinitesimal character of Definition
8.2.1
. The weight
\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}
can be determined from the explicit triples in
[
CM93
, Chp 5.2]
. For
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, the conclusion is as follows

(9.1.3)

\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}=\rho^{+}(q).

Thus, comparing (
9.1.2
) and (
9.1.3
), it suffices to show that
g(y)\cup f_{C}(x)=q
. By (
9.1.1
), we have

p^{t}=(\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i},\sum_{i\geq 2}a_{i},...,\sum_{i\geq m}a_{i}).

Using the formulas above for
a_{1},...,a_{m}
, we can rewrite this in terms of
k_{1},...,k_{m}

p^{t}=(2k_{1}-2,2k_{2},2k_{3}-2,2k_{4},...,2k_{m}-2).

Let
S=\{i\text{ odd}\mid k_{i}=k_{i+1}+1\}
and
T=\{i\text{ odd}\mid k_{i}\geq k_{i+1}+2\}
. Then

y=y(p^{t})=\bigcup_{i\in S}(2k_{i}-2,2k_{i+1}),\qquad x=x(p^{t})=\bigcup_{i\in T}(2k_{i}-2,2k_{i+1}).

Hence by definition

g(y)=\bigcup_{i\in S}(2k_{i}-1,2k_{i+1}-1),\qquad f_{C}(x)=\bigcup_{i\in T}(2k_{i}-1,2k_{i+1}-1),

and therefore

g(y)\cup f_{C}(x)=\bigcup_{i\in S\cup T}(2k_{i}-1,2k_{i+1}-1)=(2k_{1}-1,2k_{2}-1,...,2k_{m}-1)=q

as desired. For
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
or
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, the proof is analogous.

Exceptional types

There are a total of 26 distinguished nilpotent orbits in the simple exceptional Lie algebras. For each
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
, the dual orbit
\mathbb{O}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
is listed in
[
Car93
, Sec 13.4]
. In the majority of cases,
\mathbb{O}
is rigid (see
[
DE09
, Sec 4]
for a complete list of rigid orbits in simple exceptional types). In the table below, we list all such
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
:

In these cases, part (i) of Proposition
9.1.1
is automatic: any
G
-equivariant cover of a rigid nilpotent orbit is birationally rigid. For (ii), we argue as follows. Since
\mathbb{O}
is rigid, we have
\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})
. Then
\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})=\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}
by
[
Pre13
, Thm B]
.

Next, we handle the orbits
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
for which
\mathbb{O}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
is induced. For each such
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
, we verify (i) and (ii) of Proposition
9.1.1
using essentially the same arguments. We will summarize these arguments now before carrying out the casework.

For each
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
with
\mathbb{O}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
induced we record the following information in tables:

•

The dominant weight
\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
. This weight is denoted in fundamental weight coordinates, and is computed using the weighted Dynkin diagram of
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
.

•

The dual orbit
\mathbb{O}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
.

•

The reductive part
\mathfrak{r}
of the centralizer of
e\in\mathbb{O}
, see
[
Car93
, Sec 13.1]
.

•

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})
, see
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
.

•

The Levi subgroup
L:=L_{\mathbb{O}}
corresponding the weighted diagram of
\mathbb{O}
, see (
2.4.1
).

•

The integer
m(\mathbb{O})
, see the remarks preceding Lemma
7.6.14
. This is deducible from
[
DE09
, Sec 4]
.

•

The codimension 2 orbits
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. For each
\mathbb{O}
, we find that there is unique such orbit by inspection of the tables in
[
FJLS15
, Sec 13]
.

•

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
, see
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
.

In each case, we find that
\mathbb{O}
satisfies the following four properties:

(1)

\mathbb{O}
is even.

(2)

X=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
contains a unique codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{1}\subset X
.

(3)

The Kleinian singularity
\Sigma_{1}
is of type
A_{1}
.

(4)

\mathfrak{r}
is semisimple.

Property (1) is immediate from the weighted Dynkin diagram. Properties (2) and (3) are verified using
[
FJLS15
, Sec 13]
. (4) is of course immediate from the determination of
\mathfrak{r}
. Note that (1) implies that
\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{L}\{0\}
, see Proposition
2.4.2
. Properties (2)-(4) imply that
\dim(\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}))=\dim(\mathfrak{P}^{X})=1
and hence that
M_{1}=L
.

In each case, we will show that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid using Proposition
7.6.15
. By that proposition, it is sufficient to exhibit a strict inequality

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}>m(\mathbb{O}).

Once we have determined that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid, we proceed to computing the infinitesimal character
\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})
. By Proposition
8.1.3
, we have

(9.1.4)

\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})=\rho(\mathfrak{l})+\delta,

where
\delta\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
is the weight corresponding to the barycenter parameter
\epsilon\in\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{1}=\mathfrak{P}^{X}
under the natural identification
\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})\simeq\mathfrak{P}^{X}
. To compute this weight, we use Proposition
7.7.4
. In each case, we find that
|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})_{\mathrm{ab}}|=2
. Thus,

c_{1}=2|\Pic(\mathbb{O}_{L})||\Pic(\mathbb{O})|^{-1}=2|\Pic(\{0\})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})_{\mathrm{ab}}|^{-1}=1.

Let
\alpha_{k}
be the simple root
not
contained in
L
and let
\varpi_{k}
be the corresponding fundamental weight. Then by Proposition
7.7.4
, equation (
9.1.4
) becomes

(9.1.5)

\gamma_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})=\rho(\Pi-\{\alpha_{k}\})+\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{k},

where
\rho(\Pi-\{\alpha_{k}\})
is the half-sum of the positive roots in the Levi defined by
\Pi-\{\alpha_{k}\}
. We write this sum in fundamental weight coordinates and compute its dominant
W
-conjugate using
atlas
. In each case, this dominant weight coincides with
\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}
, proving (ii) of Proposition
9.1.1
.

9.2.
Refined BVLS duality

Let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
be a nilpotent
G^{\vee}
-orbit. By
2.4.1
, there is a pair
(L^{\vee},\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{0})
, unique up to conjugation by
G^{\vee}
, consisting of a Levi subgroup
L^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
and a distinguished
L^{\vee}
-orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{0}
, such that

\mathbb{O}^{\vee}=\mathrm{Sat}^{G^{\vee}}_{L^{\vee}}\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{0}.

Let
\mathbb{O}_{0}:=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{0}^{\vee})
. By Proposition
9.1.1
, the universal
L
-equivariant cover
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}_{0}
is birationally rigid. Consider the equivalence class

(9.2.1)

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}):=[\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{0}].

This defines a map

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}:\{\text{nilpotent orbits for }G^{\vee}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{equivalence classes of nilpotent covers for }G\}.

Proposition 9.2.1
.

The map
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}
has the following properties:

(i)

For every
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
,
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
covers
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
.

(ii)

If
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is distinguished, then
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
consists of birationally rigid covers.

(iii)

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}
is injective.

(iv)

For every
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
, there is an equality of ideals

I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})).

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}=\mathrm{Sat}^{G^{\vee}}_{L^{\vee}}\mathbb{O}_{0}^{\vee}
for
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{0}
distinguished. By (
9.2.1
),
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
covers
\Ind^{G}_{L}\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{0}^{\vee})
, and
\Ind^{G}_{L}\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{0}^{\vee})=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
by Proposition
3.2.1
. This proves (i). (ii) is an easy consequence of Proposition
9.1.1
(i). For (iii), first recall that all distinguished orbits are special (distinguished orbits are Richardson by Remark
2.6.3
, and Richardson orbits are special by Proposition
3.2.1
). Thus,
\mathsf{D}
is injective when restricted to distinguished nilpotent orbits. Since
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
covers
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
, the restriction of
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}
to distinguished orbits is injective as well. Now (iii) follows immediately from Proposition
2.4.1
(iii). For (iv), note that

\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}=\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{0}}=\gamma_{0}[\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{0}]=\gamma_{0}[\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{0}]=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})).

The second equality follows from Proposition
9.1.1
, and the third from Proposition
8.1.1
. Thus by Proposition
3.1.1
(ii), there is an inclusion of primitive ideals
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}))\subseteq I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}})
. By Proposition
6.1.2
(i)

V(I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})))=\overline{\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})}=V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}})).

Hence, the inclusion
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}))\subseteq I_{\mathrm{max}}(\frac{1}{2}h_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}})
is an equality by Proposition
3.1.1
(iii).
∎

As an immediate consequence, we obtain the following result (Theorem
1.5.7
from the introduction).

Corollary 9.2.2
.

Every special unipotent bimodule (cf. Definition
1.2.1
) is unipotent (cf. Definition
6.0.2
).

Remark 9.2.3
.

In light of Corollary
9.2.2
, our classification of unipotent bimodules (Theorem
6.6.2
) gives, in particular, a classification of special unipotent bimodules (even in cases when
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is not special and the results of
[
BV85
]
do not apply). The result is as follows. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be the maximal cover in the equivalence class
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
and let
\Pi=\Aut_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. Then there is a natural bijection

\{\text{irreducible representations of }\Pi\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}^{\vee}}^{s}(G).

It is well-known that BVLS duality is order-reversing with respect to the closure ordering on orbits, see Proposition
[
BV85
, Prop A2]
. We conjecture that refined BVLS duality satisfies an analagous property.

Define a partial order on the set of equivalence classes of equivariant nilpotent covers as follows. Write
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{1}]\gg[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2}]
if

•

\mathbb{O}_{1}>\mathbb{O}_{2}
with respect to the closure ordering on orbits, or

•

\mathbb{O}_{1}=\mathbb{O}_{2}
and the maximal cover in
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{2}]
covers the maximal cover in
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{1}]
.

Conjecture 9.2.4
.

The map

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}:\{\text{nilpotent orbits for }G^{\vee}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{equivalence classes of nilpotent covers for }G\}.

is order-reversing.

We can prove this conjecture for classical groups.
The proof amounts to a tedious calculation with partitions, and we do not include it here. In the example below, we verify the conjecture for
G=G_{2}
. We expect similar arguments to work for other exceptional groups.

Example 9.2.5
.

For
G=G_{2}
,
\mathsf{D}
is as follows (see
[
Car93
, Sec 13.4]
).

\mathbb{O}^{\vee}

\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})

0

G_{2}

A_{1}

G_{2}(a_{1})

\widetilde{A}_{1}

G_{2}(a_{1})

G_{2}(a_{1})

G_{2}(a_{1})

G_{2}

\{0\}

Let
\mathbb{O}_{1}^{\vee}=G_{2}(a_{1})
,
\mathbb{O}_{2}^{\vee}=\widetilde{A}_{1}
, and
\mathbb{O}_{3}^{\vee}=A_{1}
. Note that
\mathbb{O}_{1}^{\vee}>\mathbb{O}_{2}^{\vee}>\mathbb{O}_{3}^{\vee}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{1}^{\vee})\ll\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{2}^{\vee})\ll\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{3}^{\vee})
.

Define the Levi subgroups
L_{1},L_{2}\subset G
as in Example
8.4.1
. Since
\mathbb{O}_{1}^{\vee}
is distinguished,
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{1}^{\vee})
contains the universal cover of
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{1}^{\vee})=G_{2}(a_{1})
. On the other hand,
\mathbb{O}_{2}^{\vee}
and
\mathbb{O}_{3}^{\vee}
are saturated from the principal orbits for the short and long root Levis. Thus,

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{2}^{\vee})=[\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{A_{1}}\{0\}],\qquad\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{3}^{\vee})=[\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{\widetilde{A}_{1}}\{0\}],

Using the computations in
Example

8.4.1
we deduce

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{2}^{\vee})=\{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{3}\},\qquad\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{3}^{\vee})=\{\mathbb{O}\}.

In particular,
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{1}^{\vee})\ll\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{2}^{\vee})\ll\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}_{3}^{\vee})
, as asserted.

9.3.
Motivation: symplectic duality

Our construction of refined BVLS duality should be viewed as a special case of a more general (but still largely conjectural) duality known as
symplectic duality
(a closely related duality is known as the
3D mirror symmetry
), see
[
BLPW16a
, Section 10]
. Since there is no rigorous definition of symplectic duality in the cases that concern us, our exposition in this section will be largely speculative.

Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity, cf. Definition
4.3.3
. To
X
one can attach numerous invariants which come in pairs. The most basic example is the pair
(\mathfrak{P}^{X},\mathfrak{t}^{X})
. As usual,
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
is the (complex) Namikawa space, parameterizing the Poisson deformations of a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization
Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
. The second space,
\mathfrak{t}^{X}
, is constructed as follows. Consider the group
H^{X}
of graded Hamiltonian automorphisms of
\mathbb{C}[X]
. This is an algebraic group. Choose a maximal torus
T^{X}\subset H^{X}
, and let
\mathfrak{t}^{X}:=\mathrm{Lie}(T^{X})
.

Example 9.3.1
.

Let
X
be the affinization of a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Then
\mathfrak{P}^{X}\simeq\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
, where
\mathfrak{l}
is a Levi subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
X
is birationally induced from a birationally rigid
L
-equivariant nilpotent cover, see Proposition
7.2.2
. In most cases,
H^{X}=G
(assuming
G
acts faithfully on
X
), and therefore
\mathfrak{t}^{X}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. However, in some cases, even the maximal reductive subgroup of
H^{X}
can be larger, see
[
BK94
, Thm 4]
.

Example 9.3.2
.

Let
e^{\vee}\in\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
be a nilpotent element and choose an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e^{\vee},f^{\vee},h^{\vee})
. For
X^{\vee}
take the intersection of
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
with the Slodowy slice
S^{\vee}
to
e^{\vee}
.
In this case,
Y^{\vee}
is the preimage of
S^{\vee}
under the Springer resolution.
In most cases, we have
\mathfrak{P}^{X^{\vee}}\simeq\mathfrak{h}
, see
[
LNS12
, Theorem 1.3]
, and the group
H^{X^{\vee}}
coincides with
Z_{G^{\vee}}(e^{\vee},h^{\vee},f^{\vee})
. So
\mathfrak{t}^{X^{\vee}}
is identified with connected component of the center of a minimal Levi subalgebra containing
e^{\vee}
.

To a first approximation, symplectic duality is a conjectural duality between conical symplectic singularities. To get a precise correspondence, the conical symplectic singularities, on both sides, should be equipped with certain ‘decorations’, whose precise nature is unclear in the cases that concern us. We will ignore this complication. Write
X^{\vee}
for the symplectic dual of
X
. Some expected properties of symplectic duality are as follows:

•

(X^{\vee})^{\vee}\simeq X
.

•

There are natural isomorphisms

\mathfrak{t}^{X}\simeq\mathfrak{P}^{X^{\vee}},\qquad\mathfrak{P}^{X}\simeq\mathfrak{t}^{X^{\vee}}.

•

The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

T^{X}
acts on
X
with unique fixed point,
0
.

(ii)

X^{\vee}
admits a symplectic resolution (equivalently, all
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalizations are smooth).

Let
Y
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization of
X
. It is expected that the slices to the symplectic leaves in
Y
are the formal neighborhoods of
0
in conical symplectic singularities. Let
X_{1},...,X_{k}
be the conical symplectic singularities corresponding to the
minimal
symplectic leaves. It is also expected that the connected components of the normalization of the
T^{X^{\vee}}
-fixed point locus in
X^{\vee}
are conical symplectic singularities (compare to
[
Los17a
, Section 5.1]
), denoted
X_{1}^{\prime},...,X_{\ell}^{\prime}
. Then a general expectation is that
k=\ell
and (up to permutation of indices),
X_{i}^{\vee}\simeq X_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
. Note that this expectation generalizes the third bullet above.

Now let
X^{\vee}
be as in Example
9.3.2
. It is natural to expect that
X
should have something to do with nilpotent orbits in
\mathfrak{g}
, see, e.g.
[
BLPW16a
, Section 10.4]
. Suppose
e^{\vee}
is distinguished. Then
T^{X^{\vee}}=\{1\}
. Since
\mathfrak{t}^{X^{\vee}}\simeq\mathfrak{P}^{X}
,
X
should have no Poisson deformations. If we assume that
X^{\vee}
is the affinization of a nilpotent cover, this cover should be birationally rigid, see Corollary
7.6.1
. This motivates the first part of Proposition
9.1.1
.

Now consider an arbitrary element

e^{\vee}\in\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
and the corresponding variety
X^{\vee}
. Let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
denote the
G^{\vee}
-orbit of
e^{\vee}
, and
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
be a minimal Levi subalgebra containing
e^{\vee}
. Let
\underline{S}^{\vee}
denote the Slodowy slice to
e^{\vee}
in
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
and let
\underline{X}^{\vee}
be the intersection of
\underline{S}^{\vee}

with the nilpotent cone in
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
. Thus
T^{X^{\vee}}=Z(L^{\vee})^{\circ}
. The fixed point locus of
T^{X^{\vee}}
in
S^{\vee}
is
\underline{S}^{\vee}
. It follows that the fixed point locus of
T^{X^{\vee}}
in
X^{\vee}
is

\underline{X}^{\vee}
.

Now let
X
be the affinization of
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
. We have
\mathfrak{P}^{X}\simeq\mathfrak{t}^{X^{\vee}}
and, in most cases,
\mathfrak{t}^{X}\simeq\mathfrak{P}^{X^{\vee}}\simeq\mathfrak{h}
. The slice to the minimal leaf in
Y
is identified with the symplectic dual of
\underline{X}^{\vee}
. We know that
Y
is of the form
G\times^{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}\times X_{L})
. So the slice in question is
X_{L}
. This discussion motivates the construction of refined BVLS duality in Section
9.2
.

Finally, we speculate on how the special unipotent infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}
relates to symplectic duality. Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity. Write
\mathfrak{P}
for
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
to simplify the notation.
Suppose that
0
is the unique
T^{X}
-fixed point in
X
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}
denote the Rees algebra of
\Gamma(\mathcal{D}^{Y_{\mathrm{univ}}})
, where

\mathcal{D}^{Y_{\mathrm{univ}}}
is the universal quantization of
Y
, see Proposition
4.7.4
.
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}
is a graded algebra over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
, with a Hamiltonian
T^{X}
-action. Choose a generic one-parameter subgroup
\nu:\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow T^{X}
, where ‘generic’ means that
\nu(\mathbb{C}^{\times})
has a unique fixed point in
X
. The choice of
\nu
defines a grading on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}
. Write
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}^{i}
for the
i
th graded component. Now consider the so called
Cartan subquotient
of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}
(called the
B-algebra
in
[
BLPW16a
]
)

\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}):=\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}^{0}/\sum_{i>0}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}^{-i}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}^{i}.

Note that
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar})
has the structure of a graded
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
-algebra. The condition that
0
is the unique
T^{X}
-fixed point in
X
is equivalent to the condition that

\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar})
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
-module. On the other hand, the quantum co-moment map
S(\mathfrak{t}^{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar}
gives rise to a homomorphism
S(\mathfrak{t}^{X})\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar})
. In fact, there is a canonical choice of co-moment map, cf.
[
Los12
, Section 5.4]
. So
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar})
has a distinguished
\mathbb{C}[(\mathfrak{t}^{X})^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
-algebra structure.

One can write down a similar algebra on the
X^{\vee}
-side motivated by a conjecture of Hikita
[
Hik17
]
and its extension due to Nakajima, see
[
KTWWY19
, Conjecture 8.9]
. We call this the
deformed Hikita conjecture
. Consider the equivariant cohomology algebra
H^{*}_{T^{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(X^{\vee})
, where
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a contracting torus. This is an algebra over
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{Lie}(T^{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})]
. For
\hbar
we take
d\in\operatorname{Lie}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, where
-d
is the weight of the Poisson bracket on
X^{\vee}
.

In a related but different setup, Nakajima has conjectured
a graded
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
-algebra isomorphism

(9.3.1)

\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{P},\hbar})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{*}_{T^{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee}).

Do Kien Hoang has informed us that this isomorphism fails in our setting. Nevertheless, we do expect a relation between the two algebras that will make what follows work, for the simplicity of the exposition, we assume that there is an isomorphism.

In general, we do not know how to define a
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}^{X*}]
-algebra structure or a contracting
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on the right hand side of (
9.3.1
). However, both of these issues disappear when
X^{\vee}
is the nilpotent part of a Slodowy slice. Namely, there is a natural contracting
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action (recalled in
Section
3.5
) on
Y^{\vee}
. In the case when
e^{\vee}
is distinguished, this is the unique action such that the weight of the Poisson bracket is
-2
. For
Y^{\vee}
we take the preimage of
S^{\vee}
under the Springer resolution
T^{*}\mathcal{B}^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
. The
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}^{X*}\oplus\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
-algebra structure on
H^{*}_{T^{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee})

comes by pullback from
Y^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
. Namely, there is a pullback homomorphism in equivariant cohomology,
H^{*}_{T^{\vee}_{X}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathcal{B}^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{*}_{T\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee})
, and the source algebra is a quotient of
\mathbb{C}[(\mathfrak{t}^{X})^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
. It is natural to conjecture that (
9.3.1
) is
\mathbb{C}[(\mathfrak{t}^{X})^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{P},\hbar]
-linear.

Let us return to the issue of the special unipotent infinitesimal character. By construction, the homomorphism
\mathbb{C}[(\mathfrak{t}^{X})^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{P},\hbar]\rightarrow H^{*}_{T^{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(X^{\vee})
factors through
H^{*}_{T^{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathcal{B}^{\vee})
, where
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
acts on
\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
via the Kazhdan action. The formula for the Kazhdan action is
t\mapsto t^{-2}t^{h^{\vee}}
, where
t^{h^{\vee}}
denotes the 1-parameter subgroup of
G^{\vee}
corresponding to the element
h^{\vee}
.

Since we are interested in the canonical quantization, we specialize
\mathfrak{P}
to
0
in (
9.3.1
). Thus, we obtain an isomorphism

(9.3.2)

\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\hbar})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{*}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(X^{\vee}).

We see that (
9.3.2
) factors through the algebra
H^{*}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathcal{B}^{\vee})=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t^{\vee}}/W]}\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
, where the homomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}/W]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
comes from the morphism
\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}/W,z\mapsto W(zh^{\vee}/2)
. It follows that the infinitesimal character of the kernel of
U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}
is
\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}
.

Our definition of refined BVLS duality raises the following question: what is the symplectic dual of the affinization of a more general nilpotent cover? This question is further explored in the paper
[
MMY23
]
.

10.
Construction and unitarity of unipotent bimodules

The main goal of this section is to construct all unipotent bimodules for linear classical groups and to deduce that they are unitary. Our approach is as follows. Suppose
G
is linear classical, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. We will construct a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a rigid orbit
\mathbb{O}_{L}
such that all bimodules in
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
are built out of bimodules in
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}_{L}}(L)
via the following operations:

•

Unitary induction.

•

Complementary series.

•

Extraction of direct summands.

The proof of this fact uses the classification of unipotent bimodules from Section
6.6
and a notion of parabolic induction for Harish-Chandra bimodules over Hamiltonian quantizations, which is developed in Section
10.2
.

Since all three operations preserve unitarity, our construction reduces the question of unitarity to the case of rigid nilpotent orbits. For such orbits, the unitarity of
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}}(G)

follows from a result of Barbasch (
[
Bar89
, Prop 10.6]
).
