consists of unitary bimodules.
Then
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
consists of unitary bimodules.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
denote the maximal cover in the equivalence class
[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}]
. By Proposition
6.6.3
, there are category equivalences

\Aut_{\mathbb{O}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\modd\xrightarrow{\sim}\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})),\qquad\Aut_{\mathbb{O}_{M}}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M})\modd\xrightarrow{\sim}\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})).

In particular, the categories
\HC(U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}))
and
\HC(U(\mathfrak{m})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}))
are semisimple. This means that
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}_{M}}
, regarded as an object in
\HC(U(\mathfrak{m})/I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}))
, is a direct sum of bimodules in
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}}(M)
and hence unitary. By Proposition
7.3.3
,
\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}_{M}}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
. So,
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
is unitary by Proposition
10.3.1
(setting
\chi=0
). By Corollary
6.6.3
, the bimodules in
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
are isotypic components in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
, and thus unitary as well.

∎

We will also need the following general fact about birationally rigid orbits.

Lemma 10.6.4
.

Suppose
\mathbb{O}
is a birationally rigid orbit. Then there is a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a rigid orbit
\mathbb{O}_{L}
such that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
, where
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
, and
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}_{L}
are the universal equivariant covers.

Proof.

Step 1
. Choose a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a birationally rigid
L
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
such that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
. By Lemma
2.5.1
, there is a surjective homomorphism
\{1\}=\pi_{1}^{G}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}^{L}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
. Thus,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is the universal
L
-equivariant cover
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
of
\mathbb{O}_{L}
.

Step 2
. Next, we show that
\mathbb{O}_{L}
is 2-leafless, cf. Definition
7.6.2
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\mathbb{O}_{L}
. By Corollary
7.6.1
,
\mathbb{O}
is 2-leafless. Hence,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is 2-leafless, see Lemma
7.6.6
. Suppose
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}\subset\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}_{L}])
is a codimension 2 leaf, and let
\mathbb{O}_{L}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
be the correspondig codimesion 2
L
-orbit, see Lemma
7.5.9
. Note that
\widetilde{Y}:=G\times^{P}(\mathbb{O}_{L}^{\prime}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})\subset G\times^{P}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})=\widetilde{Y}
is a singular subset of codimension 2. Since
\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}
is a Poisson partial resolution and
G\times^{P}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{L}^{\prime}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})
is singular,
\rho(G\times^{P}(\mathbb{O}_{L}^{\prime}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}))
is contained in the singular locus of
\widetilde{X}
. Consider the codimesion 2
G
-orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=\Ind_{L}^{G}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}_{L})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{X}
be the preimage of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
under the cover
\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Since
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is
2
-leafless,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
is contained in
\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. On the other hand, since
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
is induced from
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}_{L}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
intersects nontrivially with
\rho(G\times^{P}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}))
. This is a contradiction.

Step 3
Suppose
\mathbb{O}_{L}=\Ind^{L}_{L^{\prime}}\mathbb{O}_{L^{\prime}}
for a proper Levi subgroup
L^{\prime}\subset L
, and let
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L}=\mathrm{Bind}^{L}_{L^{\prime}}\mathbb{O}_{L^{\prime}}
. Since
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
covers
\mathbb{O}_{L}
and
\mathbb{O}_{L}
is 2-leafless,
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is 2-leafless, see Lemma
7.6.6
. Thus, Corollary
7.6.1
implies that
H^{2}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L},\mathbb{C})\neq 0
, since
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is, by definition, birationally induced. Hence,
\mathfrak{P}^{\widehat{X}_{L}}\neq 0
, since the pullback map
H^{2}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{L},\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L},\mathbb{C})
is a embedding. It follows that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is biratioally induced. This contradicts Step 1.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.6.1
.

Choose a Levi subgroup
K\subset G
and a birationally rigid
K
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{K}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{K}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{K}
. Choose a Levi subgroup
M\subset K
and a birationally rigid orbit
\mathbb{O}_{M}
such that
\mathbb{O}_{K}=\mathrm{Bind}^{K}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
. Finally, choose (using Lemma
10.6.4
) a Levi subgroup
L\subset M
and a rigid orbit
\mathbb{O}_{L}
such that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M}=\mathrm{Bind}^{M}_{L}\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
, where
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
(resp.
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
) is the universal equivariant cover of
\mathbb{O}_{M}
(resp.
\mathbb{O}_{L}
).

By Lemma
10.6.2
, the set
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}_{L}}(L)
consists of unitary bimodules. Hence by Lemma
10.6.3
, applied to
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M}=\mathrm{Bind}^{M}_{L}\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
, the same is true of
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M}}(M)
. Note that
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M}}(M)=\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbb{O}_{M}}(M)
, since
[\widehat{\mathbb{O}}_{M}]=[\mathbb{O}_{M}]
. Hence by Propositions
10.5.1
,
10.5.2
, and
10.5.3
the set
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{K}}(K)
consists of unitary bimodules. Finally, Lemma
10.6.3
, applied to
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{K}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{K}
, shows that
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
consists of unitary bimodules. This completes the proof.
∎

10.7.
Spin and exceptional groups

The strategy outlined in the beginning of Section
10
for proving unitarity has two basic components:

(1)

Show that every unipotent bimodule is obtained from one attached to a rigid nilpotent orbit via unitarity-preserving operations (i.e. parabolic induction, complementary series, and extraction of direct summands).

(2)

Show that every unipotent bimodule attached to a rigid orbit is unitary.

For linear classical groups, this strategy was successfully implemented in Section
10.6
. For (1), we use an exhaustion argument (involving a detailed understanding of fundamental groups and birational induction). For (2), we appeal to an old result of Barbasch.

For spin and exceptional groups, several problems arise. For exceptional groups, the result of Barbasch does not apply at all. In low-rank cases,
atlas
is a reasonable substitute (we have checked, for example, that all unipotent bimodules for
G_{2}
and
F_{4}
are unitary). But for complex groups of types
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
, and
E_{8}
, the unitarity calculations are too big for
atlas
to handle. The unitarity of these bimodules remains a conjecture.

For both spin and exceptional groups, there are issues implementing the reduction step. For the exhaustion arguments in Section
10.4
we assume that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
is abelian. For spin and exceptional groups, this is often not the case. We emphasize, however, that this condition on
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
is an artifact of the proof. It should be possible to weaken this condition (or dispense with it altogether). We do not pursue this issue here.

Appendix A
Coincidence of Inductions for Harish-Chandra Bimodules

In Section
3.4
, we recalled the classical construction of parabolic induction for Harish-Chandra
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodules. In Section
10.2
, we defined parabolic induction for Harish-Chandra bimodules for Hamiltonian quantizations of nilpotent covers. In this appendix, we will show that under certain conditions these two constructions coincide (more precisely, are intertwined by forgetful functors). A similar result was obtained by Vogan in
[
Vog90
]
, see, for example,
[
Vog90
, Corollary 6.16]
. Checking the conditions of Vogan’s result in our setting is difficult, so we will pursue a different approach.

We will need some notation:

•

Let
Q\subset G
be a parabolic subgroup with Levi decomposition
Q=MU
. Let
Q^{-}=MU^{-}
denote the opposite parabolic.

•

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
be an
M
-equivariant nilpotent cover and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\ell}
and
\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
be Hamiltonian quantizations of
\widetilde{X}_{M}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}])
.

•

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}:=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\ell}
and
\mathcal{A}^{r}:=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
, Hamiltonian quantizations of
\widetilde{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
.

•

Let

\Phi^{\ell}:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{\ell},\quad\Phi^{r}:U(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{r},\quad\Phi^{\ell}_{M}:U(\mathfrak{m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\ell},\quad\Phi^{r}_{M}:U(\mathfrak{m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}

denote the quantum co-moment maps.

•

Set

I^{\ell}:=\ker{\Phi^{\ell}},\quad I^{r}:=\ker{\Phi^{r}},\quad I^{\ell}_{M}:=\ker{\Phi^{\ell}_{M}},\quad I^{r}_{M}:=\ker{\Phi^{r}_{M}}.

•

Let
\gamma^{\ell}_{M},\gamma^{r}_{M},\gamma^{\ell},\gamma^{R}_{M}
denote the respective infinitesimal characters.

•

Let

\Phi^{*}:\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A}^{\ell},\mathcal{A}^{r})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g})),\qquad\Phi_{M}^{*}:\HC^{M}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\ell},\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\HC^{M}(U(\mathfrak{m}))

denote the forgetful functors.

•

Let
\mathcal{B}_{M}
be an irreducible object in
\HC^{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\ell}_{M},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
and
\mathcal{B}:=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathcal{B}_{M}\in\HC^{G}(\mathcal{A}^{\ell},\mathcal{A}^{r})
. Note that the existence of
\mathcal{B}_{M}
implies that
\gamma^{\ell}_{M}
and
\gamma^{r}_{M}
differ by a character of
M
.

Proposition A.0.1
.

Suppose that
I^{\ell},I^{r},I^{r}_{M}

are maximal ideals.
Then there is an isomorphism in
\HC^{G}(U(\mathfrak{g}))

\Phi^{*}\mathcal{B}\simeq\Ind^{G}_{M}\Phi_{M}^{*}\mathcal{B}_{M}

The proof scheme is classical, compare to
[
Vog90
]
. We embed the left hand side into the right hand side and show that the
G
-types coincide.

The proof requires some preparation. Recall from Section
10.2
that
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
,
\mathcal{A}^{r}
,
\mathcal{B}
are obtained as the global sections of sheaves on
G/Q
, denoted here by
\mathcal{D}^{\ell}
,
\mathcal{D}^{r}
, and
\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{loc}}
, respectively. Write

\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}
,
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}
, and
\bar{\mathcal{B}}
for the sections of these sheaves over the open Bruhat cell

U^{-}\hookrightarrow G/Q
. Note that
\bar{\mathcal{B}}
is an
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}
-
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}
-bimodule.
There are
Q^{-}
-equivariant decompositions

(A.0.1)

\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}=D(U^{-})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\ell},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}=D(U^{-})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{B}}=D(U^{-})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{M},

where
M
acts
diagonally and
U^{-}
acts on the first factor only. The isomorphisms in (
A.0.1
) are compatible with algebra and bimodule structures.

We will need to define several auxilary categories of bimodules. Let
\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{\ell}},\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{r}}
denote the
central reductions of
U(\mathfrak{g})
corresponding to
\gamma^{\ell},\gamma^{r}
. Choose a one-parameter subgroup
\nu:\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow Z(M)
such that
\nu(t)
acts on
\mathfrak{u}
by positive powers of
t
, and let
h
denote the element of
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{m})
corresponding to
\nu
. Consider the category

\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{\ell}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
consisting of all
\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{\ell}}
-
\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
-bimodules

V
such that

(1)

h
acts diagonalizably on
V
,
and the set of eigenvalues is bounded from above, i.e. there are
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
depending on
V
such that every eigenvalue is of the form
c_{i}-n
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
for some
i
.

(2)

Each
h
-eigenspace is a finite length Harish-Chandra
U(\mathfrak{m})
-bimodule, where the action on the right is via the quantum co-moment map
\Phi_{M}^{r}:U(\mathfrak{m})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
.

Define the categories
\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{r}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),\mathrm{OHC}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),\mathrm{OHC}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
, etc., in a similar fashion. These categories interpolate between a version of category
\mathcal{O}

and the category of Harish-Chandra bimodules (justifying our notation).

Consider the grading on
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}
defined by
\nu
. We can decompose

\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}
as the direct sum of vector spaces
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}_{<0}\oplus\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}_{0}\oplus\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}_{>0}
,
where the subscript
<0
(resp.
>0
) indicates the direct sum of homogeneous components of
negative (resp. positive) degree. It is easy to
see that
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}/\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}_{>0}
is naturally identified with
U(\mathfrak{u}^{-})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
as an
M
-equivariant
\left(U(\mathfrak{u}^{-})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}\right)-\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
-bimodule. Also
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}/\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}_{<0}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}\otimes\mathbb{C}[U^{-}]
, an
M
-equivariant isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
-
(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}\otimes\mathbb{C}[U^{-}])
-bimodules.

Similarly, we can consider the decompositions

\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}=\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}_{<0}\oplus\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}_{0}\oplus\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}_{>0},\qquad U(\mathfrak{g})=U(\mathfrak{g})_{<0}\oplus U(\mathfrak{g})_{0}\oplus U(\mathfrak{g})_{>0}

as well as the induced decompositions for
\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{\ell}},\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{r}}
.
Note that
U(\mathfrak{g})/U(\mathfrak{g})U(\mathfrak{g})_{>0}\simeq U(\mathfrak{q}_{-})
and
U(\mathfrak{g})/U(\mathfrak{g})_{<0}U(\mathfrak{g})\simeq U(\mathfrak{q})
.

We will need the following objects

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}):=(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}/\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}_{>0})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}}\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}\in\mathrm{OHC}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}):=\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}_{\mathcal{A}^{r}_{L}}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}/\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}_{<0}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})\in\mathrm{OHC}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{A},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M}):=(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}/\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}_{>0})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\ell}_{M}}\mathcal{B}_{M}\in\mathrm{OHC}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{A},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M}):=\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\ell}_{M}}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}/\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}_{<0}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell},\mathcal{B}_{M})\in\mathrm{OHC}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}):=(U(\mathfrak{g})/U(\mathfrak{g})U(\mathfrak{g})_{>0})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{m})}\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}\in\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{r}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}):=\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}_{U(\mathfrak{m})}(U(\mathfrak{g})/U(\mathfrak{g})_{<0}U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})\in\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{r}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{U},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M}):=(U(\mathfrak{g})/U(\mathfrak{g})U(\mathfrak{g})_{>0})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{m})}\mathcal{B}_{M}\in\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{\ell}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{U},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M}):=\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}_{U(\mathfrak{m})}(U(\mathfrak{g})/U(\mathfrak{g})_{<0}U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{B}_{M})\in\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma^{\ell}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}).

Here
\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}
denotes the direct sum of Hom’s from graded components as in the definition
of (parabolic) dual Verma modules. It is easy to check that the objects above lie in the specified categories.

Proof of Proposition
A.0.1
.

The proof has several steps.

Step 1
. The composition
U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{r}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}

gives rise to a forgetful functor
\mathrm{OHC}(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})\rightarrow\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{r}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
. Since the homomorphism
U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}

is
M
- and hence
\nu
-equivariant, there are natural
homomorphisms

(A.0.2)

\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),\qquad\nabla^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})\rightarrow\nabla^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})

in

\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{r}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
. Note that
both
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}),\Delta^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
are identified with

U(\mathfrak{u}^{-})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
and, under this identification, (
A.0.2
) is the identity. So

\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Delta^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
. For similar
reasons,
\nabla^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})\xrightarrow{\sim}\nabla^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
, and

\Delta^{\mathcal{U},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Delta^{\mathcal{A},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M}),\nabla^{\mathcal{A},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M})\xrightarrow{\sim}\nabla^{\mathcal{U},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M}).

Step 2
. We can define
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}),\nabla^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})

for every Harish-Chandra
U(\mathfrak{m})
-
\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
-bimodule
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. These are objects in

\mathrm{OHC}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
. They enjoy usual properties of Verma and dual Verma modules including:

(1)

If
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is simple, then
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
has a unique simple quotient,

\nabla^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
has a unique simple sub, and these
simple objects coincide.

(2)

\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})

has finite length.

(3)

\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}),\nabla^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})

are isomorphic as
U(\mathfrak{m})
-
\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
-bimodules (to
U(\mathfrak{u}^{-})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
) and hence have the same
composition series in
\mathrm{OHC}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
.

The proofs are standard.

Step 3
. We claim that
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
is a simple object
in
\mathrm{OHC}(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{r}},\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})
. Assume the contrary. We first note that

I^{r}\otimes U(\mathfrak{m})+U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes I^{r}_{M}
is a maximal ideal in

U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes U(\mathfrak{m})
.
Indeed, it is primitive because it coincides with the kernel of

U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes U(\mathfrak{m})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{r}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
and the target is a quantization of a nilpotent cover. Every such kernel is primitive, see Proposition
6.1.2
. The associated variety of
I^{r}\otimes U(\mathfrak{m})+U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes I^{r}_{M}
has the same dimension as the associated variety of the maximal ideal containing it. This can be deduced from
Proposition
3.3.1
. So

I^{r}\otimes U(\mathfrak{m})+U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes I^{r}_{M}
is indeed maximal.

Thanks to Step 1, the action of
\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{r}}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
on
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
factors through
U(\mathfrak{g})/I^{r}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
. Also recall that thanks to
Lemma
3.5.2
,
the inclusions
U(\mathfrak{g})/I^{r}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{r},U(\mathfrak{m})/I^{r}_{M}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
are isomorphisms.

It makes sense to talk about good filtrations on objects in
\mathrm{OHC}(U(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
.
The object
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
comes with a good filtration induced from the filtration on
\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
.
The associated graded for this filtration is the
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{N}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}_{M}]
-algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{u}^{-}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}_{M}]
, where the module structure is as follows: the homomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{N}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}_{M}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{u}^{-}]
comes from the inclusion
\mathfrak{u}^{-}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}
, while the homomorphism

\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{N}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}_{M}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}_{M}]
comes from the diagonal embedding
\widetilde{X}_{M}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}\times\widetilde{X}_{M}
.
It follows that the associated variety of
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
in
\mathcal{N}\times\widetilde{X}_{M}
is the subvariety
\mathfrak{u}^{-}\times\widetilde{X}_{M}\subset\mathcal{N}\times\widetilde{X}_{M}
. The multiplicity of
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
is equal to
1
.
Recall, see (2) from Step 2, that
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
has finite length.

From our assumption that
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
is not simple, it follows that
there is a simple constituent
S
of
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
whose associated
variety is properly contained in
\mathfrak{u}^{-}\times\widetilde{X}_{M}
. Let
\mathcal{J}

be the annihilator of
S
in
U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M}
. By
[
Los17
, Theorem 1.2]
, the GK dimension of
(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})/\mathcal{J}
is at most
twice that of
S
. On the other hand,
(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})/\mathcal{J}

is a quotient of
U(\mathfrak{g})/I^{r}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
. Since
U(\mathfrak{g})/I^{r}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}(=U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes U(\mathfrak{m}))/(I^{r}\otimes U(\mathfrak{m})+U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes I^{r}_{M}))
is a
simple algebra, it coincides with

(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})/\mathcal{J}
. So the GK dimension of

(U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{r}_{M})/\mathcal{J}
is
2\dim(\mathfrak{n}^{-}\times\widetilde{X}_{L})
,
which is strictly larger than twice that of
S
, a contradiction. So
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
is simple, as asserted.

Step 4
. Combining the conclusion of Step 3 with (1) and (3) of Step 2, we see that
there is an isomorphism
\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})\xrightarrow{\sim}\nabla^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})
.

Step 5
. We now produce an injective
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}
-
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}
-bilinear map

(A.0.3)

\bar{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}}(\nabla^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}),\nabla^{\mathcal{A},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M})).

Recall that we can identify

\bar{\mathcal{B}}
with
D(U^{-})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{M}
,
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\ell}
with
D(U^{-})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\ell}
,
and
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{r}
with
D(U^{-})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}
. Under these identifications,
we have
\nabla^{\mathcal{A},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r})\simeq\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathbb{C}[U^{-}],\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}),\nabla^{\mathcal{A},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M})\simeq\Hom^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathbb{C}[U^{-}],\mathcal{B}_{M})
.
The homomorphism (
A.0.3
) sends
d\otimes b\in D(U^{-})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{M}
to the map

\varphi\mapsto b[\varphi\circ d]
.
This description
shows that (
A.0.3
) is injective.

Step 6
. Note that
\mathcal{B}
embeds into the
\operatorname{ad}(\mathfrak{g})
-finite part

\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}
of
\bar{\mathcal{B}}
. So
\mathcal{B}
also embeds into
the
\operatorname{ad}(\mathfrak{g})
-finite part of the target of (
A.0.3
).
Thanks to Step 4, we get an embedding

(A.0.4)

\mathcal{B}\hookrightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}}^{\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{fin}}(\Delta^{\mathcal{U},r}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{r}),\nabla^{\mathcal{U},\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{M}))

By Lemma
3.4.4
, the target, viewed as a Harish-Chandra
U(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule, coincides with
\operatorname{Ind}^{G}_{M}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
.

Step 7
. We will show that (
A.0.4
) is an
isomorphism. For this it is sufficient to show that the
G
-multiplicities of its source and target coincide. The target is isomorphic as a
G
-representation to

\operatorname{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
, see Proposition
3.4.3
. Recall that
\mathcal{B}=\Gamma(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{loc}})
. The higher cohomology
of this bimodule is a Harish-Chandra
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
-
\mathcal{A}^{r}
-bimodule, supported
away from the locus where the partial resolution
G\times^{Q}(\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\mathfrak{u})\rightarrow\widetilde{X}
is an isomorphism. In particular, the associated variety of the higher cohomology is a proper subvariety in
X\times\widetilde{X}_{M}
. Since

I^{\ell},I^{r}
are assumed to be maximal, this implies that the higher cohomology is
0
. So the
G
-multiplicities of
\mathcal{B}
are equal to the
G
-multiplicities of the Euler characteristic of
R\Gamma(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{loc}})
and thus of
R\Gamma(\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{loc}})
. So it suffices to show that
G
-multiplicities of the complex
R\Gamma(\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{loc}})
are equal to the
G
-multiplicities of
\operatorname{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
. This amounts to showing that for any finite dimensional
M
-representation
V
there is an isomorphism of
G
-representations

(A.0.5)

R\Gamma(G\times^{Q}\mathcal{V})\simeq\operatorname{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}V,

where
\mathcal{V}
is, by definition, the
M
-equivariant sheaf on
\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
obtained by pull back from the sky-scraper sheaf with fiber
V
at
0\in\widetilde{X}_{M}
. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{m})
, let

\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
denote the sheaf on

G\times^{Q}(\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\{\lambda\}\times\mathfrak{u})
defined similarly to
\mathcal{V}
.
The
G
-multiplicities of

R\Gamma(G\times^{Q}\mathcal{V})
are constant under flat deformation. In particular, the
G
-multiplicities of

R\Gamma(G\times^{Q}\mathcal{V}_{\lambda})
are independent of
\lambda
. For a generic
\lambda
, the sheaf
G\times^{Q}\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
coincides with the homogeneous bundle on
G/M
with fiber
V
. There is a
G
-module isomorphism
\Gamma(G\times^{Q}\mathcal{V}_{\lambda})\simeq\operatorname{AlgInd}^{G}_{M}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Maximality calculations

In this appendix, we show that for linear classical groups all unipotent ideals are maximal. We will also prove the maximality of certain auxilary ideals which appear in the deformation arguments in Section
10
. All of these proofs proceed in more or less the same fashion. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Let
I:=I_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
, and let
\gamma:=\gamma_{\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W
(cf. Definition
6.0.1
). By Proposition
6.1.2
,
I
is a primitive ideal with infinitesimal character
\gamma
and associated variety
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. As explained in Section
3.3
,
\gamma
can be used to define reductive subgroups

L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}\subset L^{\vee}_{\gamma}\subset G^{\vee}.

Consider the Richardson orbit
\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee}:=\Ind^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}}\{0\}\subset(\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}^{\vee})^{*}
, and its dual orbit
\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}:=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma})\subset\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}^{*}
. By Proposition
3.3.1
,
I
is maximal if and only if the following condition is satisfied

(B.0.1)

\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}}).

In special cases,
L_{\gamma}
is a Levi subgroup of
G
(for example, this is always the case in type
A
). In these cases, (
B.0.1
) is equivalent to the condition (see Remark
3.3.2
)

\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}.

In practice, the latter condition is often easier to check.

B.1.
Calculations in type A

Suppose
G=\mathrm{SL}(n)
. The following result is used in the proof of Proposition
10.5.1
.

Proposition B.1.1
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

Choose a Levi subgroup
M=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t}))\subset G
and a birationally rigid cover

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}=\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}^{1}\times...\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}^{t}

such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. Write
p^{i}
for the partitions of
a^{i}
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}_{M}^{i}
and write
p
for the partition of
n
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}
. By Proposition
7.6.7
and Remark
7.6.5
, there is a positive integer
d\mid n
and a partition
m
of
\frac{n}{d}
such that
p^{i}=(d^{m_{i}})
for
1\leq i\leq t
. Since
\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
, this implies
p=dm
.

By Propositions
8.1.1
and
8.2.8

\gamma:=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})=(\frac{\rho(a_{1})}{d},\frac{\rho(a_{2})}{d},...,\frac{\rho(a_{t})}{d}).

We will consider separately the cases of odd and even
d
.

d odd:

Let
e
(resp.
o
) denote the transpose of the subpartition of
a
consisting of even (resp. odd) parts. For example, if
a=(15,12^{2},9)
, then
e=(2^{12})
, and
o=(2^{9},1^{6})
. Let
S=\{0,1,...,2d-1\}
and let
S_{e}
(resp.
S_{o}
) denote the set of even (resp. odd) elements of
S
. By an easy computation

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{S}\left(\prod_{x\in S_{o}}\mathrm{GL}\left(\frac{|e|}{d}\right)\times\prod_{x\in S_{e}}\mathrm{GL}\left(\frac{|o|}{d}\right)\right)

and

L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=\mathrm{S}\left(\prod_{x\in S_{o}}\left(\prod_{y\equiv x\text{ mod }d}\mathrm{GL}(e_{y})\right)\times\prod_{x\in S_{e}}\left(\prod_{y\equiv x\text{ mod }d}\mathrm{GL}(o_{y})\right)\right).

Note that
L_{\gamma}\simeq L_{\gamma}^{\vee}
is a Levi subgroup of
G
. Thus it suffices to show that
\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}
.

For each
x\in S_{o}
, let
r_{x}(e)
denote the subpartition of
e
consisting of parts
e_{y}
with
y\equiv x\text{ mod }d
. For example, if
e=(3^{6},2^{6},1^{3})
and
d=3
, then
r_{1}(e)=(3^{2},2^{2},1)
. For each
x\in S_{e}
, define
r_{x}(o)
similarly. Then by Proposition
2.3.2

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee}=\mathrm{Ind}^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}}\{0\}=\prod_{x\in S_{o}}r_{x}(e)^{t}\times\prod_{x\in S_{e}}r_{x}(o)^{t}\subset\mathcal{N}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}},

where we have identified orbits (here and for the remainder of this subsection) with their corresponding partitions. By Proposition
3.2.4

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee})=\prod_{x\in S_{o}}r_{x}(e)\times\prod_{x\in S_{e}}r_{x}(o)\subset\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}},

and therefore

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(|e|)\times\mathrm{GL}(|o|))}e\times o=e+o=p

as desired.

d even:

Let
S=\{0,1,...d-1\}
. Then

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{S}\left(\prod_{x\in S}\mathrm{GL}\left(|m|\right)\right),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=\mathrm{S}\left(\prod_{x\in S}\left(\prod_{y\equiv x\text{ mod }d}\mathrm{GL}((a^{t})_{y})\right)\right).

For each
x\in S
, let
r_{x}(a^{t})
denote the subpartition of
a^{t}
consisting of parts
a^{t}_{y}
with
y\equiv x\text{ mod }d
. Hence

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee}=\mathrm{Ind}^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}}\{0\}=\prod_{x\in S}r_{x}(a^{t})^{t}\subset\mathcal{N}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}},

and therefore

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee}\right)=\prod_{x\in S}r_{x}(a^{t})\subset\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}}.

Now

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}\left(\frac{a}{d}\right)^{t}=p

as desired.

∎

B.1.1.
Auxilary ideals

We now proceed to proving the maximality of certain auxilary ideals attached to birationally rigid covers. These results are used in the proof of Proposition
10.5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{O}
be an orbit corresponding to a partition
p=(d^{m})
of
n
. Recall that the universal
G
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid and
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=I_{\delta}(\mathbb{O})
, where

\delta=(\underbrace{\frac{d-1}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-1}{2d}}_{m},\underbrace{\frac{d-3}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-3}{2d}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{1-d}{2d},\ldots,\frac{1-d}{2d}}_{m}).

To prove the unitarity of the spherical bimodule
U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\delta}(\mathbb{O})\in\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
, we need to prove the maximality not only of
I_{\delta}(\mathbb{O})
, but of all ideals in the one-parameter family
\{I_{t\delta}(\mathbb{O})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
.

Lemma B.1.2
.

For every
t\in[0,1]
,
I_{t\delta}(\mathbb{O})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

If
t\in\{0,1\}
,
I_{t\delta}(\mathbb{O})
is maximal by Proposition
B.1.1
. Assume
t\in(0,1)
. By Proposition
8.1.3
and a direct computation

\displaystyle\gamma:=\gamma_{t\delta}(\mathbb{O})

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathfrak{l})+t\delta

\displaystyle=(\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m},\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m})

\displaystyle+t(\underbrace{\frac{d-1}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-1}{2d}}_{m},\underbrace{\frac{d-3}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-3}{2d}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{1-d}{2d},\ldots,\frac{1-d}{2d}}_{m}).

Hence, the difference between two entries of
\gamma
is of the form
a+t\frac{b}{d}
for nonnegative integers
a\leq m-1
and
b\leq d-1
. The difference is integral if and only if
b=0
and zero if and only if
a=b=0
. Hence,

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(m)^{d}),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=H^{\vee},

where
H^{\vee}
is the (dual) maximal torus. Thus,

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee}=\Ind^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}}\{0\}=(m)\times...\times(m),

and therefore

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}^{\vee})=\{0\}\times...\times\{0\}.

Now

\Ind^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=(d^{m})=p

as desired.
∎

There are
d-1
bimodules in
\mathrm{Unip}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}}(G)
which are distinct from the spherical bimodule
U(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\delta}(\mathbb{O})
. To prove that these bimodules are unitary, we will need to consider a different set of auxilary ideals. Fix
j\in\{1,...,d-1\}
and let

\tau_{j}=(\underbrace{\frac{d-j}{d},...,\frac{d-j}{d}}_{mj},\underbrace{-j,...,-j}_{m(d-j)}).

Consider the weights

\delta_{\ell}(t):=\delta-\tau_{j}+\frac{1}{2}(t-1)(2\delta-\tau_{j}),\qquad\delta_{r}(t):=\delta+\frac{1}{2}(t-1)(2\delta-\tau_{j}).

Lemma B.1.3
.

For every
t\in[0,1]
and
j\in\{1,...,d-1\}
, both
I_{\delta_{\ell}(t)}(\mathbb{O})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
and
I_{\delta_{r}(t)}(\mathbb{O})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
are maximal ideals.

Proof.

We will prove the assertion only for
\delta_{r}(t)
. The proof for
\delta_{\ell}(t)
is analogous. Note that
\delta_{r}(1)=\delta
. Hence,
I_{\delta_{r}(1))}(\mathbb{O})=I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is maximal by Proposition
B.1.1
. On the other extreme,
\delta_{r}(0)=\frac{\tau_{j}}{2}
. By Proposition
8.1.3
and a direct computation

\displaystyle\gamma:=\gamma_{\delta_{r}(0)}

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathfrak{l})+\frac{\tau_{j}}{2}

\displaystyle=(\underbrace{\frac{n-j}{2d},\frac{n-2d-j}{2d},...,\frac{2d-n-j}{2d},\ldots,\frac{n-j}{2d},\frac{n-2d-j}{2d},\ldots,\frac{2d-n-j}{2d}}_{mj},

\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{\frac{n-d-j}{2d},\frac{n-3d-j}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-n-j}{2d},\ldots,\frac{n-d-j}{2d},\frac{n-3d-j}{2d},\ldots,\frac{d-n-j}{2d}}_{m(d-j)}).

Hence,

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(mj)\times\mathrm{GL}(m(d-j))),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(j)^{m}\times\mathrm{GL}(d-j)^{m}).

So,

\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\Ind^{L_{\gamma}^{\vee}}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}}\{0\}=(m^{j})\times(m^{d-j}),

and therefore

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma})=(j^{m})\times((d-j)^{m}).

Now,

\Ind^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=(j^{m})+((d-j)^{m})=(d^{m})=p

as desired.

Next, suppose
t\in(0,1)
. By Proposition
8.1.3
and a direct computation

\displaystyle\gamma_{\delta_{r}(t)}:=\gamma_{\delta_{r}(t)}(\mathbb{O})

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathfrak{l})+(1-t)\frac{\tau_{j}}{2}+t\delta

\displaystyle=(\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m},\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{m-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-m}{2}}_{m})

\displaystyle+(\underbrace{\frac{(1-t)(d-j)}{2d},\ldots,\frac{(1-t)(d-j)}{2d}}_{mj},\underbrace{\frac{-(1-t)j}{2d},...,\frac{-(1-t)j}{2d}}_{m(d-j)})

\displaystyle+(\underbrace{\frac{t(d-1)}{2d},\ldots,\frac{t(d-1)}{2d}}_{m},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{t(1-d)}{2d},\ldots,\frac{t(1-d)}{2d}}_{m}).

The difference between two of the first
mj
entries of
\gamma_{\delta_{r}(t)}
is of the form

a+\frac{tb}{d},\qquad a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\quad|a|\leq m-1\quad|b|\leq j-1.

The difference between two of the last
m(d-j)
entries is of the form

a+\frac{tb}{d},\qquad a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\quad|a|\leq m-1\quad|b|\leq d-j-1.

The difference between one of the first
mj
entries and one of the last
m(d-j)
entries is of the form

a+\frac{tb}{d}+\frac{(1-t)}{2},\qquad a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\quad|a|\leq m-1\quad|b|\leq d-1.

From these formulas we deduce

L^{\vee}_{\gamma_{\delta_{r}(t)}}=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{GL}(m)^{d})\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma_{\delta_{r}(t)},0}=H^{\vee},

where
H^{\vee}
denotes the (dual) maximal torus. Now, proceed as in the proof of Lemma
B.1.2
.
∎

B.2.
Calculations in type C

Suppose
G=\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
. Our main result in this subsection is the following. It is used in the proof of Proposition
10.5.2
.

Proposition B.2.1
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G
-equivariant cover. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

We will prove this proposition by induction (on the number of
\mathrm{GL}
-factors in the Levi subgroup of
G
from which
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally induced). The base case is the following.

Lemma B.2.2
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a birationally rigid
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

Let
p
be the partition of
2n
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}
and let
q:=p^{t}
. By Proposition
7.6.7
,
p
satisfies

•

p_{i}\leq p_{i+1}+2
for all
i
.

•

If
p_{i}
is odd, then
p_{i}\leq p_{i+1}+1
.

Thus,
q
satisfies

•

all parts in
q
occur with multiplicity
\leq 2
.

•

If
i
is even, then
q_{i}\neq q_{i+1}
.

Let
x\subset q
(resp.
y\subset q
) be the subpartition of multiplicity 1 (resp. 2) parts. By Proposition
8.2.8

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})=\rho^{+}(g(y)\cup f_{C}(x))

(the notations
f_{C}(x)
,
g(y)
, and
\rho^{+}
are explained in Definitions
8.2.7
,
8.2.2
, and
8.2.1
). For any partition
r
, let
e(r)
(resp.
l(r)
) denote the partitions obtained from
r
by adding (resp. deleting) a single box from the first (resp. last) part in
r
. In
[
McG94
]
, Mcgovern considers the finite set of infinitesimal characters

Q(\mathbb{O})=\{\rho^{+}(q)\mid C(l(q))=p\},

where
C
denotes the
C
-collapse. By
[
McG94
, Thm 5.1]
),
V(I_{\mathrm{max}}(\gamma))=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
for every
\gamma\in Q(\mathbb{O})
. Thus, it suffices to show

(B.2.1)

C(l(g(y)\cup f_{C}(x))^{t})=p.

We begin by providing an alternative description of the partition
g(y)\cup f_{C}(x)
. First, decompose
q
into consecutive subpartitions (called
blocks
)

q=(\underbrace{q_{1},...q_{l_{1}}}_{b^{1}},\underbrace{q_{l_{1}+1},...,q_{l_{1}+l_{2}+1}}_{b^{2}},...,\underbrace{q_{2n-l_{t}+1},...,q_{2n}}_{b^{s}})

such that

•

For every
i
(
1\leq i\leq s
) and even
j
(
1\leq j\leq l_{i}-1
),
b^{i}_{j}=b^{i}_{j+1}+1
.

•

For every
i
(
1\leq i\leq s-1
),
b^{i}_{l_{i}}\geq b^{i+1}_{1}+2
.

For example, if
p=(6,5^{4},4^{3},2,1^{2})
, then
q=(11,9,8^{2},5,1)
,
b^{1}=(11,9,8^{2})
, and
b^{2}=(5,1)
. Note that if
\#q
is even, then
l_{i}
is even for
1\leq i\leq s
. Otherwise,
l_{i}
is even for
1\leq i\leq s-1
,
l_{s}
is odd, and
b^{s}
contains (an odd number of) parts of multiplicity 1, including the smallest part of
b^{s}
, namely
b^{s}_{l_{s}}
. For
1\leq i\leq s
, define

\widetilde{b}^{i}:=e(l(b^{i})).

Since
b^{i}_{l_{i}}\geq b_{1}^{i+1}+2
, we have
\widetilde{b}_{l_{i}}^{i}\geq\widetilde{b}_{1}^{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq s-1
. So we can define the partition

\widetilde{b}:=(\widetilde{b}^{1},\widetilde{b}^{2},...,\widetilde{b}^{s}).

We will define one final partition
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}
, as follows: if
\#q
is even, define
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}=(\widetilde{b},1)
. Otherwise, define

\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)=(\widetilde{b}^{1},...,\widetilde{b}^{s-1},e(b^{s}))

(i.e. add a single box to the smallest part in
\widetilde{b}
). For example, if we choose
q=(11,9,8^{2},5,1)
as above, then

b^{1}=(11,9,8^{2}),\ b^{2}=(5,1),\qquad\widetilde{b}^{1}=(12,9,8,7),\ \widetilde{b}^{2}=(6),\qquad\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}=(12,9,8,7,6,1).

If, on the other hand,
q=(6,4,2)
, then

b^{1}=(6,4),\ b^{2}=(2),\qquad\widetilde{b}^{1}=(7,3),\ \widetilde{b}^{2}=(2),\qquad\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}=(7,3^{2}).

We will sometimes write
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}^{i}(q)
and
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)
to indicate dependence on
q
. We claim, first of all, that
g(y)\cup f_{C}(x)=\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)
. We proceed by induction on
s
, the number of blocks in
q
. For notational convenience, let
h(q):=f_{C}(x)\cup g(y)
. The base case (namely,
s=1
) is an easy exercise, which we leave to the reader. Now suppose
s\geq 2
and let
q^{\prime}:=(b^{2},...,b^{s})
. If
b^{1}
contains no parts of multiplicity 1, then by definition
h(b^{1})
contains 1 with positive multiplicity. Let
h^{\prime}(b^{1})
be the partition obtained from
h(b^{1})
by deleting one such part. If, on the other hand,
b^{1}
contains at least one part with multiplicity 1, let
b^{1}_{r}
be the maximal such and define
h^{\prime}(b^{1})
be removing one box from
h(b^{1})_{r}
. Then clearly

h(q)=h(b^{1},q^{\prime})=(h^{\prime}(b^{1}),h(q^{\prime})).

By the
s=1
case, discussed above,
h(b^{1})=\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(b^{1})
, and hence
h^{\prime}(b^{1})=\widetilde{b}(b^{1})=\widetilde{b}^{1}
. By induction,
h(q^{\prime})=\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})
. Thus,

(B.2.2)

h(q)=(\widetilde{b}^{1},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime}))=(\widetilde{b}^{1},...,\widetilde{b}^{s-1},e(b^{t}))=\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)

as asserted. Now, comparing (
B.2.1
) and (
B.2.2
), it suffices to show that
C(l((\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q))^{t}))=p
. We will do so, again, by induction on
s
. First, suppose
s=1
. There are two cases to consider. If
\#q
is even, then
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)=(\widetilde{b}(q),1)=(e(l(q)),1)
. Thus,
l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t})
is obtained from
p
by adding a box to the first row and deleting a box from the first column. By the algorithm for the
C
-collapse (see
[
McG94
, Lem 6.3.3]
) it is clear that
C(l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}))=p
. If, on the other hand,
\#q
is odd, then
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)=p
. So again,
C(l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}))=p
.

Now suppose
s\geq 2
. As above, let
q^{\prime}:=(b^{2},...,b^{s})
and recall that

(B.2.3)

\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)=(\widetilde{b}^{1}(q),\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})).

Write
\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime}))
for the number of columns in
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})
(note that
\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime}))=\mathrm{col}(q^{\prime})+1
). Then from (
B.2.3
), we obtain

\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}_{i}=\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})^{t}_{i}+l_{1},\qquad i\leq\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})).

Since
l_{1}
is even, this implies

C(l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}))_{i}=C(l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})^{t}))_{i}+l_{1},\qquad i\leq\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime}))-1.

Hence by the induction hypothesis (applied to
q^{\prime}
) we obtain

C(l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}))_{i}=(q^{\prime})^{t}_{i}+l_{1}=p_{i},\qquad i\leq\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime}))-1.

For
i=\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime}))
, the row
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}_{i}
is odd, appearing with odd multiplicity. Hence,

C(l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}))_{i}=\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}-1=p_{i},\qquad i=\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})).

It remains to show that

(B.2.4)

C(l(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}))_{i}=p_{i},\qquad i>\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})).

But indeed

\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q)^{t}_{i}=(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}^{1})^{t}_{i},\qquad i>\mathrm{col}(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{b}$}}(q^{\prime})).

So (
B.2.4
) follows from the base case of the induction (applied to
q=b^{1}
).
∎

The induction step in our proof of Proposition
B.2.1
will depend on the following definition.

Definition B.2.3
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{r})
and
\nu=(\nu_{1},...,\nu_{s})
be partitions and assume
\mu_{1}
is even. Define the
(|\mu|+|\nu|-\mu_{1})
-tuple

\displaystyle v(\mu,\nu)=(

\displaystyle\underbrace{r-1,...,r-1}_{\mu_{r}},\underbrace{r-2,...,r-2}_{\mu_{r-1}},...,\underbrace{1,...,1}_{\mu_{2}},\underbrace{0,...,0}_{\frac{\mu_{1}}{2}},

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{2s-1}{2},...,\frac{2s-1}{2}}_{\nu_{s}},\underbrace{\frac{2s-3}{2},...,\frac{2s-3}{2}}_{\nu_{s-1}},...,\underbrace{\frac{1}{2},...,\frac{1}{2}}_{\nu_{1}}).

We say that a tuple
v
is
triangular
if there are partitions
\mu
and
\nu
as above such that
v=v(\mu,\nu)
.

Now, choose a Levi subgroup of the form

M=\mathrm{GL}(a)\times G(m)\subset G,\qquad a+m=n.

Let
\gamma^{\prime}=v(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
be a triangular
m
-tuple, and let

\gamma=(\frac{a-1}{2},\frac{a-3}{2},...,\frac{1-a}{2},\gamma^{\prime}).

Regard
\gamma
(resp.
\gamma^{\prime}
) as a weight (in standard coordinates) for
G
(resp.
G(m)
). Form the reductive groups
L_{\gamma}
and
L_{\gamma^{\prime}}
, and the nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}\subset\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma}^{*}
and
\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\subset\mathfrak{l}_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{*}
.

Lemma B.2.4
.

The
n
-tuple
\gamma
is (
W
-conjugate to) a triangular one, and there is an equality

\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma^{\prime}}})+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{GL(a)}).

Proof.

First, suppose
a
is odd. Define partitions
\mu
and
\nu
by

(B.2.5)

\mu_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu^{\prime}_{i}+2&\quad i=1\\
\mu^{\prime}_{i}+1&\quad 2\leq i\leq a\\
\mu^{\prime}_{i}&\quad\text{else}\end{array}\right.\qquad\nu=\nu^{\prime}.

It is easy to see that
\gamma
is
W
-conjugate to
v(\mu,\nu)
. This proves the first part of the proposition.

Define the integers

R^{\prime}=|\mu^{\prime}|-\frac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime}_{1},\qquad R=|\mu|-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1},\qquad S^{\prime}=|\nu^{\prime}|=|\nu|.

Then

L^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime}}=\mathrm{SO}(2R^{\prime}+1)\times\mathrm{SO}(2S^{\prime}),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime},0}=\mathrm{SO}(\mu^{\prime}_{1}+1)\times\prod_{i=2}^{r}\mathrm{GL}(\mu^{\prime}_{i})\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}\mathrm{GL}(\nu^{\prime}_{j}),

and

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{SO}(2R+1)\times\mathrm{SO}(2S^{\prime}),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=\mathrm{SO}(\mu_{1}+1)\times\prod_{i=2}^{r}\mathrm{GL}(\mu_{i})\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}\mathrm{GL}(\nu^{\prime}_{i}).

Hence, by (
B.2.5
) and Proposition
2.6.5

\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\Ind^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}}\{0\}=\mathrm{Sat}^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{GL}(a)\times L^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\left(\mathbb{O}_{\mathrm{GL}(a)}^{\mathrm{prin}}\times\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\vee}\right).

Using Proposition
3.2.1
we obtain

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma})=\mathrm{Ind}^{L_{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{GL}(a)\times L_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\left(\{0\}\times\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}\right).

Since induction preserves codimension (see Proposition
2.3.1
(iii)), this implies

\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma^{\prime}}})+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{GL(a)})

as asserted. Next, suppose
a
is even. Define

(B.2.6)

\mu=\mu^{\prime},\qquad\nu_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\nu^{\prime}_{j}+1&\quad 1\leq j\leq a\\
\nu^{\prime}_{j}&\quad\text{else}.\end{array}\right.

Then
\gamma
is
W
-conjugate to
v(\mu,\nu)
. Set

R^{\prime}=|\mu^{\prime}|-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{\prime}=|\mu|-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1},\qquad S^{\prime}=|\nu^{\prime}|,\qquad S=|\nu|.

Then

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{SO}(2R^{\prime}+1)\times\mathrm{SO}(2S),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=\mathrm{SO}(\mu^{\prime}_{1}+1)\times\prod_{i=2}^{r}\mathrm{GL}(\mu^{\prime}_{i})\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}\mathrm{GL}(\nu_{j}).

So again

\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{Sat}^{L^{\vee}_{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{GL}(a)\times L^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\left(\mathbb{O}_{\mathrm{GL}(a)}^{\mathrm{prin}}\times\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\right).

Now the equality follows exactly as above.
∎

We are now prepared to prove Proposition
B.2.1
.

Proof of Proposition
B.2.1
.

Choose a Levi subgroup
M=\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})\times G(m)
and a birationally rigid cover

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}=\{0\}\times...\{0\}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G(m)}

such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. We will prove the following statement by induction on
t
:

I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})\text{ is maximal, and }\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\text{ is the }W\text{-orbit of a triangular weight.}

If
t=0
, then
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid. Hence
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is maximal by Lemma
B.2.2
. Any weight of the form
\rho^{+}(q)
(for some partition
q
) is triangular. Thus,
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is triangular by Proposition
8.2.8
.

Now suppose
t\geq 1
. Let
G^{\prime}:=G(n-a_{1})
. Consider the Levi subgroup

M^{\prime}=GL(a_{2})\times...\times GL(a_{t})\times G(m)\subset G^{\prime}

and the
M^{\prime}
-equivariant nilpotent cover

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M^{\prime}}:=\{0\}\times...\times\{0\}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G(m)}.

Consider the
G^{\prime}
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G^{\prime}}_{M^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M^{\prime}}
. By the transitivity of induction

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times G^{\prime}}\left(\{0\}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}\right)

and hence (writing
\mathbb{O}
and
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
for the underlying orbits)

\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times G^{\prime}}\left(\{0\}\times\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\right).

For notational convenience, let
\gamma:=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
and
\gamma^{\prime}:=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime})
. By Proposition
8.1.1

\gamma=(\gamma_{0}(\{0\}),\gamma^{\prime})=(\frac{a_{1}-1}{2},\frac{a_{1}-3}{2},...,\frac{1-a_{1}}{2},\gamma^{\prime}).

By the induction hypothesis,
\gamma^{\prime}
is triangular and
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime})\subset U(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is maximal. Thus, by Lemma
B.2.4
,
\gamma
is triangular, and

\displaystyle\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\codim(\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}_{G^{\prime}})+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})})

(Proposition
2.3.1
(iii))

\displaystyle=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma^{\prime}}})+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})})

(Proposition
3.3.1
)

\displaystyle=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})

\displaystyle\text{(Lemma \ref{lem:inductionsteptypeC})}.

Hence,
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is maximal by a second application of Proposition
3.3.1
.
∎

B.2.1.
Auxilary ideals

We now proceed to proving the maximality of certain auxilary ideals attached to birationally rigid covers. These results are used in the proof of Proposition
10.5.2
.

Suppose
\widetilde{O}
is a birationally rigid cover. Choose a Levi subgroup
L=\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{s})\times G(m)\subset G
and a birationally rigid
L
-orbit

\mathbb{O}_{L}=\{0\}\times...\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{O}_{G(m)}\subset\mathcal{N}_{L}

such that
\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\mathbb{O}_{L}
. For each
k\in\{1,...,s\}
let

\tau_{1}(k)=\frac{1}{2}(\underbrace{0,...,0}_{a_{1}+...+a_{k-1}},\underbrace{1,...,1}_{k},\underbrace{0,...,0}_{a_{k+1}+...+a_{s}+m})\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})

and let

\delta(t):=\frac{t}{2}\tau_{1}(s)+\sum_{k=1}^{s-1}\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}(k)\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}).

Lemma B.2.5
.

For every
t\in(0,1)
,
I_{\delta(t)}(\mathbb{O})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}=G(n-a_{1})
, let

L^{\prime}=\mathrm{GL}(a_{2})\times...\mathrm{GL}(a_{s})\times G(m)\subset G^{\prime},\qquad\mathbb{O}_{L^{\prime}}=\underbrace{\{0\}\times...\{0\}}_{s-1}\times\mathbb{O}_{G(m)},

and let
\mathbb{O}_{G^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G^{\prime}}_{L^{\prime}}\mathbb{O}_{L^{\prime}}
. Note that by Proposition
7.6.7
,
\mathbb{O}_{G^{\prime}}
admits a 2-leafless
G^{\prime}
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}_{G^{\prime}}
. Let
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G^{\prime}})
and
\gamma(t)=\gamma_{\delta(t)}(\mathbb{O}_{G})
. Then

L^{\vee}_{\gamma(t)}=\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times L^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma(t),0}=H_{a_{1}}\times M^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime}},

where
H_{a_{1}}\subset\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})
is the maximal torus. Hence

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma(t)}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma(t)})=\{0\}\times\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma^{\prime}})=\{0\}\times\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}}.

Using Proposition
B.2.1
, we deduce

\displaystyle\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma(t)},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma(t)}})

\displaystyle=\dim(\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})})+\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle=\dim(\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})})+\codim(\mathbb{O}_{G^{\prime}},\mathcal{N}_{G^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N}).

Hence,
I_{\delta(t)}(\mathbb{O})
is maximal by Proposition
3.3.1
.
∎

B.3.
Modifications for type
B

Suppose
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)
. The statements of Lemmas
B.2.2
,
B.2.4
,
B.2.5
, and Proposition
B.2.1
remain true. The proofs of Proposition
B.2.1
and Lemma
B.2.5
hold word for word. We indicate below how the proofs of Lemmas
B.2.2
and
B.2.4
should be modified.

Lemma B.3.1
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a birationally rigid
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.2.8
and
[
McG94
, Thm 5.1]
, it suffices to show

B((g(y))^{t}\cup f_{B}(x))=p.

Proceed exactly as in Lemma
B.2.2
.
∎

Define
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma
as in Section
B.2
.

Lemma B.3.2
.

The
n
-tuple
\gamma
is (
W
-conjugate to) a triangular one, and there is an equality

\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma^{\prime}}})+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{GL(a)}).

Proof.

Repeat the proof of Lemma
B.2.2
, replacing
\mathrm{SO}
factors with
\mathrm{Sp}
factors in
L^{\vee}_{\gamma}
and
L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}
.
∎

B.4.
Modifications for type
D

Suppose
G=\mathrm{SO}(2n)
. The statements of Lemma
B.2.2
, Proposition
B.2.1
, and Lemma
B.2.5
remain true. The proof of Lemma
B.2.5
holds word for word. The proofs of Lemma
B.2.2
and Proposition
B.2.1
must be altered slightly. Lemma
B.2.4
should be replaced by a slightly more elaborate argument. We indicate below how the statements and proofs should be modified.

Lemma B.4.1
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a birationally rigid
G
-equivariant cover. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.2.8
and
[
McG94
, Thm. 5.1]
, it suffices to show

B((g(y)\cup f_{B}(x))^{t})=p.

Proceed exactly as in Lemma
B.2.2
.
∎

Lemma
B.2.4
should be replaced by a pair of lemmas. Choose a Levi subgroup of the form

M=\mathrm{GL}(a)\times G(m)\subset G,\qquad a+m=n,\ m>0.

Suppose
\mu\neq(0)
, and let
\gamma^{\prime}=v(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
be a triangular
m
-tuple. Let

\gamma=(\frac{a-1}{2},\frac{a-3}{2},...,\frac{1-a}{2},\gamma^{\prime}).

Lemma B.4.2
.

The
n
-tuple
\gamma
is (
W
-conjugate to) a triangular one, and there is an equality

\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma^{\prime}}})+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{GL(a)}).

Proof.

Proceed exactly as in the proof of Lemma
B.2.4
.
∎

Next, choose a Levi subgroup of the form

M\simeq\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})\subset G,\qquad a_{1}+...+a_{t}=n,

and let
\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\{0\}
. Note that
\mathbb{O}
corresponds to a very even partition and has a decoration determined by the conjugacy class of
M
.

Lemma B.4.3
.

I_{0}(\mathbb{O})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

Let
\gamma:=\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})
. By Proposition
8.1.1

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})=(\gamma_{0}(\{0\}),...,\gamma_{0}(\{0\}))=(\rho(a_{1}),...,\rho(a_{t})),

or possibly (if all
a_{i}
are even)

\gamma_{0}(\mathbb{O})=(\rho(a_{1}),...,\rho(a_{t}))^{\prime},

where the prime indicates that the last entry is negated. We will show that

\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})=\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}}).

Write
e\subset a
and
o\subset a
for the subpartitions consisting of even and odd parts. Define partitions
\mu=(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{r})
and
\nu=(\nu_{1},...,\nu_{s})
by

\mu_{i}=2(o^{t})_{2i-1},\qquad\nu_{j}=2(e^{t})_{2j},

and put
R=|\mu|-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}
and
S=|\nu|
.

Note that

\gamma=v(\mu,\nu)\text{ or }v(\mu,\nu)^{\prime}

(see Definition
B.2.3
). First suppose
\gamma=v(\mu,\nu)
. Then

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{SO}(2R)\times\mathrm{SO}(2S),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=\mathrm{SO}(\mu_{1})\times\prod_{i=2}^{r}\mathrm{GL}(\mu_{i})\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}\mathrm{GL}(\nu_{j}).

Hence
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathbb{O}^{\vee,o}_{\gamma}\times\mathbb{O}^{\vee,e}_{\gamma}
where
\mathbb{O}^{\vee,o}_{\gamma}
is the nilpotent
\mathrm{SO}(2R)
-orbit corresponding to the partition
(2o^{t})^{t}
and
\mathbb{O}^{\vee,e}_{\gamma}
is the nilpotent
\mathrm{SO}(2S)
-orbit corresponding to the partition
(2e^{t})^{t}
. Note that
(2o^{t})^{t}
is just
o
, but with multiplicities doubled. Similarly for
(2e^{t})^{t}
. Hence, by Proposition
2.6.5
we have

\mathbb{O}^{\vee,o}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{Sat}^{\mathrm{SO}(2R)}_{\prod\mathrm{GL}(o_{i})}\prod\mathbb{O}^{\mathrm{prin}}_{\mathrm{GL}(o_{i})},\qquad\mathbb{O}^{\vee,e}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{Sat}^{\mathrm{SO}(2S)}_{\prod\mathrm{GL}(e_{j})}\prod\mathbb{O}^{\mathrm{prin}}_{\mathrm{GL}(e_{j})}.

Using Proposition
3.2.1
we obtain

\mathbb{O}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\gamma})=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee,o}_{\gamma})\times\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee,e}_{\gamma})=\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{SO}(2R)}_{\prod\mathrm{GL}(o_{i})}\{0\}\times\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{SO}(2S)}_{\prod\mathrm{GL}(e_{j})}\{0\}.

Since induction preserves codimension

\displaystyle\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\codim(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{SO}(2R)}_{\prod\mathrm{GL}(o_{i})}\{0\},\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{SO}(2R)})+\codim(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{SO}(2S)}_{\prod\mathrm{GL}(e_{j})}\{0\},\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{SO}(2S)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\dim(\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{GL}(a_{i})})

\displaystyle=\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N})

as desired.

Next, suppose
\gamma=v^{\prime}(\mu,\nu)
. In this case, we have
o=(0)
and
a=(e)
. Compute

L^{\vee}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{SO}(2n),\qquad L^{\vee}_{\gamma,0}=\prod_{j=1}^{s}\mathrm{GL}(\nu_{j})^{\prime}.

Arguing as above (with
\mu=(0)
) we deduce

\codim(\mathbb{O}_{\gamma},\mathcal{N}_{L_{\gamma}})=\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N}).

∎

Corollary B.4.4
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G
-equivariant cover. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a maximal ideal.

Proof.

Choose a Levi subgroup
M\simeq\mathrm{GL}(a_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{GL}(a_{t})\times G(m)
of
G
and a birationally rigid
M
-equivariant cover

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}=\{0\}\times...\{0\}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G(m)}

such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. If
m=0
, use Lemma
B.4.3
. Otherwise, proceed by induction on
t
. The base case is Lemma
B.4.1
. The induction step is Lemma
B.4.2
.
∎

Appendix C
Singularities of type
m

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a simple exceptional Lie algebra and let
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a rigid nilpotent orbit. Premet shows in
[
Pre13
]
that in almost all cases, there is a unique multiplicity 1 primitive ideal
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
with
V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. In six cases, however, there are several such ideals. The orbits in question are:

(C.0.1)

\widetilde{A}_{1}\subset G_{2},\quad\widetilde{A}_{2}+A_{1}\subset F_{4},\quad(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime}\subset E_{7},\quad A_{3}+A_{1},A_{5}+A_{1},D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{2}\subset E_{8}.

For each of these orbits, Premet constructs two multiplicity 1 primitive ideals with associated variety
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. From Premet’s computations, it is not at all clear which of these ideals is unipotent (in fact, the situation is even murkier—in two cases, Premet does not claim that his list of ideals is exhaustive. In these cases, it is not even clear that
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is among the two ideals he constructs). In this appendix, we will determine the unipotent ideals attached to the six orbits in (
C.0.1
), thus completing the determination of the unipotent spectrum for rigid nilpotent orbits.

There is a geometric feature which these orbits have in common: in each case,
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
contains a (unique) codimension 2 orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
and the transverse slice for
(\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\overline{\mathbb{O}})
is a singularity of type
m
, see the incidence tables in
[
FJLS15
]
. Recall, a type
m
singularity is a a non-normal conical singularity with an
\mathrm{SL}(2)
-action admitting an open orbit isomorphic
to
\mathbb{C}^{2}-\{0\}
. See
[
FJLS15
, Sec 1.8.4]
for details.

Let
R^{\prime}
denote the reductive part of the centralizer
of an element
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
. In the following table, we indicate the orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
and the isomorphism type of
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
. As usual, we use
[
Car93
, Sec 13.1]
for
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
and
[
FJLS15
, Tables]
for
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

\mathfrak{g}

\mathbb{O}

\mathbb{O}^{\prime}

R^{\prime}

G_{2}

\widetilde{A}_{1}

A_{1}

A_{1}

F_{4}

\widetilde{A}_{2}+A_{1}

A_{2}+\widetilde{A}_{1}

A_{1}

E_{7}

(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime}

2A_{2}+A_{1}

2A_{1}

E_{8}

A_{3}+A_{1}

2A_{2}+A_{1}

G_{2}+A_{1}

E_{8}

A_{5}+A_{1}

A_{4}+A_{3}

A_{1}

E_{8}

D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{2}

A_{4}+A_{3}

A_{1}

By inspection, we arrive at the following.

Lemma C.0.1
.

The orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
is rigid and of principle Levi type. The Lie algebra

\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
is semisimple.

Choose an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e^{\prime},h^{\prime},f^{\prime})
and let
R^{\prime}=Z_{G}(e^{\prime},h^{\prime},f^{\prime})
. Let
S^{\prime}
be the Slodowy
slice
e^{\prime}+\mathfrak{g}_{f^{\prime}}
. The intersection
S^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathbb{O}}
is a
singularity of type
m
, by our choice of
(\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
. The following claim was
established in
[
FJLS15
, Sec 1.8.4]
.

Lemma C.0.2
.

The action of
R^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
factors through an epimorphism

R^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{SL}(2)
. The induced action of
\mathrm{SL}(2)
on
S^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
coincides with the usual
\mathrm{SL}(2)
-action on a type
m
singularity.

It follows that
R^{\prime}=R_{1}^{\prime}\times R_{0}^{\prime}
, where
R_{1}^{\prime}\simeq\mathrm{SL}(2)
and
R_{0}^{\prime}

is an algebraic group with semisimple identity component.

C.1.
Algebras
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}

Now let
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
be a multiplicity 1 primitive ideal with
V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Set
\mathcal{A}:=U(\mathfrak{g})/I
. Combining Lemmas
3.5.2
and
3.5.5
, we equip
\mathcal{A}
with a good algebra filtration such that
\gr\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
. Since
\mathbb{O}
is rigid,
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
admits a unique filtered quantization, see Corollary
7.6.1
. Thus,
\gr\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
if and only if
I=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Let
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
denote the
W-algebra for the orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
and let
\bullet_{\dagger}
denote the
corresponding restriction functor, see Section
3.5
. Consider the ideal
I_{\dagger}\subset\mathcal{W}^{\prime}

and the quotient
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}:=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}/I_{\dagger}
.
By (ii) of Proposition
3.5.1
,
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
coincides with the pullback of
\gr\mathcal{A}
to
S^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[S^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{O}]=\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
. There is a Hamiltonian action of
R^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
and on
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
.
Note that the action of
R_{0}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
is trivial. So the quantum comoment map for the
R^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
can be thought of as a homomorphism
U(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
.

The following lemma is the first step towards computing
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Lemma C.1.1
.

The following claims are true:

(1)

The kernel of
U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
has infinitesimal character in
\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
.

(2)

This infinitesimal character is
\pm\frac{1}{2}
(after
\rho
-shift) if and only if

I=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Proof.

The algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
is a Harish-Chandra bimodule for
U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
and its associated variety
is the image of
S^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathbb{O}}
in
\mathfrak{sl}(2)^{*}
. This image is the nilpotent cone. Moreover,
the kernel of
U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
is a completely prime ideal, hence primitive. Note that
\gr\mathcal{A}
embeds into
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
as an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-module with finite dimensional kernel.
This is possible if and only if the infinitesimal character is in
\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
.

If
I=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
, then
\mathcal{A}/I
is the (unique) quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
, and hence
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}

quantizes
\mathbb{C}[S^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{O}]=\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
. It follows that
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
is the Weyl algebra in two variables. The Hamiltonian
\mathrm{SL}(2)
-action on
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
lifts the standard action on

\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
and so is determined uniquely. We get the standard action of
\mathrm{SL}(2)
on the Weyl algebra. The claim about its infinitesimal character is classical (see, e.g., (
4.8.4
) for the case of
n=1
).

Now suppose
I
is not unipotent. This implies that the inclusion
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
is proper. Indeed, assume (for contradiction) that
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
. By (ii) of Proposition
3.5.1
, this implies that the cokernel of
\gr\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
is supported away from
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, i.e. has support of codimension at least
4
. The microlocalization of
U(\mathfrak{g})/I
to the complement of this support is a filtered quantization thereof. Similarly to
[
Los16
, Proposition 3.1]
, the global sections of this microlocalization is a filtered quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
. So
I
is the kernel of the quantum comoment map to a filtered quantization of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}]
. Since
\mathbb{O}
is rigid (and hence birationally rigid) this quantization is unique, see Corollary
7.6.1
. So
I
is the unipotent ideal, a contradiction.

Since
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
is proper, the infinitesimal character is different from
\pm\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

C.2.
Category
\mathsf{O}
for
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}

Our method for computing the infinitesimal characters of unipotent ideals involves some additional machinery from
[
BGK08
]
and
[
Los12a
]
, which we will now briefly review.

Choose a minimal Levi subgroup
\underline{G}\subset G
such that
e^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
(by Lemma
C.0.1
,
e^{\prime}
is then principal in
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
). We can assume without loss that
\underline{G}
is a standard Levi sugroup. Choose a dominant one-parameter subgroup
\nu:\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow R^{\prime}
such that the centralizer in
G
of
\nu(\mathbb{C}^{\times})
coincides with
\underline{G}
. Write
\mathfrak{h}
for the Cartan subalgebra and
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
for the center of
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
. We can regard
\nu
as an element of
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
(by evaluating
d\nu(1)
). Recall, see Section
3.5
, that
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
embeds into
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, so we can also view
\nu
as an element of
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
.

Following
[
BGK08
, Sec 4.4]
,
[
Los12a
]
, we will consider the category
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
consisting
of finitely generated
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
-modules
M
such that

•

\nu\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
acts locally finitely on
M
with finite dimensional generalized
eigenspaces.

•

The eigenvalues are bounded from above, i.e. there are complex
numbers
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
(depending on
M
) such that any eigenvalue
z
of
M

satisfies
z_{i}-z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
for some
i
.

We will now recall the classification of irreducible objects in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
,
see
[
BGK08
, Thm 4.5]
. The one-parameter subgroup
\nu
gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{i}
. We will consider the subspace
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{>0}\subset\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
and the

Cartan subquotient

\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}):=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}/\sum_{i>0}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{-i}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{i}.

For
M\in\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
consider the subspace of singular vectors,

\operatorname{Ann}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{>0}}(M)
. This is a
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
-module.
The assignment
L\mapsto\operatorname{Ann}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{>0}}(L)

defines a bijection between the set of simples in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})

and the set of simple finite dimensional
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
-modules.

A description of
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
was given in
[
BGK08
, Section 4.1]
.
Let
\underline{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
denote the W-algebra for
(\underline{\mathfrak{g}},e^{\prime})
.
Since
e^{\prime}
is principal, the algebra
\underline{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
is isomorphic
to the center of
U(\underline{\mathfrak{g}})
, i.e., to
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{\underline{W}}
,
where
\underline{W}
is the Weyl group of
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
. By

[
BGK08
, Theorem 4.3]
, see also
[
Los12a
, Rmk 5.5]
, there is an isomorphism
\iota:\underline{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
. We note that there are natural
embeddings
\mathfrak{h}^{0}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime},\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
, the latter
induced from the embedding
\mathfrak{h}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}
. The next lemma describes the restriction of
\iota
to the subspace
\mathfrak{h}^{0}\subset\underline{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, define
\Delta^{+}(n):=\{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}\mid\alpha(h^{\prime})=n\}
and consider the element

(C.2.1)

\rho_{e^{\prime}}:=\frac{1}{2}(\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}(0)}\alpha+\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}(1)}\alpha)\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}

The next lemma follows from
[
BGK08
, Theorem 4.3]
, see also
[
Pre13
, Sec 2.6]
.

Lemma C.2.1
.

\iota(x)=x+\langle\rho-\rho_{e},x\rangle
for every
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{0}
.

The isomorphism
\iota:\underline{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
shows
that the space of singular vectors in any simple module
L
is one-dimensional
and the action is given by a character of
\underline{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}
, i.e. an element in

\mathfrak{h}^{*}/\underline{W}
. We will represent this element by an

integrally anti-dominant
(for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
) weight
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. This means

\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\notin\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\qquad\forall\alpha\in\Delta^{+}(\underline{\mathfrak{g}},\mathfrak{h}).

Write
L_{\lambda}
for the irreducible module in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
corresponding
to
\lambda
. For what follows we will need a formula for the action of
\mathfrak{h}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})

on
L_{\lambda}
. The next Corollary is a direct consequence of Lemma
C.2.1
.

Corollary C.2.2
.

The subspace
\mathfrak{h}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
acts on
L_{\lambda}
by the character
(\lambda-\rho_{e})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}
. This character will be called the
highest weight
of
L_{\lambda}
.

Next, consider the category
\mathsf{Wh}
of
generalized Whittaker modules
for
\mathfrak{g}
, see
[
Los12a
, Sec 4]
. These are
U(\mathfrak{g})
-modules
M
such that

•

\nu
acts locally finitely on
M
.

•

Each generalized eigenspace for the
\nu
-action is a Whittaker module for

\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
in the sense of Kostant,
[
Kos79
]
.

We note that the irreducible objects in
\mathsf{Wh}
are parameterized by their
highest weight spaces for
\nu
. Each highest weight space is an irreducible
Whittaker module for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
, and they are in bijection with
\mathfrak{h}^{*}/\underline{W}

(by taking infinitesimal character). If
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is integrally anti-dominant, we write

\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}
for the irreducible object in
\mathsf{Wh}
corresponding
to
\lambda
. The following is a direct corollary of
[
Los12a
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Proposition C.2.3
.

There is a category equivalence
\Psi:\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Wh}
such that
\Psi(L_{\lambda})\simeq\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}

for all
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}/\underline{W}
.

We will need a result concerning the behavior of annihilator of
L_{\lambda}

in a special case. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
we write
I(\lambda)
for the annihilator of the irreducible

U(\mathfrak{g})
-module with highest weight
\lambda-\rho
.

There is a map
\bullet^{{\ddagger}}
from the set of two-sided ideals in
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
to the set of
two-sided ideals in
U(\mathfrak{g})
, see
[
Los10a
, Theorem 1.2.2]
, extending the map constructed
in Section 3.5.

Proposition C.2.4
.

Let
\lambda
be
integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then
\operatorname{Ann}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}(L_{\lambda})^{{\ddagger}}=I(\lambda)
.

Proof.

By
[
Los12a
, Thm 4.1]
,
\operatorname{Ann}_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\Psi(L_{\lambda}))=\operatorname{Ann}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}(L_{\lambda})^{\ddagger}
. By Proposition
C.2.3
,

\Psi(L_{\lambda})=\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}
. By
[
Los11
, Thm 5.1.1]

(and its proof),
\operatorname{Ann}_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\widetilde{L}_{\lambda})=I(\lambda)
.
∎

C.3.
Category
\mathsf{O}
for
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}

Consider the full subcategory
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
of
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
consisting
of all modules annihilated by
I_{\dagger}
. We can form the Cartan subquotient
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}

similarly to
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
in the previous section. Taking the subspace of singular vectors
defines a bijection between the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible modules in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
and the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
-modules.

The following is our main result on the structure of irreducible modules in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
.
Note that the choice of
\nu
gives rise to a system of simple roots in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
.

Proposition C.3.1
.

The following are true:

(1)

There is a single irreducible module in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
.

(2)

Its highest weight, see Corollary
C.2.2
, is of the form

z\omega_{1}
, where
z\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
and
\omega_{1}
is the fundamental weight of the factor

\mathfrak{r}_{1}^{\prime}\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(2)
in
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
(see the discussion after Lemma
C.0.2
).

(3)

We have
z=-\frac{1}{2}
if
I
is a unipotent ideal and
z\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}

otherwise.

Proof.

Recall that there is the quantum comoment map
U(\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
.
Its restriction to the semisimple factor
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}_{0}
is zero, while the kernel of

U(\mathfrak{r}_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
has infinitesimal character in
\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}

by Lemma
C.1.1
. It follows that a central reduction
of
U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
has a filtered embedding into
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
. The image has
a complimentary nonzero Harish-Chandra bimodule summand. Let
h
denote the image of
the standard basis element of
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
in
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
. It defines a grading such that
the sum of even graded components is the central reduction of
U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
, while there
are also odd components. This grading is proportional to the grading defined by
\nu
.

We claim that
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
is generated by the image of
h
. Indeed, the degree
0
component of
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
surjects onto
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
, so it is enough to show that it coincides with
\mathbb{C}[h]
. The proof of that reduces to the analogous claim for
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
, which in turn, reduces to
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
, thanks to the embedding
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
.
The claim that the degree
0
part of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
is generated by
h
is clear.

It follows that
an irreducible module in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
is determined by its highest weight up to an isomorphism. The highest weight
must have the form
z\omega_{1}
because
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}_{0}
acts by
0
. By Lemma
C.1.1
,
z\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
. This proves (2).

Now we prove (1). Take an irreducible object
L\in\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})
and restrict
it to
U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
. This restriction lies in the category
\mathsf{O}
for
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
. Its
infinitesimal character is half-integral, so it is semisimple. We claim that both irreducible
