objects in the block occur in the restriction. Indeed, if only one simple occurs,
then the elements of odd degree in
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
act by
0
on
L
. It follows that

L
is annihilated by a proper two-sided ideal in
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
. Since
\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]
with finite dimensional cokernel, we see that
\operatorname{Spec}(\gr\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})

has two symplectic leaves,
\{0\}
and the open leaf. It follows that any proper two-sided
ideal in
\mathcal{A}_{\dagger}
must have finite codimension. This is impossible because its central
character for
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
is in
\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
. It follows that the highest weight
of
L
is the larger of the highest weights of the two irreducible
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-modules.
This determines the highest weight of
L
uniquely. Since the highest weight of
L

determines it uniquely, (1) is proved.

Now we prove (3). By Lemma
C.1.1
,
I
is unipotent if and only if
the infinitesimal character of the
U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
-module
L
is
\pm\frac{1}{2}
. The description of
the highest weight of
L
in the previous paragraph implies that it is
-\frac{1}{2}
.
The case when
I
is not unipotent is handled similarly.
∎

The following technical corollary is our main computational tool for determining
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
for
\mathbb{O}
in (
C.0.1
). A weight
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is
integrally dominant
(for
\mathfrak{g}
) if

\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0},\qquad\forall\alpha\in\Delta^{+}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}).

It is a classical fact that
\lambda
is integrally dominant if and only if
I(\lambda)
is maximal, see
[
Dix74
, Thm 7.6.24]
. Recall the weight
\rho_{e}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
defined in (
C.2.1
).

Corollary C.3.2
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be integrally dominant for
\mathfrak{g}
and integrally
anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
. Suppose that
I=I(\lambda)
is a multiplicity 1 primitive ideal such that
V(I)=\overline{\mathbb{O}}
.
Then the following are true:

(1)

The kernel of the operator
(\lambda-\rho_{e^{\prime}})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}
coincides with the Cartan subalgebra
of
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}_{0}
.

(2)

Choose
h_{1}\in\mathfrak{h}^{0}
in the nonnegative span of the positive coroots and orthogonal to the subspace
\ker{(\lambda-\rho_{e^{\prime}})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}}
. If

\langle\lambda-\rho_{e^{\prime}},h_{1}\rangle<0,

then
I(\lambda)=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Proof.

We claim that the unique irreducible in
\mathsf{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\dagger})

is
L_{\lambda}
. Indeed,
\operatorname{Ann}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}(L_{\lambda})^{\ddagger}=I

by Proposition
C.2.4
. By
[
Los10a
, Theorem 1.2.2]
, for any ideal

J\subset\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, we have
(J^{\ddagger})_{\dagger}\subset J
. Applying this to
J:=\operatorname{Ann}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}(L_{\lambda})
we see that
L_{\lambda}
is annihilated by

I_{\dagger}
, which implies our claim.

By Corollary
C.2.2
, the highest weight of
L_{\lambda}
is

(\lambda-\rho_{e})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}
. By (2) of Proposition
C.3.1
,
this highest weight vanishes on
\mathfrak{h}^{0}\cap\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}_{0}
, which is a codimension
1

subspace in
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
, and is nonzero. Hence
\mathfrak{h}^{0}\cap\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}_{0}
is the kernel. This proves (1).

To prove (2) we observe that
h_{1}
must be a positive multiple of the standard basis element

h\in\mathfrak{r}_{1}^{\prime}\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(2)
. By Proposition
C.3.1
, the unipotent
ideal is uniquely characterized by the property that the corresponding highest
weight
(\lambda-\rho_{e^{\prime}})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}
is negative on
h
. (2) follows.
∎

C.4.
Computations

Let
\mathbb{O}
be one the six orbits in (
C.0.1
), and let
I(\lambda)
be one of the (two) multiplicity 1 primitive ideals constructed by Premet (note that Premet’s highest weights do not include the
\rho
-shift, unlike ours). Assume that
\lambda
is integrally dominant for
\mathfrak{g}
. We will use the following algorithm to decide whether
I(\lambda)=I(\mathbb{O})
.

(1)

Fix a standard Levi subgroup
\underline{G}\subset G
in which
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
is principal. Then
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is spanned by the fundamental coweights corresponding to the simple roots which are not contained in
\underline{G}
.

(2)

Replace
\lambda
with a
W
-conjugate which is both integrally dominant for
\mathfrak{g}
and integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(3)

Copy the character
\rho_{e}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
from Premet, and compute the difference
\lambda-\rho_{e}
.

(4)

If
\dim(\mathfrak{h}^{0})=1
, then there is a unique simple root
\alpha_{i}
not contained in
\underline{G}
. If

\langle\lambda-\rho_{e},\varpi_{i}^{\vee}\rangle<0,

then
I(\lambda)=I(\mathbb{O})
.

(5)

If
\dim(\mathfrak{h}^{0})>1
(this happens in exacly two cases), compute the kernel of the operator
(\lambda-\rho_{e})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}
. In both cases, this kernel is the span of a collection of fundamental coweights
\varpi_{j}^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{h}^{0}
.

(6)

In the setting of (5), let
\varpi_{i}^{\vee}
denote the unique fundamental coweight in
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
not contained in
\ker{(\lambda-\rho_{e^{\prime}})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}}
. Note that
\varpi_{i}^{\vee}
differs from
h_{1}
in Corollary
C.3.2
by an element of
\ker{(\lambda-\rho_{e^{\prime}})|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}}
. If

\langle\lambda-\rho_{e},\varpi_{i}^{\vee}\rangle<0,

then
I(\lambda)=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

For the computations below, we will use the standard (Bourbaki) numbering of simple roots in
\Delta^{+}
, see the Dynkin diagrams in Section
9.1
. This is also numbering used by Premet in
[
Pre13
]
. As usual, we will write elements of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
(resp.
\mathfrak{h}
) in the basis of fundamental weights (resp. simple coroots). One advantage of this convention is that the pairing
\langle\lambda,x\rangle
is the dot product. Very often, we will need to convert from the bases of simple roots (resp. fundamental co-weights). For this, we use
[
Bou02
, Planches I-IX]
as a reference (usually without comment).

C.4.1.
\widetilde{A}_{1}\subset G_{2}

Premet’s ideals correspond to the weights

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(1,1),\qquad\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(5,-1).

We claim that
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Note that
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=A_{1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=A_{1}
.
\underline{G}
is the standard Levi subgroup corresponding to the short simple root
\alpha_{2}
, and therefore
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is spanned by the fundamental co-weight
\varpi_{1}^{\vee}
. Note that
\lambda_{1}
is (integrally) dominant for
G
and integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
, so Corollary
C.3.2
is applicable. In our (fundamental weight) coordinates,
\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(3,0)
, see
[
Pre13
, Sec 5.6]
. Thus
\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(-2,1)
. Note that

\langle\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{1}^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\frac{1}{2}(-2,1),(2,3)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}

Hence,
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
by Corollary
C.3.2
. Since
\lambda_{1}
is integrally dominant,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is thus the maximal ideal of infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{2}(1,1)
.

C.4.2.
\widetilde{A}_{2}+A_{1}\subset F_{4}

Premet’s ideals correspond to the weights

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{3}(1,1,1,1),\qquad\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{3}(-1,-1,5,2).

We claim that
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Note that
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=A_{2}+\widetilde{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=A_{1}
.
\underline{G}
is the standard Levi subgroup corresponding to the simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}\}
, and therefore
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is spanned by the fundamental co-weight
\varpi_{3}^{\vee}
. Note that
\lambda_{1}
is (integrally) dominant for
G
and integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
, so Corollary
C.3.2
is applicable. In our (fundamental weight) coordinates,
\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(-1,2,0,1)
, see
[
Pre13
, Sec 5.4]
. Thus
\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{6}(5,-4,2,-1)
. Note that

\langle\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{3}^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\frac{1}{6}(5,-4,2,-1),(4,8,6,3)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}

Hence,
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
by Corollary
C.3.2
. Since
\lambda_{1}
is integrally dominant,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is thus the maximal ideal of infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{3}(1,1,1,1)
.

C.4.3.
(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime}\subset E_{7}

Premet’s ideals correspond to the weights

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(1,1,0,1,0,1,1),\qquad\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(3,4,-4,-1,3,1,1).

We claim that
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Note that
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=2A_{2}+A_{1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=2A_{1}
.
\underline{G}
is the standard Levi subgroup corresponding to the simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\}
, and therefore
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is spanned by the fundamental co-weights
\varpi_{4}^{\vee},\varpi_{7}^{\vee}
. Note that
\lambda_{1}
is (integrally) dominant for
G
and integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
, so Corollary
C.3.2
is applicable. In our (fundamental weight) coordinates,
\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(-1,2,3,-1,0,2,0)
, see
[
Pre13
, Sec 4.10]
. Thus
\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(3,-3,-6,3,0,-3,1)
. Note that

\displaystyle\langle\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{4}^{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{1}{2}(3,-3,-6,3,0,-3,1),(4,6,8,12,9,6,3)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\langle\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{7}^{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{1}{2}(3,-3,-6,3,0,-3,1),\frac{1}{2}(2,3,4,6,5,4,3)\rangle=0

Hence,
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
by Corollary
C.3.2
. Since
\lambda_{1}
is integrally dominant,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is thus the maximal ideal of infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{2}(1,1,0,1,0,1,1)
.

C.4.4.
A_{3}+A_{1}\subset E_{8}

Premet’s ideals correspond to the weights

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(1,1,0,1,0,1,1,2),\qquad\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(-3,3,-2,1,2,1,1,2).

We claim that
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Note that
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=2A_{2}+A_{1}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=A_{1}+G_{2}
. So
\underline{G}
is the standard Levi subgroup corresponding to the simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\}
, and therefore
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is spanned by the fundamental co-weights
\varpi_{4}^{\vee},\varpi_{7}^{\vee},\varpi_{8}^{\vee}
. Note that
\lambda_{1}
is (integrally) dominant for
G
and integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
, so Corollary
C.3.2
is applicable. In our (fundamental weight) coordinates,
\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(0,2,4,-2,-1,4,-1,2)
, see
[
Pre13
, Sec 3.8]
. Thus
\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(1,-1,-4,3,1,-3,2,0)
. Note that

\displaystyle\langle\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{4}^{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{1}{2}(1,-1,-4,3,1,-3,2,0),(10,15,20,30,24,18,12,6)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\langle\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{7}^{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{1}{2}(1,-1,-4,3,1,-3,2,0),(4,6,8,12,10,8,6,3)\rangle=0

\displaystyle\langle\lambda_{1}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{8}^{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{1}{2}(1,-1,-4,3,1,-3,2,0),(2,3,4,6,5,4,3,2)\rangle=0

Hence,
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
by Corollary
C.3.2
. Since
\lambda_{1}
is integrally dominant,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is thus the maximal ideal of infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{2}(1,1,0,1,0,1,1,2)
.

C.4.5.
A_{5}+A_{1}\subset E_{8}

Premet’s ideals correspond to the weights

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{6}(2,2,1,1,1,1,1,1),\qquad\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{6}(2,2,1,7,-11,7,1,1).

We claim that
I(\lambda_{1}-\rho)=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Note that
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=A_{4}+A_{3}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=A_{1}
. So
\underline{G}
is the standard Levi subgroup corresponding to the simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8}\}
, and therefore
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is spanned by the fundamental co-weight
\varpi_{5}^{\vee}
. Although
\lambda_{1}
is clearly (integrally) dominant for
G
, it is not integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
. A bit of tinkering shows that
\lambda_{1}
is
W
-conjugate to the weight
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{6}(2,2,1,2,-1,2,1,1)
(the simple reflection
s_{\alpha_{5}}
takes
\lambda_{1}
to
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
). It is straightforward to check that
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
is integrally dominant for
\mathfrak{g}
and integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
.

In our (fundamental weight) coordinates,
\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(0,2,0,0,0,1,1,-1)
, see
[
Pre13
, Sec 3.15]
. Thus
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{6}(2,-4,1,2,-1,-1,-2,4)
. Note that

\langle\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{5}^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\frac{1}{6}(2,-4,1,2,-1,-1,-2,4),(8,12,16,24,20,15,10,5)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}.

Hence
I(\lambda_{1}-\rho)=I(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-\rho)=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
by Corollary
C.3.2
. Since
\lambda_{1}
is integrally dominant,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is thus the maximal ideal of infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{6}(2,2,1,1,1,1,1,1)
.

C.4.6.
D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{2}\subset E_{8}

Premet’s ideals correspond to the weights

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{4}(-1,-1,-1,4,-1,4,-1,-1),\qquad\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(-1,-1,-1,8,-9,8,-1,-1).

We claim that
I(\lambda_{1})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

Note that
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=A_{4}+A_{3}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=A_{1}
. So
\underline{G}
is the standard Levi subgroup corresponding to the simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8}\}
, and therefore
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is spanned by the fundamental co-weight
\varpi_{5}^{\vee}
. Although
\lambda_{1}
is integrally dominant for
G
, it is not integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
. Using
atlas
, we see that
\lambda_{1}
is
W
-conjugate to the dominant weight
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{4}(1,1,1,0,1,1,1,1)
(the Weyl group element
s_{\alpha_{1}}s_{\alpha_{2}}s_{\alpha_{3}}s_{\alpha_{1}}s_{\alpha_{5}}s_{\alpha_{7}}s_{\alpha_{8}}s_{\alpha_{7}}
takes
\lambda_{1}
to
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
). It is straightforward to check that
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
is integrally anti-dominant for
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Again,
\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{2}(0,2,0,0,0,1,1,-1)
. Thus
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-\rho_{e}=\frac{1}{4}(1,-3,1,0,1,-1,-1,3)
. Note that

\langle\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-\rho_{e},\varpi_{5}^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\frac{1}{4}(1,-3,1,0,1,-1,-1,3),(8,12,16,24,20,15,10,5)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}

Hence
I(\lambda_{1})=I(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-\rho)=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
by Corollary
C.3.2
. Thus,
I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
is the maximal ideal of infinitesimal character
\frac{1}{4}(1,1,1,0,1,1,1,1)
.
